#3184. Your headphones have hidden LE Audio features you’ll want to know about

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Your JBL, Sony, or Bose Bluetooth headphones got a top-to-bottom wireless makeover -- not just Auracast.

The shift from Classic Bluetooth to LE Audio is in full swing, as JBL and Sony release flagship headphones and earbuds with built-in LE Audio. Bose is the latest to upgrade its flagship headphones via an OTA firmware update.

Also: Apple has a Bluetooth problem, and Android is taking advantage

Auracast, the Bluetooth protocol that enables one-to-many audio streams and is the most popular LE Audio feature, but several other LE Audio upgrades don’t get as much attention. These features improve wireless voice call quality, reduce battery drain in true wireless earbuds, and remove ecosystem barriers to low-latency mobile gaming. Here’s why they matter just as much as Auracast.

LE Audio includes the Low Complexity Communication Codec (LC3), which includes the unified Telephony and Media Audio Profile (TMAP) to handle audio streams and voice calls simultaneously. Bluetooth Classic relies on the Advanced Audio Distribution Profile (A2DP) and Hands-Free Profile (HFP), which handle the same functions but require both profiles.

Also: Bluetooth’s latest could solve a common connectivity problem

The profiles in Bluetooth Classic, given that they must alternate, result in extremely poor voice quality during hands-free calls over cellular or video calls on Zoom or Teams. These profiles also account for degraded voice quality when chatting while playing a mobile game.

LE Audio consolidates these two profiles, creating higher-quality, bidirectional audio and preserving voice call quality when using wireless headphones.

Earlier this year, JBL gave its flagship Tour Pro 3 earbuds an OTA firmware update to support LE Audio, and Sony’s WF-1000XM6 earbuds, released in February, have LE Audio support out of the box. There’s one major true wireless earbud improvement wrapped in LE Audio: native multi-stream audio.

Also: Frustrated with multipoint? How it works – and why it sometimes won’t

In Bluetooth Classic, your source device, such as your phone, sends audio to one earbud, which then retransmits it to the other earbud. This complex audio transmission method is why one of your earbuds always has a lower battery than the other. Though premium earbuds usually have an independent stream workaround, many midrange and budget earbuds don’t, and that’s where LE Audio comes in handy.

LE Audio enables independent audio streams built into the Bluetooth stack, so manufacturers don’t need to implement their own methods to avoid asymmetrical battery drain.

Software ecosystems, like Apple’s iOS and Samsung’s Galaxy, dictate which audio features are reserved for devices inside of them and withhold features from devices outside of them. Hardware ecosystems are equally responsible for the fragmentation of audio features across manufacturers, typically through codecs.

Sony’s LDAC codec for high-quality audio is only available on Sony headphones, earbuds, and smartphones with the proper hardware, which is limited in the US market. Snapdragon’s AptX codecs aren’t as widely available on phones either, leaving most people to rely on Bluetooth Classic SBC and AAC codes, even though their headphones support more advanced ones.

Also: Your iPhone may support Bluetooth Channel Sounding – and it’s a big deal

LE Audio eliminates the tedious codec issue, especially in wireless gaming contexts, delivering lower latency, improved voice audio streams, and eliminating asymmetrical audio transmission.

For now, as long as you have a compatible Android phone, you have access to several models of headphones, earbuds, and Bluetooth speakers that support LE Audio. As more manufacturers update their devices to support the Bluetooth audio streaming protocol, this suggests the technology is becoming more reliable.

So, what about your AirPods? I wouldn’t bet on it anytime soon.

#3183. Google’s latest Home updates just fixed my biggest annoyances with its speakers and cameras

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Google details 42 fixes to its Home app and the devices compatible with it.

Google’s latest release notes detail early access updates to Gemini for Home and software updates to the Google Home app, focused on bug fixes and general improvements to cameras, doorbells, Nest Wi-Fi, Google TV Streamers, and in-app controls and interfaces.

Also: Google Home Speaker vs. Amazon Echo Dot Max

There are 42 fixes Google addresses in its release notes, ranging from stability improvements to more pressing issues such as Gemini misunderstanding commands and devices connecting more quickly after a network timeout.

Google fixed a few issues that, if they had continued, would have made me consider using another manufacturer for my home’s cameras and doorbell.

Google’s release notes promise improvements in Gemini’s ability to transfer music between rooms. If you have several Google Home speakers around your house, you might want to use your voice to command one to transfer audio.

According to Google, Gemini’s understanding of these commands is now deeper and more aware of the rooms in your home. (It’s something that Sonos does really well.) Now, you can tell Gemini to “move the music here,” and it should work.

Also: I changed these Android Auto settings to limit what Gemini learns about me

In the same vein, Google says it fixed a volume issue where users would command Gemini to adjust the volume on a speaker in another room, but the speaker in closer proximity would respond instead.

Google’s release notes also cite fixes to timer and alarm volume issues, unprompted responses from speakers and displays, and more reliable YouTube TV commands on smart displays.

Google’s release addresses major bugs in the Google Home app when users are trying to access a camera’s live view. For iOS users, the issue with scrolling to the end of an event and receiving a “Video Not Available” error should be fixed. On foldable Androids, watching a live event while folding or unfolding the phone would prompt an error. Google says this issue is also fixed.

Additionally, software updates for Nest Cam (1st gen), Nest Cam Outdoor (1st gen), Nest Doorbell (wired, 1st gen / Nest Hello), Nest Cam IQ Indoor, and Nest Cam IQ Outdoor have all migrated to the Google Home app as Google continues to phase out the older Nest app.

I enjoy my home’s outdoor Nest cameras, but their connectivity issues are often frustrating, especially during inclement weather. The camera above my garage would often disconnect from Wi-Fi, so I purchased a cheap extender to help it maintain a connection.

If my power went out, even for a brief moment, the garage camera wouldn’t come back online for hours, an issue my backyard camera didn’t experience. According to Google, a software update has addressed the issue that caused cameras to stall or crash when reconnecting after brief interruptions.

Also: Amazon’s Echo Show 16 makes Apple’s 7-inch HomeView look puny

During the same brief power and Wi-Fi outage at my home, my Nest Thermostats also appeared as Offline, even though my furnace and air conditioner were working fine. My power momentarily disconnects often during hurricane season, so these issues were very frustrating. According to Google, devices that were inaccurately displaying as Offline were affected by an iOS-specific bug that has now been fixed.

If you have a wired 1st-gen Nest Doorbell, the software update also includes improvements to Familiar Face detection and audio/video synchronization.

#3182. Your Netgear router may be vulnerable to a security exploit – how to check and patch it

A vulnerability in some Netgear routers could allow an attacker with access to your network to run malicious commands on the device.

Do you own a Netgear router? The bad news is that the device could be susceptible to a security flaw that would leave it vulnerable to an attacker. The good news: This exploit affects only a small number of Netgear routers, and the company has already issued patches to squash the bugs.

Also: Run this 10-step security check on your phone once a year

The vulnerabilities differ slightly among the various affected models, as described in Netgear’s security advisory. In general, these flaws could be exploited by an attacker who gains access to your network, allowing them to run unauthorized and potentially malicious commands on the router.

All the flaws are ranked as medium severity but clearly still pose a threat, prompting Netgear to roll out new firmware for each impacted model. You should be able to update your router to the latest firmware directly through its settings, but you can also grab each update from Netgear’s website.

Here are descriptions of the vulnerabilities, the names and models of the affected routers, and links to download the patches.

Described in CVE-2026-11732, the first vulnerability could allow an attacker who already has access to your network, most likely through your Wi-Fi credentials, to run unauthorized commands on the device. The flaw is exploitable only if you’ve enabled the Block Sites feature, which blocks access to specific web sites based on domain name or keywords.

Also: Firefox and Chrome patch 200+ security flaws combined

Affected products and the firmware download pages to patch the bug:

Among these, the RAX5 4-Stream AX1600 WiFi 6 Router has reached end of service (EoS), meaning no further updates will be available after this latest one. In this case, Netgear advises owners to upgrade to a newer router.

Defined in CVE-2026-11731, the second vulnerability could allow an unauthenticated attacker with access to your local network and your router’s management service view and modify certain device details and administrative settings.

Also: Chrome warns you about suspicious websites now

Affected products and the download pages to patch the bug:

Documented in CVE-2026-11730, the third vulnerability impacts two Netgear range extenders. Here, an attacker who already has local network access could hijack an authenticated administrator session on the device’s web management portal.

Affected products and their firmware download pages:

Among these two, the EX3700 AC750 WiFi Range Extender Essentials Edition has reached end of service, which means it’s time to move to a newer device.

The remaining routers impacted by security flaws have all reached end of service. Again, that means no further updates will be available, and Netgear advises owners to switch to a newer device.

Described in CVE-2026-9218, an attacker who’s connected to the router’s local network and has the administrator username and password could run unauthorized commands on the device.

Also: The best Wi-Fi routers: Expert tested and reviewed

Affected products and their firmware download pages:

With the CBR40 Orbi AC2200 Tri-band WiFi Cable Modem Router, the latest firmware will not fix the current security bug, so owners should move to a newer device to stay secure.

The remaining Netgear router impacted by security flaws is also at end-of-service, and the latest firmware won’t fix its security flaws. Again, Netgear advises owners to switch to a newer and more secure device.

#3181. Is Your Echo Show Display Filling With AI Slop? I Found How to Turn It Off

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

My Amazon smart display has been filling with AI garbage. Here's how to clear it out if you're struggling.

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

Senior Editor / Home Security and Smart Home

With Amazon announcing a new Echo Show 16 to square off with Apple’s upcoming HomeView, the smart displays are having a moment, but I’ve noticed something seems off. In the past week or two, my Echo Show has started showing me a lot of lazy, annoying AI-generated ads under a playback feature called Created For You. So I figured out how to improve my experience with a few settings changes.

Not only is this AI slop vague and accompanied by poorly generated images, but it also doesn’t seem to have anything to do with my recorded interests or personalization, either. The slides range from questions about squirrels finding nuts and how to make cornfield mazes to facts about daylight saving time. Basically, it’s very general content I don’t really care about and don’t want showing on a central display at home.

I suspect Amazon is pushing its AI podcasts and similar chatty content with this latest round of generated ads, but if you’re not interested in viewing any of these canned clips, they can come across as irksome. So I reviewed my settings options and found out how to push my Show display away from these creations and toward more interesting content.

The good news is you can control what your Echo Show, well, shows you — but you have to know what settings to change. This isn’t very easy through the Alexa app, but I found a better way using the Show’s own touchscreen controls. Here’s what to do when you have a minute.

First, when an AI slide like this appears, tap the three-dot menu button and tap the thumbs-down option to indicate your dislike. It’s not a perfect solution, but it should help start weeding out these types of ads.

Next, swipe down on your Echo Show device to access the main menu, and choose Settings. Then choose Display & Appearance, and head to Content Categories.

Here you have two options. You can manually turn off all the categories you don’t want to see, especially those connected to the AI slop that you’ve been seeing. Keep focus on topics that you’re interested in if you still want to see other related content. You can also toggle the Rotate Once setting on, which will show you Amazon’s slides only once, my preferred option for handling this kind of unwanted content.

As an alternative, you can switch your Echo Show to show only a photo album of your choosing. I like getting some Alexa recommendations like recipes and updates, so this option isn’t for me, but if you have a good photo album to share with Alexa, you can limit your slides to only your own content.

With these settings adjusted, you should see far fewer AI-generated images and more slides tailored to your own interests. I’ll keep an eye on my Echo Shows and let you know if anything changes, but this has worked well for me so far.

#3180. Amazon vs. Target: One Is Riding the AI Boom, While the Other Faces a Consumer Spending Squeeze

Amazon and Target are two familiar retail names, but their contrasting revenue trends reveal which company may be better positioned.

Amazon.com (AMZN +2.06%) and Target (TGT -2.51%) are two of America's largest retailers, but their revenue trends reveal a widening gap in growth. While Amazon continues to expand through e-commerce, cloud computing, and digital services, Target is working to regain momentum in a challenging retail environment.

A closer look at their quarterly revenue highlights how differently these companies are navigating changing consumer spending habits and broader economic conditions.

Amazon.com vs. Target Revenue over the last 8 quarters.

Amazon generates revenue through its global e-commerce operations, Amazon Web Services (AWS) cloud computing platform, advertising, and subscription services. Its diversified business model provides exposure to both consumer spending and enterprise technology investment, helping sustain revenue growth across different economic environments.

Amazon's latest earnings highlight the strength of these revenue streams. In the third quarter of 2026, revenue increased 12% year over year to $201.9 billion, supported by growth across its retail and cloud businesses. AWS was particularly impressive, with revenue jumping 39% to $46 billion as demand for cloud computing and artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure accelerated. Meanwhile, North American retail sales increased 6%, demonstrating continued expansion despite a more challenging consumer environment. Together, these businesses have helped Amazon maintain double-digit revenue growth, with its rapidly expanding cloud division becoming an increasingly important contributor to overall sales.

Target generates revenue via its nationwide network of retail stores and e-commerce operations, selling everything from groceries and household essentials to apparel, electronics, and home furnishings. Its business relies heavily on consumer spending, particularly discretionary purchases, making revenue sensitive to inflation, household budgets, and broader economic conditions.

Target's latest earnings suggest its sales recovery is gaining momentum after a period of declining revenue. In the second quarter of fiscal 2026, net sales increased 5.3% year over year to $26.5 billion, while comparable sales rose 3.8%. Importantly, customer traffic increased by 3.6%, suggesting that more shoppers are returning rather than sales growth being driven primarily by higher prices. Digital comparable sales also climbed 8.7%, supported by growing demand for same-day delivery.

Revenue here refers to the data provider's standardized income-statement revenue line item, which helps investors measure total sales volume and overall demand before accounting for business expenses.

Target's recent sales stabilization is encouraging, but the broader trend remains relatively flat. Consumers continue to prioritize essential purchases over discretionary spending, creating challenges for retailers dependent on apparel, home furnishings, and other nonessential goods. Target's efforts to improve pricing, attract shoppers, and strengthen its digital business could eventually support a turnaround, but consistent revenue growth has yet to materialize.Amazon, meanwhile, has several advantages extending beyond traditional retail. Its e-commerce operations continue to grow, while AWS, advertising, and subscription services provide additional revenue streams. Strong demand for cloud computing and AI infrastructure offers opportunities that Target simply doesn't have.

All things considered, Amazon offers a more attractive risk-reward opportunity than Target. Its consistent revenue growth, diversified operations, and expanding cloud business provide a stronger foundation for future returns. The key risk to monitor is Amazon's substantial AI infrastructure spending, which could pressure cash flow if returns fall short of expectations. Nevertheless, Amazon's established growth engines make it a more compelling investment than Target, whose turnaround remains uncertain.

RJ Fulton has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon and Target. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

I'm finally ready to upgrade to a smart calendar - and Amazon's newest Echo device looks like an organizational solution for my family and I.

I’ve never claimed to be the most organized person, and I’m fully aware I need to take steps to be more on top of appointments, schedules, to-do lists, and everything that comes with family life. I’ve been tempted by smart display calendars like the Skylight, but they seemed a little gimmicky and expensive for what’s essentially a large tablet. (ZDNET’s Maria Diaz argues against that gimmick notion, by the way.)

The Echo Show 16, though, might just be the device that gets me to take the leap.

Also: Echo Show 16 makes Apple’s 7-inch HomeView look puny

When I first saw this device, I admit, it wasn’t the first thing to catch my eye. But then I started learning more about what it can do — and now I’m ready to boot my Echo Show 5 for an upgrade. It won’t be a cheap upgrade (I’ll address that later), but if it does what Amazon is promising, it’ll be more than worth it to me.

The first thing I noticed was how sleek the device looks. Amazon says it has 10% more viewing area than the Echo Show 15, but a frame that’s almost 40% thinner. Since I’ll be opting for a wall mount, I wanted something that wouldn’t be too obtrusive. Since my current device is an Echo Show 5 — the small counter-top version — it’s not exactly comparable. Still, it’ll be a huge upgrade to go from an Alexa device that shows pictures to something much more capable.

The biggest appeal for me is the organization. Amazon says the new Show offers a family hub with color-coded schedules, to-do lists, meal planning, and more. My Google Calendar holds all of that now, but it’s easy to miss a notification. And if I don’t make it a priority to check my app every day (something I need to get better about), it’s easy to miss something.

Also: The 4 biggest Amazon smart home announcements from this week

My current Show can display lists and events, but it doesn’t have a permanently present display.

Another significant upgrade is the camera. My current Show has a camera, but it’s not the greatest, especially at night or in low-light conditions, since it’s 2 MP.  The Echo Show 16 features a wide-angle 13-megapixel lens, the widest field of view on any Echo Show device to date. According to Amazon, this doubles the range of Visual ID from 8 feet to 17 feet (from the previous generation). The Show 16 also adds night vision, making it much easier to keep an eye on your home when it’s dark.

I check in on my dogs several times a day, and my current Show does an OK job, but it’s pretty much useless at night. The improved camera on the Show 16 offers a whole new level of functionality.

My biggest concern? The price. The Echo Show 16 starts at $500. If you add any accessories or upgrade to the anti-glare Nanomatte display, you’re closer to $600. That’s double the price of the similarly sized 15-inch Skylight calendar. At the time I’m writing this, a $100 coupon is available, bringing the price down to $400.

I’m already heavily invested in the Amazon ecosystem, so it makes sense that I’d bring a display into the fold as my new smart home centerpiece. A purchase like this is hardly a necessity, but if it can wrangle just a little of my life’s chaos, it’ll be a worthy investment.

The new gaming phone includes an 8,400-mAh battery, easily outdoing the 7,500-mAh battery on the RedMagic 11 Pro and the 5,000-mAh units we often see on mainstream flagship phones like the Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra.

Also around the back are the phone’s 50-megapixel main and 50-megapixel ultrawide cameras.

By comparison, last spring’s RedMagic 11S Pro went on sale for $849 for the same configuration. That goes up to $1,049 if you want 16GB of RAM with 512GB of storage, or $1,199 if you want 1TB of space. These price increases are likely a result of the ongoing RAM shortage that’s making most electronics more expensive.

While the 12 Pro will ship with Android 17, it’s worth noting that RedMagic’s two-year software and three-year security update commitment is quite short for this price range. Most phones that cost $700 and more, including those from Samsung and Google, typically come with seven years of software and security updates.

Following Tuesday’s launch, the RedMagic 12 Pro will have an early bird voucher period starting Nov. 12 for those who want to officially buy the phone on Nov. 17, followed by its open sale date on Nov. 18.

#3177. Samsung adds better sleep tracking to Galaxy Watches – here’s what to expect

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The FDA-approved sleep apnea tracking feature will first come to the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2, Watch 9, and Ring.

Samsung’s sleep-tracking feature has been around on the Samsung Galaxy Watch for a few years now, but it recently got an upgrade: A new AI algorithm, that can deliver more detailed sleep reports than Samsung’s previous sleep apnea detection technology.

Users can see the insights behind their breathing disorders each hour. Prior to this update, the smartwatch would simply detect whether or not a user had sleep apnea but would not provide details on the condition’s specifics or timing.

Users wear a Galaxy Watch to sleep for a few nights, and the sleep apnea results are divided into three stages: mild, occasional, and frequent. Samsung says this classification allows for greater specificity in condition management.

Many smartwatch and health tracker companies have implemented some sleep apnea detection features on their devices. The often underdiagnosed condition impacts around 30 million Americans, according to the American Medical Association (AMA). It’s a condition with gateway effects: if it goes untreated, it can lead to high blood pressure, heart disease, and impaired focus, according to the AMA. Despite sleep apnea’s prevalence, it’s highly treatable — and a great example of wearables passively detecting conditions at home.

Of course, the Galaxy Watch can’t aid in the treatment plan of a sleep apnea diagnosis.

The feature will be available in Korea first, starting in November, with no confirmed date for when it will arrive on US Samsung watches and smart rings. The FDA-cleared feature will first come to the Galaxy Watch Ultra 2, Watch 9, and Ring. Samsung says an update will bring the advanced sleep apnea detection to Galaxy Watch 7 and 8 through future software updates.

To use the feature, a Galaxy user will need to record two nights of sleep within a 10-day period to learn details about their condition.

#3176. Bloom Energy's New Guidance Implies Up to $1.55 Billion in Q4 Revenue. Can It Deliver?

Bloom Energy is growing at a staggering pace, and the company expects its strong momentum to continue into the fourth quarter.

The artificial intelligence data center build-out faces a massive bottleneck: the power grid. Hyperscalers are turning everywhere to secure reliable, cleaner-burning electricity. However, securing direct hookup to the power grid can take up to seven years.

As data centers' energy needs continue to grow, Bloom Energy (BE +9.05%), a power equipment provider, has benefited from this unprecedented demand. The company raked in $1.24 billion in revenue in the third quarter, its second consecutive billion-dollar quarter, and the company expects revenue of up to $1.55 billion in the fourth quarter.

Bloom Energy is seeing staggering demand for its equipment, and now it must deliver on its landmark agreements. Here's what the company must do to deliver for investors in Q4.

Because grid interconnection times are so long, Bloom's fuel cell technology, which runs on natural gas and can be delivered to site in a few short months, has seen incredible demand. For hyperscale data centers, the need for power is real, and they are ready to take delivery as soon as Bloom Energy can ship cells.

Bloom Energy has had a banner year. The company surpassed $1 billion in quarterly revenue for the first time in the second quarter. In Q3, revenue grew another 16% sequentially and 138% year over year. However, operating cash was an outflow of $27.1 million in the quarter, against an inflow of $226.4 million in the second quarter, while capital spending rose to $153.5 million from $51.6 million.

During its Q3 earnings call, Bloom Energy announced it was raising its full-year revenue guidance once again, to between $4.4 billion and $4.6 billion, up from its prior range of $3.9 billion to $4.2 billion. This updated guidance implies Q4 revenue of $1.35 billion to $1.55 billion. Meanwhile, full-year gross margins are projected to be around 34%.

Heading into Q4, Bloom expects to maintain this momentum as the company executes on manufacturing and delivering its solid-oxide fuel cell systems (Bloom Energy Servers) for data-center-related customers, including Oracle, Nebius, data center developer Brookfield Asset Management, and utility provider American Electric Power.

For Bloom Energy, meeting this massive demand is a big undertaking, and management is stepping up to the challenge. During its earnings call with analysts, CEO KR Sridhar said he doesn't believe Bloom itself will be a bottleneck for hardware assembly, and the company noted no electronic or component shortages that will affect its 2026 production. Given the backdrop, I'm optimistic Bloom can deliver another strong quarter for investors.

To meet growing demand for power, Bloom Energy is expanding its manufacturing capacity, with a longer-term goal of manufacturing 5-plus gigawatts (GW) of its solid-oxide fuel cells annually. To do so, Bloom signed a lease in August for a 158,000-square-foot manufacturing and warehouse facility in Fremont, California.

The story surrounding Bloom Energy stock has transformed from that of a clean-technology energy company to that of a crucial infrastructure provider for data centers that need power quickly. As the company sees historic demand for its equipment, it should continue to benefit in the coming years as more data centers come online while the power grid slowly catches up.

#3175. The Bambu Lab H3C Is Here and It’s Powerful and Uncompromising

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The Bambu Lab H3C is a large-format 3D printer with nine printheads, and it's available now.

Multicolor 3D printing has been around for many years. Like all technologies, manufacturers are constantly looking for ways to push it further than ever before. Bambu Lab announced today the H3C, a large-format 3D printer that uses nine separate nozzles to print not only different colors or types, but also different-sized filaments. It’s designed to allow you to mix and match as you see fit, expanding your creative options beyond the standard.

The Bambu Lab H3C starts at $2,500 and comes with the nine-filament system and Automated Material System. I’ve spent the last few weeks using it, and while I haven’t finished my review yet, I already have quite a few thoughts.

This is not a small machine by any measure. I originally had it on my workbench, but the H3C sits so tall that opening the lid and loading the filament was arduous, even for a 6-foot-1 person like myself. Bambu Lab also sent me a metal rolling cart, sold separately, that lowers the machine to a usable height. That said, it still stands 63 inches tall on the cart, so yeah, it’s a big’un.

Despite its size and complex design, setting it up was simple. I would recommend having a second person around to do it, as it’s cumbersome — but not difficult to plug in all the parts. The AMS 3 that sits atop the machine is different from most of the best 3D printers because the H3C requires it operate. It’s not a separate add-on but an integral part of the machine.

You can tell from every metric that the H3C is designed to be a Bambu Lab flagship. It has all the top-of-the-line hardware, including a heatbed that can reach 120 degrees Celcius and an optional but very cool bird’s-eye camera to monitor prints from above. It’s fast, too, with a top toolhead speed of 1,400 mm/s. Of course, that isn’t the print speed, but it does show you the type of speed it could reach if you pushed it to the max.

Printing at high speed does have some disadvantages, though. I have had several print failures on tall prints where the supports or purge tower have fallen due to accidental touches or vibration. The H3C moves fast, prints fast and fails fast if you aren’t careful. Most of those failures occurred at 125% speed, but that’s the standard setting I use for large prints on other machines, and they handle it just fine.

Bambu calls its nine-nozzle system, which heats using conductive heating, the Swaptek system. This isn’t to be confused with another nine-nozzle system you may have heard of: INDX, which uses conductive heating. INDX and Swaptek are extremely similar in execution but differ in a few areas. INDX is purely mechanical, whereas Bambu says its Swaptek system supplies data and power to each tool, allowing the nozzles to start heating faster than those in a standard INDX system — in around 6 seconds per nozzle.

In practice, there isn’t a huge difference between what I’m seeing on the H3C and the Prusa Core One + INDX in terms of speed. The Core One has the advantage that it’s calibrated to avoid a purge tower, and it doesn’t purge as much waste as the Swaptek system. The H3C has its own neat tricks, though: Not only can it print with different nozzle sizes and materials, but it can also print with materials of different diameters.

One of the biggest roadblocks with printing TPU — the very soft, rubbery material that 3D-printed shoes are made from — through an AMS is that it’s too soft to push through the AMS feeder. To combat this, you have to use a harder TPU that isn’t nearly as flexible as you would need for shoes or things that bounce. Adding toolheads capable of printing 2.85mm-diameter materials lets Bambu create TPU that’s more flexible after melting but thicker and easier to feed in the AMS. It works really well.

And it doesn’t stop at TPU, either. You can use 2.85mm PLA with a 0.8mm nozzle to print the infill of a model, while using a 0.2mm nozzle and 1.7mm PLA to print the detailed outer walls. It gives you so many options. The AMS even has two material slots with active heating chambers, so you can print materials that need to be warm and dry, along with other, more shelf-stable materials.

Given the $2,500 price and the H3C’s flagship status, it would be shocking if the print quality were bad. All of the models that didn’t fail due to purge tower collapse look great, and the nine separate nozzles make light work of just about any color project. I love watching this machine print as it swaps across the nozzles at blistering speeds.

Some of them did fail, though, and the H3C was good at catching the errors, pausing the print and letting me know. I chose to keep one of them going despite the failed tower, and it turned out fine, though a little damaged. I had some fun 3D printing my scanned head at life size, using silver for my greying beard — and I love the way it came out.

The CNET test print wasn’t nearly as good as the one the H2C produced, which pretty much maxed out every test, but it wasn’t the worst either. Accuracy was good, and despite the printer’s speed, ringing was kept to a minimum. It struggled on the 0.2mm tolerance test, but it did well on bridging and overhangs, showing that its temperature control is excellent. I still have more of these to do before I’m ready to give a complete score, but so far, I’m happy with the result.

In video games, there’s a concept called endgame content: the harder challenges you tackle after completing most of the story. It’s not for the faint of heart. The H3C is endgame content. It isn’t hard to use, but it’s designed for someone who has gone as far as possible with machines such as the P2S and A1 combos of the world. If you’re looking to go beyond what you thought you could do with a 3D printer, the H3C is the machine to get you there.

Swaptek, INDX and multi-toolhead printers are here to stay. And, like all 3D printers, they can come in many sizes and budgets. The Core One INDX is smaller but cheaper, but the H3C has a lot of options right out of the box to make some incredibly complex models. We live in interesting times and the landscape can only get better.

#3174. Android 17 adoption hits a staggering 1% – why you really should update now

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

Only a quarter of Android phones have even made it to Android 16.

Android 17 adoption hasn’t been speedy. According to the most recent data from heise.de via Android Studio, as reported by Android Authority, Android 17 has reached just 1% of active devices since its release in June 2026.

While Android 16 is finally the most used Google OS for Android devices, at 25.4% of active Android devices, these distribution figures indicate that getting users to upgrade to even newer software is an uphill battle.

It’s true that Android 17 has had less time to reach devices than Android 16 did when the previous distribution snapshot was published, but the minuscule adoption rate underscores how slowly the latest release is infiltrating the Android ecosystem.

Also: How Android 17 stops network snoops and SMS blasters now

Plus, the data show that more Android users than not are running versions that are many years old. I’m all for not fixing what’s not broken, but when Android 14 is the second most widely used version (15.% of devices are still running it), while Android 15 trails not far behind at 14.7%, there’s a pretty large chunk of individuals who may be putting themselves (and their data) at risk.

And it doesn’t end there. Android 13 and Android 11 are still on the table for 11.8% and 10.1% of users, with almost another 10% still using Android 12. That’s about another 30% of users still running Android 13 or older.

Though the numbers indicate that almost 60% of active Android devices are running Android 14 or newer, about 40% of users haven’t even upgraded to Android 14 or later.

Also: This Android 17 setting logs suspicious activity on your phone for troubleshooting

While the numbers are staggering, they’re not surprising. Android updates typically lag behind iOS updates. While the iPhone and its software are both designed, controlled, and distributed by Apple, Android updates must roll out from a single starting point to a wider array of mobile devices and systems to reach users. While Google is responsible for the core of the Android system, those updates must then be curated, reviewed, and pushed to every device that uses Android. This includes phones from Google, Samsung, Nothing, and more, which usually requires adaptations from each manufacturer. Apple’s updates are often adopted faster because there’s no middle entity between iOS and the user, while Android’s process is rather fragmented.

Keep in mind that Google no longer publishes a regularly updated public distribution chart, so this data isn’t from a real-time survey of every Android device. There’s only cumulative adoption data through Android Studio, and these estimates are derived from those figures and cover devices that contacted the Google Play Store during a seven-day period.

It’s always good practice to keep your device updated to the latest available software that your system allows. There are a few great reasons why those eligible for Android 17 should upgrade, including improved features — like the ability to “bubble” apps and stop network snoops and SMS blasters.

Also: I enabled Android’s new security feature that detects fake cell towers – here’s why

Plus, it’s the most proactive thing you can do for your device. Software updates usually include improvements to your phone’s operation, speed, security, and more, and it’s an easy (and free) way to keep your phone healthy.

#3173. After 120 Hours of Testing, This Phone Had the Longest-Lasting Battery Life

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

We benchmarked the battery life on nearly three dozen smartphones from companies like Apple, Samsung, Motorola and Google.

A shiny brand-new phone might not have a longer battery life than your old phone.

The phones that CNET tested in 2026 had, on average, the same battery life as models we tested in 2025 and in 2024. That doesn’t mean that we didn’t have standouts. Apple’s iPhone 18 Pro and 18 Pro Max top our testing results, along with foldable phones like the Motorola Razr (2026) and Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8, each of which has a silicon-carbon battery and offers increased capacity without requiring a larger physical battery. The most recently released device we tested, the iPhone Duo, tied for the top spot in one of our tests.

Late-2025 models like the iPhone 17 and OnePlus 15 (RIP OnePlus) are among the best values for getting the most time between charges.

Having your phone run out of battery is extremely frustrating, especially when it happens at an inconvenient time. No one wants to be seated on the floor of an airport terminal with their phone plugged into an outlet.

Battery life is affected by several factors. If your phone is 2-3 years old, the battery capacity is likely less than when it was fresh out of the box. How much you use your phone affects how long you have between charges. Are you a Light Lift Lisa and rarely on your handset? Or are you an Obsessively Online Oliver and your phone never leaves your hand?

While it seems that companies add larger batteries to new smartphones, those same phones come with new power-hungry features like brighter screens, more powerful processors and AI tools that gobble up that “extra” battery life. And CNET’s phone battery testing shows just this.

CNET tested the battery life of 28 phones in 2026 so far, and our results continue to show that a higher capacity (typically measured in milliamp-hours or mAh) doesn’t always mean the longest-lasting battery throughout the day. These devices, along with four handsets that didn’t get updates this year, are included in our results. Each phone was put through a 3-hour streaming video test and a 45-minute anecdotal stress test, both with the battery at 100%. The results show the remaining battery percentage – the higher, the better. We averaged the results to determine our rankings. 32 phones were each tested for 3 hours and 45 minutes, meaning CNET has spent 120 hours testing smartphone battery life over the past year.

The iPhone 18 Pro Max is our overall winner for having the longest battery life. Apple gave the 18 Pro Max a larger battery compared to the equally impressive 2025 iPhone 17 Pro Max. That increased capacity which along with iOS 27 optimizations and the efficient Apple A20 Pro chip, gave the 18 Pro Max an edge over last year’s model and every phone that we’ve tested in 2026.

Most phones sold in 2026 were hit with a price increase due to US tariffs and higher RAM and component costs driven by demand from AI data centers. That doesn’t mean you can’t find an affordable phone with great battery life. Notably, the $500 Motorola Moto G Stylus (2026) and the $600 Motorola Edge (2026) both rank in our top 10. Interestingly, while Motorola incorporated silicon-carbon batteries into its Razr phone line, these two budget handsets lack them. It’s like that the screens and processors on the Stylus and Edge don’t consume as much power as a $1,000 flagship phone with all the bells and whistles.

If you’re looking for an affordable iOS phone, you might need to adjust your definition. The cheapest phone Apple sells is the $699 iPhone 17E, which I’d argue isn’t what’s considered a budget device. However, the 17E lasted a long time in our tests and its performance tied with phones that are nearly twice and three times its $700 price: the $1,400 Motorola Razr Ultra (2026) and the $1,999 Apple iPhone Duo – both foldable phones.

We tested a variety of phones from brands you know well, like Samsung, Google and Apple, to handsets you likely never heard of, like Xiaomi’s wonderful photography-centric Leica Leitzphone, or the controversial “gold” T1 Phone from Trump Mobile (a rebranded 2024 HTC U24 Pro nicknamed the “Trump Phone”) that scored toward the bottom of our ranking.

We run two battery-life tests on all the phones we review at CNET: one that stresses the phone and the other for video streaming. In our 45-minute stress test, during which we stream videos, make a video call, play games and scroll social media, and with the battery starting at 100%, the iPhone 18 Pro Max and iPhone Duo took the top spots, only dropping 1%. Google’s Pixel 11 Pro XL deserves a shoutout for tying for third, losing only 3%. Compare that with the 2025 Pixel 10 Pro XL, which dropped by 7% in the same test.

In the 3-hour video battery test, where a video is streamed over Wi-Fi for 3 hours with the screen at full brightness and the battery starting at 100%, the smaller iPhone 18 Pro took the top spot, losing only 6%. The iPhone 17 deserves your attention, as it scored among the top results for both tests, as did the Motorola Razr (2026) and (more expensive) Razr Ultra (2026) flip phones.

If you’re wondering how to remember all these model names and qualifiers like Pro, Ultra, XL and Max, we also looked at how all the models from a specific brand did by averaging the results. We only did this for companies where we tested more than one model. So if you’re looking for the brand that sells phones with the best battery life, go with Apple.

This is the second year in a row that the iPhone lineup has performed better in our battery life tests than other brands. OnePlus, a subsidiary of the Chinese brand Oppo, came in second but sadly announced that it will stop releasing phones in the US after the OnePlus 15 series. So if you see a great deal on the OnePlus 15 or budget OnePlus 15R, they are both excellent options for amazing battery life.

This year we saw Motorola and Samsung begin adopting silicon-carbon batteries in some models, mostly foldable phones. But I imagine in 2027 and beyond that we’ll see more companies using that type of power cell.

Lastly, if you’re happy with your current phone and your only complaint is that it doesn’t hold a charge like when you first got it, don’t buy a new phone. Instead, replace the battery, either yourself or have a service do it for you. If you’re an iPhone owner, the Apple Store will do this for $80-$100, depending on the model and how much your battery has degraded. Replacing the battery will save you hundreds of dollars and add a few more years to your phone’s life-span.Of course, there are always power banks to help keep your phone’s battery topped off, too.

Black Friday is just weeks away and there are already deals on power banks, including this one specifically designed for Apple’s iPhone Duo foldable.

#3172. CoreWeave credit default swaps hit distress levels as investors sour on AI debt

CoreWeave's credit stress continued to spiral on Thursday amid a broader sell-off in AI-related names.

CoreWeave — a so-called neocloud company — has taken on more than $51 billion of debt and obligations to finance its AI infrastructure build-out. It's now facing a rebellion from stock and bond investors as concerns mount about AI overspending and debt levels.

Yields on CoreWeave debt have continued to climb, with the yield to maturity on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 reaching 15.78%. Bond yields move inversely to prices. Meanwhile, the spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, at one point soared above 1,140 basis points. It last stood at nearly 1,038 bps — a 16% week-over-week jump and 164% higher than a year ago.

Credit concerns are spreading into AI equities too. CoreWeave's stock dropped during trading Thursday before recovering and closing 5.94% higher. The moved followed Wednesday's 15.5% nosedive.

Other neocloud stocks were under similar pressure. Nebius also sank before climbing higher to close 1.29% and and Iren ultimately ended the day down less than 1%.

Anxiety about AI overspending is also spreading. For instance, shares of Micron Technology and Advanced Micro Devices sold off following a big sell-off in South Korean chip stocks. Micron closed up 1.87%, while AMD fell 1.5%.

While AI debt worries have been rising steadily, investors have suddenly grown skittish about the underlying finances of companies borrowing heavily to buy chips, build data centers, and rent out computing power.

Coreweave's credit default swaps are one place to see that stress.

Credit default swaps are contracts where buyers pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. The spread widening in CoreWeave's CDS means investors view the debt as riskier.

The market is "basically saying 'no mas' to AI debt without operating cash flow support," the Evercore ISI trading desk said.

A spread level of 1,000 basis points or above is generally a sign of credit distress, said Gavan Nolan, executive director of fixed income pricing at S&P Global Market Intelligence.

No one is questioning whether demand for CoreWeave's services is real, since the company has contracts with all the big hyperscalers, Nolan said. The question is whether the cash flows will ultimately arrive.

"It's obviously heavily negative free cash flow at the moment, and it needs that cash flow to arrive really to justify all the capex that it's spending," Nolan said.

CEO Mike Intrator dismissed the market moves on Wednesday, according to a memo obtained by CNBC.

"When you are building a new type of technology company there will be periods of time when the market misunderstands and acts irrationally," he wrote. "We are confident in our backlog which more than supports all of our debt obligations."

The backlog is over $100 billion, with around two-thirds of it with investment-grade counterparts including Microsoft, Meta, IBM and Caterpillar, Deutsche Bank said in a note Thursday. The firm reiterated its buy-rating on the stock Wednesday, calling the dislocation a buying opportunity.

"The problem is this kind of concern can prove to be a self-fulfilling prophecy, and risks triggering broader issues in other AI-related names," said Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research.

Domino effects may already be happening. Oracle's five-year CDS spread also spiked this week. It was last at 282 basis points, a roughly 10% increase in one week.

"A downgrade from its BBB rating would risk pressuring IG-only investors into forced sales," Reid said.

#3171. Following the Crowd: Investing and Social Media

If you use social media for investment information, here are some tips to consider.

Social media has fundamentally changed how some people access financial information and connect with other investors. While these platforms can offer exposure to a broad range of investment ideas and conversations, you should carefully evaluate the information you find there.

According to the FINRA Investor Education Foundation's (FINRA Foundation) 2025 National Financial Capability Study Investor Report, a majority (60 percent) of investors under 35 cite social media as an information source. And 61 percent of that age group follows financial influencers, or "finfluencers," who create and share financial content on social media and sometimes receive compensation for doing so.

Others follow the buzz around meme stocks, investments that gain popularity primarily based on social media sentiment. Among investors under 35 surveyed by the FINRA Foundation, 29 percent report purchasing meme stocks or other viral investments. And while younger investors rely on social media to a greater degree, investors of all ages report using it as an information source.

If you use social media for investment information, here are some tips to consider.

1. Understand the Information Sources. Social media content can include interactive discussions between numerous users, finfluencer videos and promotions, posts from well-known industry professionals and more.

If you're taking tips from a celebrity or social media personality, consider that person's qualifications. Are they a registered financial professional? Do they have a verifiable financial degree or employment experience in the financial services field? Check out those who tout registration or financial qualifications by using FINRA BrokerCheck or the SEC's Investment Adviser Public Disclosure database, and contact your state regulator. You can also use FINRA's Professional Designations Database to help research credentials promoters might claim to hold.

Also keep in mind that individuals who aren't registered financial professionals often aren't required to disclose conflicts of interest. Ask yourself what they might have to gain by promoting a particular product or service, including potential compensation for the post itself or your engagement with it. Remember that even those with legitimate credentials might not provide investment advice that fits your personal circumstances.

And don't rely solely on social media for your investment advice. Whether you invest on your own or work with an investment professional, use a variety of information sources such as company financial statements and regulatory filings, financial publications, and research from brokerage firms and independent analysts. Tools such as FINRA's Market Data and Investments Explorer and Fund Analyzer are also helpful resources.2. Recognize Your Motivations. Social media thrives on emotional engagement. Try to separate your feelings from your finances. If you find yourself getting swept up in excitement or fear of missing out (FOMO) due to investment-related social media content, it's best to pause and evaluate.

Before making an investment decision, ask yourself whether it aligns with your financial goals. Make sure you understand and are comfortable with the level of risk involved. Your investment risk tolerance is very personal and should take into account your investment objectives, time horizon, financial circumstances and needs, and personality, among other things.

3. Know What You're Investing In. Some social media forums encourage buying investment products or using investing strategies that can be risky or complex.

Meme stocks, for example, are shares of companies that become extremely popular and heavily traded based on social media hype and online community enthusiasm rather than traditional measures of value, such as a company's performance. This online buzz can drive share prices up rapidly, but meme stocks can be extremely volatile and unpredictable. Their prices can fall as quickly as they rise.

And while all investing comes with risk, some securities--such as options and leveraged products--involve unique added risks and considerations. Be aware, too, that trading in a margin account also presents additional risks, including the risk that you can lose more funds than you deposit in the account.

Always do your research to understand the key aspects and risks of investment products and strategies before choosing them for yourself.

4. Focus on a Balanced Strategy. Some social media platforms and brokerage firms offer tools that analyze or aggregate information from social media sources. While they can be useful for identifying market trends or gauging investor sentiment on particular stocks or sectors, such tools might also miss tone or context and be affected by data quality issues. And depending on how it's presented, social sentiment information--particularly real-time discussion platforms and buy/sell indicators driven by social sentiment--can sometimes lead to emotionally driven or impulsive investment decisions that you might later regret.

Stick to a disciplined, goal-based investment process tailored to your needs. And be wary of social promotions urging hyper-aggressive strategies like taking early withdrawals from retirement accounts or borrowing against your home to make investments.

Weigh the potential pros and cons of any potential investment carefully, and don't compromise your financial security by investing money you can't afford to lose. Consider talking with an investment or tax professional about your personal circumstances.

5. Stay Alert for Fraud. FINRA Foundation research shows that social media users are more likely than non-users to exhibit overconfidence in their level of investment knowledge. This gap can lead to costly mistakes--and make investors more vulnerable to fraud. Indeed, in a 2026 research brief, the FINRA Foundation found that social media users and finfluencer followers reported losing money to investment fraud at a much higher rate (68 percent and 69 percent, respectively) than those not using social media or following finfluencers (26 percent and 29 percent, respectively).

Some bad actors exploit the emotional or community-seeking aspects of social media to commit fraud, sometimes posing as registered representatives or inviting investors to join "investment groups" online. If you participate in financial discussions online, be vigilant: Don't reveal any of your personal financial information, and know the red flags of fraud, including guaranteed returns, pressure to act quickly and demands for secrecy.

If you have information about potentially fraudulent, illegal or unethical activity, contact your local law enforcement, and submit a regulatory tip to FINRA. If you think you've been the victim of any cyber-enabled scam, file a report with the FBI's Internet Crime Complaint Center.

Invest better with The Motley Fool. Get stock recommendations, portfolio guidance, and more from The Motley Fool's premium services.

Making the world smarter, happier, and richer.

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Apple’s HomeView smart home hub could soon double as an indoor security camera via HomeKit Secure Video, according to settings discovered by a Reddit user pdfu.

Also: 5 ways HomeView could make me switch from Echo

Once these settings are enabled, users can configure HomeView to detect motion, stream real-time video, and record video. These strings appear in HomeView software version 27.2, suggesting that Apple will wrap this feature into an upcoming software update. Still, there’s a possibility that this feature gets delayed to an even later software release.

Via HomeKit Security Camera, only users registered to the home can view HomeView’s video streams and recordings. Additionally, a green status light will appear, and a chime will sound to alert house dwellers that HomeView is recording or someone is starting a camera stream.

Finally, all members registered to the Home app will receive a notification when someone else changes HomeKit Secure Video settings, such as allowing HomeView to detect motion or record video.

HomeView may have four video options: Off, Detect Activity, Stream, and Stream & Allow Recordings. With Detect Activity, home members would be alerted to motion but won’t be able to rewatch who or what triggered the notification.

Stream would allow users to stream video from HomeView in real time, but the home hub won’t record the livestream. Finally, Stream & Allow Recordings would allow home members to stream video from HomeView in real time, with all members able to access its recordings.

Also: Hold onto your old HomePod – an upgrade is on the way

Code strings also point to customization options, such as allowing HomeView to detect motion, stream, or record video only when you’re home, away, or asleep, using home members’ iPhone or other device locations to determine proximity.

Apple’s bottom-up ecosystem control gives it an advantage over competing home security brands like Ring, Nest, and Wyze. Users can easily access their HomeView camera stream without a third-party app or delayed notifications.

However, Apple’s greatest advantage may be HomeView’s potential versatility; instead of purchasing a separate camera device, HomeView lets users interact with Siri while cooking, be used as an intercom throughout the house, lock and unlock compatible smart locks from its display, and view an in-home camera feed all from one screen.

#3169. Firefox’s Built-In VPN Hides Your IP Address at No Cost. What You Should Know

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Firefox’s built-in VPN masks your IP address while you browse, gives you 50GB a month, and only requires a free Mozilla account.

Firefox comes with 50GB of free VPN browsing every month, and all it asks for is a free Mozilla account. Mozilla added the built-in VPN to desktop Firefox in March, then brought it to Android in July. It routes your browser traffic through a proxy server, hiding your IP address and location from the sites you visit, and you can choose which server location to use. It doesn’t protect other apps on your device, but for everyday browsing, it covers a lot without a subscription.

Keep in mind that free VPNs can be dangerous. If they’re not from a trusted provider, they can put your data at risk or include vulnerabilities you wouldn’t find in some of the more popular paid VPN services.

In CNET’s tests, among VPN services that offer a free tier, the best free plan on the market is Proton VPN’s free service. (It’s the only free VPN CNET currently recommends.) But the free Proton VPN service is missing some features found in the company’s premium plan, such as the ability to choose a server manually or connect multiple devices at the same time.

Mozilla’s overall VPN technology has undergone independent audits from Cure53, has resolved security issues over its history and uses WireGuard, which gives it a good security foundation.

The browser-based free version may give the impression that it offers the same level of overall protection as a stand-alone VPN. However, it only protects web traffic viewed through the Firefox browser.

“The fundamental limitation is scope,” said Jacob Kalvo, a cybersecurity expert and CEO of Live Proxies, which provides technical services to businesses and individuals. “[The free Firefox VPN] only protects browser traffic, not apps, system processes or other network activity. That creates a false sense of ‘full protection’ for less technical users.”

That could make it a useful feature for casual use while browsing the web for those who don’t already have a VPN service. And Kalvo says the 50GB data limit is generous for a browser-based VPN.

But, he said, for anything involving “sensitive data, competitive intelligence, or large-scale operations,” he doesn’t recommend it.

#3166. Firefox’s new Mini Window is every multitasker’s dream – Chrome can’t do this

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The open-source browser's newest feature lets you pop out a page - or part of a page - into its own mini window. See why this is incredibly cool.

The developers at Mozilla are always hard at work to make Firefox the most useful browser on the market. To that end, they’ve added a new feature (still in beta), called Mini Window. I started using Mini Window as soon as it appeared in Firefox Nightly, and I’ve been very happy with how it performs.

Also: Why I’m sticking with Firefox as my browser

The idea behind the Firefox Mini Window feature is simple: allow users to move a currently open tab (or a section of a tab) into a separate mini window. Think of it as moving a tab from one window to what appears like a web app. Essentially, I can move a page (or section) into what looks like a browser window, minus the browser bits. At first blush, there’s no address bar, controls, scroll bar, or anything else that would normally be associated with Firefox. The Mini Window appears to be a web app, without having to actually create it.

However, when I hover my cursor inside the Mini Window, I do see the address bar, the back/foward/reload buttons, and the window close button– and that’s it. The browser gets out of your way, so the only thing I see is the content.

Also: Firefox and Chrome patch 200+ security flaws combined

On top of that (pun intended), the Mini Window stays on top of other Firefox windows, so I can have that popped-out content always in focus while I work on other things. This feature comes in handy when I’m working with limited display space (say, on a laptop) and need to reference a page while working on another. For multitasking, it comes in very handy.

Using the Firefox Mini Window feature is quite simple, but until the feature comes out of beta, it can only be found in Firefox Nightly. I’ve been using Nightly as my default browser on both Linux and MacOS for some time now and have yet to run into a problem (YMMV).

For those who don’t want to risk using a potentially less-than-stable alpha version of Firefox, know that the Mini Window feature is slated to move to stable on November 10. Update Firefox on that day and can start enjoying the feature without upgrading to Nightly.

Until then, you can test Mini Window by installing Firefox Nightly, which can run alongside the stable Firefox release.

Also: Firefox just fixed my biggest annoyance with web browsers

Once I installed Nightly, there was nothing more to do. I didn’t have to enable Mini Window, as it’s already there. I simply had to right-click on an open tab and select Move Tab > Move to Mini Window.

Once I did that, the mini window popped out and hovered over Firefox. If I hovered my cursor over the mini window, the address bar and title bar for the window appeared, so I could drag the pop-up to wherever I needed.

I could also open a specific section of a tab in a Mini Window. To do that, I highlighted the section in question, right-clicked the section, and then clicked Move Section to Mini Window.

Let’s say I’m writing a paper on Linux and need to read a particular article for background information. I could have my document open in the main window, and then open the article in question in a Mini Window. I could then reference the article without having to click back and forth between them — even when working on a smaller display.

Here’s another use case I’ve found: When using MacOS’s Stage Manager feature, I only have one window available at a time. The Mini Window bypasses that limitation, so I can have my main browser window focused as well as the page open in the Mini Window.

Another handy way I like to use Mini Window is to open a favorite search engine, move it to a Mini Window, and then I have an always-ready search tool at my fingertips. I could also open my favorite AI service and use it in the same way.

The closest thing Chrome has to Mini Window is Picture-in-Picture mode and the Mini Player. The caveat to Chrome’s feature is that those two options are only used for video and media playback. The next closest thing Chrome has is the ability to save a website as a web app, which you could then open alongside the main Chrome window. That feature, however, isn’t nearly as flexible as Firefox’s Mini Window option, and it’s not guaranteed to always hover over the main window.

#3164. Prediction: This Will Be SpaceX's Stock Price by the End of 2027

Elon Musk's tech giant continues to wow the market.

This has been an exciting year for stock market investors, and few companies exemplify this more than Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +3.62%). The diversified technology giant went public at a record valuation of $1.77 trillion, driven by optimism about its space and generative artificial intelligence (AI) businesses. Recent results haven't disappointed.

But will SpaceX's booming operational growth reward investors? Let's dig into the pros and cons of the stock to decide if it can maintain its momentum and try to project what the company might be worth by the end of next year.

SpaceX has been around since 2002. And at the time of its initial public offering (IPO) 24 years later, many investors were justifiably worried that the company's best days of growth were behind it. However, recent earnings results are helping put these concerns to rest.

Third-quarter revenue skyrocketed 161% year over year to $14.1 billion, driven by the company's artificial intelligence segment, which it now calls "super intelligence" (SI). This business has been formed by assets SpaceX acquired with its purchase of xAI earlier this year. It includes the Grok large language model (LLM) along with vast quantities of data center hardware designed to run and train AI algorithms.

With a market share of just 7%, Grok has fallen behind frontier models like ChatGPT and Claude in enterprise adoption. However, instead of betting all its chips in the risky and expensive LLM arms race, SpaceX has decided to focus on AI infrastructure.

The company is now renting out its unused AI compute capacity to former rivals like Alphabet, which is now paying SpaceX $920 million per month, and Anthropic, which is paying $1.25 billion per month. These huge deals are causing SpaceX's revenue to skyrocket, with Chief Executive Officer Elon Musk claiming the company could earn $500 billion to $1 trillion in recurring revenue by the end of 2027 because of soaring demand for AI compute.

Musk's long-term vision for SpaceX may involve a combination with his other public company, Tesla, which specializes in electric vehicles (EVs), autonomous driving, and hardware production. On the surface, this combination could benefit both companies by unifying Musk's attention and leadership and allowing the two companies to work together on projects (such as hardware production) without potential conflicts of interest.

The combination would also give SpaceX investors access to some of Tesla's most exciting projects, such as robotaxis and humanoid robots. That said, both of those businesses remain highly speculative.

In the meantime, Tesla is arguably a stagnating EV business (third-quarter revenue was little changed at $281 billion) with an extremely high valuation of almost 14 times sales. With numbers like that, it's hard to see the merger creating real value for SpaceX investors anytime soon.

If SpaceX lives up to Musk's lofty projections and earns $1 trillion in annual revenue by the end of 2027, the company would need a market capitalization of $3.8 trillion to hit the S&P 500's average price-to-sales (P/S) multiple of 3.8. And with a current valuation of $1.85 trillion, that would represent a gain of more than 105%, or a stock price of $281.

Unfortunately, however, Elon Musk has a track record of overpromising and underdelivering. And investors shouldn't expect his SpaceX projections to be an exception.

Musk is assuming that current levels of AI compute demand will continue to accelerate, which is far from guaranteed. In fact, the current situation shows signs of being a bubble. And it's just as likely that AI companies will slow their spending to avoid spiraling losses. While there will probably be ups and downs, it wouldn't surprise me if SpaceX stock continues to trade at about $150 by the end of 2027.

New reports indicate the touchscreens are to become the standard on premium MacBook Pros.

According to reports, Apple will eliminate non-touch versions of the premium MacBook Pros as soon as next year. This would make touchscreen technology a defining feature of MacBook Pro laptops with the Pro and Max chipsets, separating them from the entry-level MacBook Pros with base-model chips.

The higher-end 16-inch MacBook Pro configurations without the touchscreen will be discontinued, Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman reported in the Power On newsletter, with the first models arriving in 2027.

Also: Apple’s new touchscreen MacBook Pros may be late, but they have one big advantage

The 14-inch MacBook Pro will remain the affordable option within the lineup and is unlikely to receive a touchscreen upgrade. It is predicted to feature an M7 processor and a redesigned thinner chassis.

Also: Forget the touchscreen MacBook – Apple’s new M6 is the laptop I recommend for most people

Also: How to use your TV as a computer monitor – before you buy a new one

In recent years, the biggest factors that have bumped a MacBook model up to a new price category have been processor performance, memory, graphics capabilities, and, to a lesser extent, display quality. Now, the touchscreen appears to be a major component.

The difference between a touchscreen MacBook Pro and a non-touchscreen model could fundamentally change how buyers evaluate and compare the MacBook Pro with other Apple laptops. Instead of simply deciding how much processing power consumers need, they might also have to decide whether a touchscreen is worth the steep price.

#3162. How AI Data Centers Became a Central Issue in Today’s Midterm Election

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Server farms for AI have become a uniting issue for voters and a bipartisan headache for candidates.

For years we’ve heard concerns from residents near hyperscale data centers at city council meetings and other public hearings in towns across the country. Now, those fears — about the environment, rising electric costs and general quality of life — are playing a big role in voters’ decisions as they head to the polls to vote in Tuesday’s midterm election.

Recent polls show majorities of Democrats and independents, along with a sizable share of Republicans, oppose new data centers in their areas. That leaves candidates in both parties answering questions about projects once courted by state and local leaders. Supporters say the facilities bring jobs, tax revenue and an advantage in AI development.

Further, a new poll from ABC News, the Washington Post and Ipsos found that a majority of Americans (57%) believe AI is bad for society, with many more pointing to its dangers than the potential benefits the tech could bring. An even larger number of those polled (66%) are opposed to the construction of new data centers in the US, and more (72%) would be opposed if it were in their community.

Opposition to AI crosses party lines. The technology has faced a surge in public disapproval this year, putting political candidates on blast and potentially affecting outcomes in key races, political and polling experts told CNET.

Ambivalence about AI also feeds into negativity toward data centers, which have, in a short time, become a rare bipartisan issue everyone’s talking about, according to Christopher Lee, partner and senior political strategist for Foresight Strategic Advisors.

Lee, who has closely followed election trends in southwestern states such as Nevada and Arizona, said the issue of data centers — massive AI server and compute farms that consume vast electricity and water resources — is going to “hammer” some candidates, especially those who weren’t transparent with constituents in the past about the cost to communities. If a politician voted for the construction of a data center earlier this year, their “political career is going to run into a stone wall,” he said.

The ABC News/Washington Post/Ipsos survey asked a wide range of questions about how Americans will vote in the midterms and what they think of the political parties. Alongside the economy, immigration and the war against Iran, the AI rollout is a critical issue.

As noted by CNET reporter Katelyn Chedraoui, AI regulation and data center construction have been the subject of controversy, protests and increased legal action in communities.

The poll confirmed as much: 67% of respondents felt the government isn’t doing enough to regulate AI, with risks including widespread job losses, psychological dependency, economic inequality and even human extinction. Fears and negativity toward AI also extend to the election process itself, with AI election interference among Americans’ top 10 fears for 2026.

A national PR campaign funded by tech leaders and supported by the Trump administration, meant to tout the alleged benefits of data centers, such as job creation and technological advancements, doesn’t seem to be having the intended effect. Some politicians, especially those running for congressional seats, seem to be better at reading the room.

According to Lindsey Cormack, an associate professor of political science at Stevens Institute of Technology and co-host of the podcast Government That Doesn’t Suck, many candidates are paying close attention to how they communicate about AI developments, with a particular focus on the problems they present rather than the benefits they provide.

“It’s difficult to persuade people with promises of economic development when they’re worried about their electricity bills or water supply,” she said.

Cormack, creator of the congressional newsletter archive DCinbox, has tracked a sharp rise in lawmakers linking data centers to consumer energy costs. In an email to CNET, she noted that between January and September 2025, just 5% of Republican newsletters discussing data centers mentioned electricity bills or ratepayers (4 of 76). Over the same period in 2026, that figure surged to 54% (97 of 180). Democrats showed a similar focus, with 40% of their data center newsletters (71 of 176) raising the issue in 2026.

The midterms won’t be the end of the battle. AI and data centers are “going to be an issue in 2028 and 2030 and way beyond that,” Lee said.
