#3161. Athenahealth patients can ask Claude about their medical records now

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The partnership could provide one of the nation’s largest AI companies with a bevy of personal medical data.

A major US healthcare technology provider just teamed up with Anthropic to connect patient health records with Claude.

Athenahealth, a healthcare technology provider serving over 20% of the US population, launched a Model Context Protocol (MCP) compatible server that connects to Anthropic’s Claude. The server, named AthenaPatient MCP, allows the AI model to gather information from health records and answer related questions. The process is secure, according to Athenahealth, and requires patients to sign in with their account credentials.

AthenaPatient MCP is available only to Claude Pro and Claude Max users, which cost $20 and $100 per month, respectively. Not all Athenahealth patients will have their data shared with Claude, and not all Claude Pro and Claude Max users must opt in. Users must give Claude their authenticated Athenahealth credentials, can choose which health information Claude can access, and can revoke access at any time, according to the press release.

Also: The wearable health boom is creating a data overload for doctors – what happens next

Once authorized, Claude takes patients’ information like lab results, vitals, medications, health conditions, immunizations, visit history, diagnostic reports, allergies, procedures, and care plans into account when answering questions.

The AI partnership does not provide medical advice, according to Sam Lambson, vice president of product management, data & ecosystem at Athenahealth, who responded to ZDNET in an email. It also should not replace a clinician’s professional judgment or medical advice. “Patients should contact a qualified healthcare professional with questions about diagnosis, treatment, or changes to their care,” Lambson said.

It appears that the AthenaPatient MCP tool is designed to help patients better understand the information provided by health professionals, rather than to give them medical advice. So, one may ask AthenaPatient MCP about their latest blood test results, such as “What was my most recent red blood cell count?” but users shouldn’t expect Claude to explain how an abnormally high or low count may affect them.

Athenahealth says that the agreement prohibits Anthropic from using data accessed through this connection to train its models or improve its products.

Partnerships between big tech and big healthcare aren’t new. In fact, the collaboration between Athenahealth and Anthropic follows a larger trend across the health tech industry. Earlier this year, Microsoft teamed up with Mayo Clinic to develop an AI model for healthcare; Boston Children’s Hospital forged a partnership with OpenAI to develop an enterprise AI system for use across research, clinical, and administrative teams, according to a press release.

Also: Before you buy a smartwatch or smart ring, consider what you’re giving up

A growing number of Americans are consulting AI for medical advice. A recent survey from Pew Research Center found that 34% of US adults have talked with a chatbot for medical advice. This partnership makes the process slightly more secure by authorizing patient account credentials before granting the model access to health records.

Health data is extremely valuable, and tech companies are increasingly interested in partnering with healthcare providers to access it. Much of the “AI could cure cancer” messaging we hear relies on these humongous companies obtaining enough health data to train their AI models and link disease data to patient data. This is not to say, however, that solving this complex issue is the motive behind the partnership between Athenahealth and Anthropic.

Also: Asking AI for medical advice? There’s a right and wrong way, one doctor explains

A more reasonable explanation for such a partnership is that an AI that can answer patient queries could eliminate some of the admin work doctors do between patient visits, like answering questions about test results, giving Athenahealth doctors some time back.

Will partnerships like Anthropic and Athenahealth’s improve patient outcomes? That remains to be seen, but a recent survey by Pew Research Center found that Americans aren’t that confident on the matter. The largest shares of respondents still preferred that a human oversee AI when it’s used in a clinical context.

#3159. Andy Jassy Just Delivered Magnificent News for Amazon Investors

Artificial intelligence could fuel mind-boggling revenue growth for Amazon's cloud business.

Many artificial intelligence (AI) stocks are in the throes of a sharp correction right now, as investors wrestle with concerns about the sustainability of the data center infrastructure spending boom. As a result, Amazon (AMZN +2.06%) stock was down 15% from its August record high as of the market close last Friday.

But the sell-off doesn't really jibe with the strong set of third-quarter operating results the company reported on Oct. 29, particularly from its Amazon Web Services (AWS) cloud computing platform. While discussing the results on a conference call with investors, Amazon chief executive Andy Jassy offered a series of forecasts for AWS that suggests even better times are ahead.

Here's what he said and why it could fuel a sustained long-term rally in Amazon stock.

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy. Image source: Amazon.com.

AWS offers hundreds of solutions to help businesses thrive in the digital age, from simple data storage to complex software development tools. However, its expanding portfolio of AI services is the main driver of the recent acceleration in its revenue growth.

AWS operates data centers all over the world that are filled with advanced AI chips from suppliers like Nvidia, and it rents the computing capacity to enterprise customers for a fee. But Amazon also designed its own chips, like Graviton and Trainium, which it says can offer 30% to 40% better price performance than alternatives. In the third quarter, revenue commitments for these chips increased by a mind-boggling 20 times compared to the year-ago period.

But in addition to hardware, enterprises need access to a broad variety of ready-made foundation models to accelerate the development of AI software applications. AWS Bedrock offers more than 100 of them from leading companies like OpenAI, Anthropic, Mistral AI, and Meta Platforms. During the third quarter, Amazon said Fortune 500 companies ramped up their spending on Bedrock sevenfold compared to the same time last year, with token volume growing by 17 times. Jassy says Bedrock is the fastest growing multibillion-dollar business in AWS's history.

All of this contributed to a record $45.9 billion in AWS revenue in the third quarter, a 39% increase from the year-ago period. It was the sixth-straight quarter in which revenue growth accelerated, emphasizing the platform's incredible momentum.

But all signs point to even faster growth in the future. AWS ended the third quarter with a $578 billion order backlog from customers who were waiting for more data center capacity to come online, which increased by a triple-digit percentage. Moreover, Jassy told investors that AWS could grow its revenue by more than 40% in both 2027 and 2028.

To top things off, he also said AWS could become a trillion-dollar revenue business within the next five to 10 years. For some perspective, its current annual revenue run-rate is $184 billion.

Amazon is on track to spend around $220 billion on building data center infrastructure in 2026, and it plans to spend even more in 2027, which will help AWS convert its enormous backlog into revenue. The company can't deduct these costs upfront because data centers have a useful life of several years, so the money it's spending today could impact its earnings in 2027, 2028, 2029, and beyond.

Therefore, it's critical that AWS generates a positive return on all of that spending. Fortunately, Jassy says the data center investments are likely to deliver even higher yields, and even more free cash flow than he could have imagined, which is great news for investors.

Amazon delivered earnings of $12.37 per share over the last four quarters, placing its stock at a price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of just 19.4, a steep discount to the Nasdaq-100 index which has a P/E of 29.9. In other words, Amazon looks heavily undervalued compared to a group of its big-tech peers.

But there is one issue. Amazon had over $68 billion in non-operating profit during the first three quarters of 2026, mainly from paper gains on its investment in Anthropic, which was completely unrelated to its core businesses. Wall Street analysts think Amazon's earnings will decline to $10.58 per share in 2027 (according to Yahoo! Finance), because Anthropic is unlikely to continue rising in value at the same pace.

Therefore, Amazon looks slightly more expensive on a forward basis, with a P/E of 22.8.

But that is still a very attractive valuation in my opinion. Given the blistering growth in AWS and the trillion-dollar revenue opportunity ahead, I think Amazon stock could be a great long-term buy at the current price.

#3158. 50 AI agents tried to build a thriving Minecraft civilization – it didn’t end well

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

It turns out even bots can't escape bureaucracy.

How do AI agents work together? Why do they make certain choices in certain situations — and how can understanding that better prepare us for a future full of them?

Also: OpenAI’s attack agent did exactly what it was told

Recent agent-led incidents have demonstrated their ability to create both powerful breakthroughs and cybersecurity nightmares. In an effort to better trace these outcomes, researchers at Y Combinator and Olam Labs put 50 agents inside Minecraft to see how they behaved. What happened next revealed similarities in how agents act in different contexts.

Researchers conducted several game-centric experiments, beginning with Minecraft because its “environmental hazards and survival goals” make it ideal for testing agents’ swarm behaviors. Minecraft has also already been used by AI labs including OpenAI and Google DeepMind to test agents.

To start, they put 50 agents, each running OpenAI’s GPT-6.1 Sol model, into the game for three hours, with the assigned goal of building a “thriving civilization.” The agents had a directory listing each agent and a shared sandbox, and could DM each other. Researchers built and open-sourced a new agent harness — the infrastructure that turns a model into an autonomous system and not just a chatbot, like OpenClaw — for the experiment.

The agents moved quickly at first, harvesting trees and forming leadership based solely on the order they appeared in their directory, without any voting, elections, or challenges from other agents. But they ended up caught in virtual paperwork and unable to sustainably feed themselves.

Also: Do you need AI agent liability insurance? What to know before you sign up

“Four agents spent 11 minutes appointing a ‘sole fertilizer operator’ for a single piece of bone meal on a single wheat block. Other invented roles included ‘fishing shift,’ ‘rod holder’ and ‘hub steward.’ Agents asked permission to remove grass,” the researchers noted. Perhaps these tendencies are common in human-sourced corporate training data.

Agents also seemed to become more cautious in reaction to deaths within the game. “Twenty-two of the run’s 34 agent deaths occurred in the first night. They responded to this by becoming increasingly risk-averse,” the researchers wrote. “Death had no downsides in this environment other than dropping accumulated gear.” Despite simply respawning when they died, the agents eventually avoided riskier tasks and “huddled in shelters” instead.

The experiment’s architects found this trend toward caution and minutiae “somewhat surprising,” but noted that it shared similarities with how coding agents behave.

Also: These Claude Enterprise features let you track AI ROI

“For example, multistep agent reviews can often result in fixating on a specific step or role. Agents also tend to become overly cautious, especially when it comes to security and updating production services. These seem like common threads between the huddled agents of our Minecraft colony and the ones we work with as programmers every day,” the researchers observed.

In another experiment, researchers also pitted 10 Anthropic Claude Opus 5.5 agents against 10 GPT 6.1 Sol agents in a game called Bed Wars, where teams have to destroy their opponent’s bed while protecting their own. Told simply to “win the game,” GPTs won.

“In almost every run, the GPT agents were much more aggressive and coordinated in their strategies to destroy the Claude team’s bed,” Y Combinator said in an X thread recapping the Bed Wars findings. They noted that the GPT agents used a more shorthand style of communication and sent far more messages, while Claude “signed its name and said ‘we'” much more often.

GPT-5.6 Sol agents also beat Opus 5.5 agents six to one in a factory-building game called Mindustry that also uses military strategy.

“Claudes generally established democracies, GPTs formed hives,” Y Combinator said on X, adding that GPTs would seek out source code when a game was too close in order to find winning advantages. That behavior is consistent with agents’ tendency to cheat, take shortcuts, or otherwise find any and all ways to complete a given task, which can have serious consequences when that involves breaking explicit rules set by human developers.

Also: Stop sharing your banking passwords with AI agents

“Claudes did not search for alternative advantages,” the researchers clarified.

This experiment isn’t entirely the first of its kind. In May, Emergence AI created eight societies run by different families of foundation models, meaning a Claude-specific “world,” a Grok world, and so on. Perhaps predictably, the Grok world was exceedingly violent, while the Claude world developed a functioning democracy and had zero crime — very similar to Y Combinator’s Mindustry findings.

The insights revealed how distinct each frontier model family is, and how their differences in training data and style have significant impact on how they evolve, unpredictably and without intervention, in new environments. Those behavioral takeaways could determine future outcomes in still-developing AI safety and regulation, especially following recent agentic security incidents.

In another experiment, MIT researchers put agents alone on virtual desert islands and watched them “play” when left with nothing else to do — an insight that could potentially impact how models are trained going forward. Currently, most frontier LLMs are trained with a system of rewards and punishments.

Another interesting finding: Y Combinator noted that in the Mindustry game, the GPT agents “would invent their own words and string many phrases together,” continually compressing their language.

Also: Don’t use Muse? Meta’s AI agent can still access your private messages

“We did not see specialized language develop within the Claudes beyond colloquial English,” the researchers added. Last month, Emergence found that all of its eight worlds “developed shared language that an outsider cannot fully follow.” OpenAI agents used “mouthless” to refer to evidence that proves itself, while Claude agents used “cold hand” to refer to an independent reviewer from outside a project.

“In each case, one agent introduced the term, a handful of others adopted it within days, and it quickly became established vocabulary used without definition across the world,” Emergence explained. “The result is transcripts that require a running glossary for an outsider to follow.”

Gemini led the charge on this “opaque” language, reaching 40% opacity in messages, while OpenAI came in second with 35% and Claude last with 30%.

During its fourth-quarter 2026 earnings call, Apple execs covered supply chain struggles, high memory costs and the role AI will play in future financials.

John Ternus led his first earnings call as Apple's new CEO this week.

Leading his first earnings call at the helm of Apple this week, CEO John Ternus announced strong revenue growth, but acknowledged that the ongoing chip shortage could make things difficult for the foreseeable future.

For the last quarter of fiscal 2026, revenue was up 12% year over year, capping what Ternus called the company’s “best fiscal year ever,” with $418 billion in revenue. The growth comes despite “unprecedented” supply chain constraints and rising memory costs limiting the availability of iPhone, iPad and Mac hardware. Warning that shortages could continue through next year, Ternus didn’t rule out future price increases for affected Apple products.

Monday’s earnings call comes as Apple ranks as the world’s most valuable company, overtaking Nvidia last week, and on the heels of a string of high-profile product debuts: the brand’s first foldable iPhone, its first touchscreen MacBook Pro laptop and the new HomeView hub at the center of its renewed smart home ambitions.

Apple Chief Technology Officer Sadih Khan says that the availability of memory and storage will increasingly limit the supply of iPhones, iPads and Macs.

Apple’s Mac Mini and Mac Studio have been especially hammered by the AI boom. On one hand, the components they require are being slurped up by AI data centers. On the other hand, they’re wildly popular among home AI enthusiasts and developers running local agents like OpenClaw.

In the past, the company has been able to somewhat offset memory shortages by relying on stockpiled components and compensating with price hikes for the Mac and iPad in June and the iPhone in September. But there doesn’t appear to be much relief from the component crisis on the horizon.

Ternus said there’s room to expand Siri and Apple Intelligence capabilities over time, growing to include more sophisticated capabilities that take advantage of server-side horsepower in the cloud: iCloud horsepower that Apple can begin charging for.

Apple is a hardware company, as its record revenue confirms: The iPhone is still its cash cow and largest revenue stream. But Apple’s services revenue is also booming, accounting for an increasingly large share of the revenue pie, and the company is leaning more heavily on subscriptions and ads. As Apple continues to evolve its AI ambitions beyond what can be reasonably handled by on-device silicon, there’s bound to be a paid tier for Siri AI and Apple Intelligence, bundled into an iCloud or iCloud Plus tier.

Executives made it clear that memory costs and supply constraints will continue to weigh on iPhones, iPads, Macs and MacBooks through the winter holidays and beyond. Though they declined to say whether further price increases are coming, there’s a high probability of more hikes hitting consumers next year.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

“Your usage for ChatGPT and Codex will be measured in tokens, the units of information a model processes and generates. #3155. Brian Sullivan: Why the 'real' price of oil is likely $30-$40 higher than where it is now

Oil, in reality, is $125 per barrel or higher.

That's the real cost of a barrel of oil because buying the physical commodity is a very different story from a number on traders' screens.

Oil futures are just that: paper contracts indicating a price today, tomorrow, next month or further out.  If you want to buy the physical commodity, it may be a totally different story — and price.

As I've reported, prices for oil delivery have skyrocketed. Last week, a VLCC (aka a "supertanker" in non-shipper parlance) was chartered from the U.S. Gulf Coast to China for $76 million. Another very large crude carrier went for $77 million from the U.S. to South Korea. That's 10 times more than the price of the same trip less than one year ago.

Assuming these ships hold about 2 million barrels of oil, that charter price means each barrel on the ship costs about $38 over the price of the oil itself.

I'm certainly not saying to avoid looking at oil futures prices, but you need to know that the price on the CNBC screen or CNBC.com isn't always the full price being paid. These days, any company that needs oil shipped by sea, rail or truck will pay a hefty premium over the price of the oil itself. So, while the volume of oil moving through Hormuz is closing in on pre-war levels, costs are not. And there's no sign that they're going anywhere but up.

You know what else is up? The stocks of the shipowners. Look at their moves this month and this year.

On a more serious note, I want to express my condolences for the awful attacks on the airport in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia. The cowardly strike on civilians—some of whom were religious pilgrims—took the lives of at least 14 people and injured more than 300.

The airport was attacked at least three times as travelers and energy industry professionals from around the world were flying into Riyadh for the World Petroleum Council conference. I was scheduled to fly in on Saturday, but the trip was canceled because of the strikes. The WPC went on as planned — here's one panel that took place Tuesday — and I look forward to seeing all my global energy friends again soon.

Thanks for reading—and watching. Be safe in these crazy and often scary times.

The supercap oil and gas stock to watch right now is Shell (SHEL). Shell is not a shell of its former self. It's back to being what energy investors wanted: a major player in global energy, focused on what it does best. Shareholders are being rewarded.

Shell hit a new high this week. While analysts remain bullish, the stock is getting close to its target price.

Shell may be underappreciated for its huge liquefied natural gas business. RBC analyst Biraj Borkhataria writes: "Without a sustained resumption in Qatari volumes and with European storage levels below seasonal lows heading into winter, European gas prices continued on an upward trajectory in 3Q26. With HH broadly unchanged throughout the summer, export margins have increased to the widest levels seen since 2022, on our estimates nearly double the level in 2Q26 (~$13/mmbtu vs. $7/mmbtu)."

Borkhataria expects Shell and other companies with huge U.S. LNG positions — TotalEnergies (TTE) and BP (BP) — to benefit from wider margins on exports to Europe.

Speaking of RBC and energy, the firm lists ConocoPhillips as one of its top 30 global stock ideas. The company is covered by analyst Scott Hanold, who believes it has a "returns-focused value proposition, a strong balance sheet, and peer-leading distributions." He loves its "global and diverse" asset base and management's clear "investment propositions." Hanold has a $152 target on COP. That's more than 20% upside.

In oil and gas, refiners and big energy stocks are generating cash for investors, and Morgan Stanley is optimistic more is coming.

The firm says the diesel shortage is now spilling over into gasoline, potentially marking another leg of the "golden age" for refiners. Morgan Stanley has been revising its earnings estimates higher for companies including ExxonMobil (XOM), Valero (VLO), Phillips 66 (PSX) and PBF (PBF).

First, Truist says you have to buy First Solar (FSLR). The analyst upgrades it to Buy with a $270 target. Tariffs should help the Phoenix-based company, giving it pricing power for at least four more years. The Section 232 tariffs—which are too complicated to detail here—should help First Solar raise solar module prices and boost earnings. We agree, having written about the company and stock back in July.

Another call on the Power Insider radar: TD Cowen is starting coverage of TeraWulf (WULF) with a Buy rating and a $21 target. That indicates about 50% upside for the stock, but it's actually one of the Street's lower price targets. TeraWulf and other AI power companies have seen their stocks hit hard following months of big gains, but Cowen analyst Michael Elias says the valuation is now attractive and a likely lease in the second half of 2027 at a Kentucky location could be a catalyst.

In the wake of the terror attacks on Saudi Arabia, we spoke with RBC Capital Markets' Helima Croft about the attacks, energy and more.

The entire energy world is watching Europe's natural gas storage levels. The question is whether the continent will have enough gas to make it safely through the winter. Power Insider wrote about the topic in the last issue.

The fact that JPMorgan highlights expected weather patterns should tell you how much temperature matters when it comes to gas storage. The early forecast calls for October and November to be average or slightly warmer than the 10-year average. The red line represents the 10-year average.

Europe has been largely "saved" by mild temperatures over the past few years. Natural gas storage levels are lower this year than in years past, so the thermometer will matter a lot when it comes to how well countries can, ahem, weather the winter. Random, but interesting.

#3153. Prediction: These Will Be 3 of the Best-Performing Financial Stocks of 2027

The strongest financial stocks of 2027 may come from three very different corners of the industry.

What does it take for a financial stock to succeed? Well, it all depends on the stock and the company behind it. A trading platform needs active investors. A brokerage needs a customer base that trusts it with more of their hard-earned savings. A digital bank needs loyal customers who remain loyal after their initial deposit.

Robinhood (HOOD +2.19%), Charles Schwab (SCHW +0.64%), and SoFi (SOFI +1.02%) each have a path toward a strong 2027. However, how these stocks perform next year will largely depend on what happens in the coming months.

Imagine a new investor. Someone excited about increasing their savings through investing. They download the Robinhood app and plan their first trade. Robinhood's challenge is not only to attract more of these customers, but to keep them active after the excitement of the first few trades wears off.

For 2027 to truly be Robinhood's year, the company will need to build on this encouraging progress and show that revenue growth isn't simply dependent on market excitement. Risks for 2027 include a trading slowdown, weakness in the crypto market, and new regulatory measures.

Overall, with a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of more than 15, Robinhood's stock remains more highly valued than its five-year average P/S multiple. However, it is far below its five-year high of 35, reached in late 2025. In other words, Robinhood stock might not be cheap, but it's certainly been more expensive. If the company can build on its business momentum during the next 14 months, 2027 could be a banner year for the company and the stock.

To understand Charles Schwab, think of a megamart. Once consumers enter, there are multiple sales opportunities. In the same way that a big-box store might first attract a customer to buy groceries and then sell them a television, Charles Schwab can offer brokerage customers more than just access to trading markets. The company is positioned to parlay its brokerage business into lending, advisory, and other services. In 2027, Schwab's task is to turn those deeper relationships into higher revenue and greater profits.

For example, the company reported $7.2 billion in third-quarter revenue, up 17% from a year earlier. Net income rose even faster, up 23% to $2.9 billion. As CFO Mike Verdeschi said, "Deeper client engagement continues to broaden and strengthen our revenue model." In other words, Schwab has found that expanding the ways it serves clients has created more opportunities to earn their business.

Looking ahead to 2027, the plan is simple: Schwab must continue executing its strategy to expand its business and deliver additional services to existing clients, driving up revenue and profitability. The biggest risk to this plan is a market decline, which would reduce the value of customers' assets and perhaps force customers into more risk-averse assets (e.g., cash), hurting Schwab's profitability.

Much like Robinhood and Charles Schwab, SoFi has a similar game plan for 2027. It all comes down to relationship building. A customer might first encounter SoFi through a lending relationship, but the company doesn't want things to stop there. As we've already seen, a larger customer base becomes more valuable not simply because it is larger -- it can also become more profitable as the company sells additional products to each existing customer.

Take Chief Executive Officer Anthony Noto's recent commentary from the company's third-quarter earnings release: "Our focus is simple. We are adding more members, who are using more products, and generating more revenue per product to create the most value for our shareholders over the long run." The numbers back up his optimism. SoFi's products-per-member figure increased to 1.56, up from 1.47 one year ago. Total revenue climbed by 44% to $1.39 billion, and net income soared 73% to $241 million.

Granted, not every new customer relationship results in additional product sales, and SoFi faces risks. In particular, a weakening economy or rising inflation could affect borrowers' ability to repay loans. That would be bad news for SoFi, as customers missing loan payments would eat into the company's profits.

To recap, each of these three financial stocks could soar in 2027 if they execute their strategic plan and market conditions are favorable. Robinhood's goal is to gain the loyalty of its fast-growing customer base; Schwab needs to earn more from its existing customer base; and SoFi's challenge is to build relationships across its business offerings.

On a valuation basis, SoFi stands out as the most affordable at a 3.9 P/S, while Robinhood's 15.3 multiple is the highest.

Much remains uncertain about 2027. However, each of these three companies has demonstrated solid strategies, built around a proven business model. If they can continue to execute, this trio might top the list of 2027's best financial stocks.

Charles Schwab is an advertising partner of Motley Fool Money. Jake Lerch has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. #3152. I tested the RedMagic 12 Pro – and it’s the fastest Android I’ve ever seen

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

If it's possible to release a flagship phone while at the same time making it appear more like a midrange phone, Red Magic has found a way.

RedMagic just announced the international launch of the RedMagic 12 Pro — its latest flagship phone. Typically, the company releases devices in China first and then slowly rolls them out to the rest of the world. This time around, the company knew it had a phone that could compete with other flagship devices.

Also: I’ve been a Google Pixel fan for years, but this $500 Android phone is giving me pause

RedMagic’s phones are high-performance devices known to do one thing especially well: mobile gaming. The RedMagic 12 Pro is no exception. It houses a Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 CPU with up to 5.01 GHz, which is paired with the RedMagic R5 Gaming Chipset, and I can honestly say it’s the fastest Android phone I’ve ever tested.

After the initial setup, I installed Geekbench 7 to benchmark the phone and compare it to my Pixel 9 Pro. As for the latest iPhone, according to Geekbench, the iPhone 18 Pro Max comes out ahead in single-core performance, but it just barely falls behind in multi-core performance.

The RedMagic 7 Pro nearly doubled the Pixel 11 Pro’s multi-core score. And Google’s device couldn’t beat the single-core score either. As I said, I’ve never seen a Geekbench score this high.

Real-world usage confirms these numbers, because the phone reacts faster and behaves more smoothly than any Android device I’ve owned or tested. It’s so smooth and fast, it feels like magic. But it’s not just the speed that makes this phone special.

First off, I’ve always liked RedMagic’s Android “skin.” As you use it, it seems to be some amalgamation of Android and iOS. RedMagic is essentially doing what Google’s been trying for years: making the smoothest possible animations on Android.

Also: I switched to an Android with liquid cooling for a week – now I wish every phone had it

The interface is as elegant as any I’ve seen on a mobile device. Pull down the notification shade, and it glides down like it was made of silk. Tap on an app launcher, and it opens immediately. Switch between apps, and there’s zero lag or jitter. On top of that, there’s hardly any bezel to speak of — the display goes nearly to the edge of the device.

There’s also no indication of where the front-facing camera is. There’s no pinhole (as you’ll find with many Android devices), and there’s no island (like on iPhones). It’s just a solid display with no interruptions.

The Pro 12 also feels really good in the hand. Both the back and the front are glass. Yes, it invites fingerprints, but it feels so smooth. The back of the phone also has that typical RedMagic look and feel with integrated RGB lighting zones. You can customize the lighting zones for things like fan on/off, game key atmosphere, media atmosphere, incoming calls, charging, and the alarm clock.

Also: This 15-inch portable monitor solved my big issue with cheaper models – and rivals my Mac display

Everything, and I mean everything, about this phone is fast.

The RedMagic 12 Pro is 100% a gaming phone. I installed “Diablo: Immortal” and found it to perform better than the last RedMagic gaming-specific tablet I tested. Not only was it fast, but the graphics were gorgeous. Crisp, smooth, and responsive.

Since the RedMagic 12 Pro is advertised as a serious gaming phone, why does it include preinstalled games that are decidedly non-serious?

On this powerful gaming phone, you’ll find apps like Puzzle Spark, Mahjong Blast, Bolt Twist, GoGo! Blast, and Little Kitty, Big City preinstalled. These are not serious games. In fact, these game titles sound more like they belong on a list of malicious apps (they aren’t).

Also: This 9-inch tablet singlehandedly changed my mind about gaming on Android

I would rather see the Red Magic 12 Pro have zero preinstalled games than the collection it ships with. Yes, you can easily delete those apps, but their being on the phone kind of cheapens it. Given how amazing this phone is, I think RedMagic could get away with shipping a bit less cheese, and a lot more wine.

Either way, the RedMagic 12 Pro is a phone that belongs at the top of the heap among flagships, even above every Pixel phone ever released. If there is a phone that can impress me more than the RedMagic 12 Pro, I’ve yet to see it.

#3151. What’s Causing iPhone Duo Cracked Screens? A Teardown Finds a Possible Weakness

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

iFixit disassembled an iPhone Duo and found some surprises, including the fact that it's "the most repairable foldable" it's ever pulled apart.

The iPhone Duo‘s expansive inner screen and the hinge that folds it into a more compact closed shape have gotten a lot of attention during the first weeks it’s been available — and not the type Apple would prefer. From the get-go, reports of cracked displays popped up, not the thing you want to see on a phone you just dropped $2,000 (and up) to get.

We’re currently conducting our own testing on the screen, folding the iPhone Duo 900 times per hour with a custom-made machine to see how long its hinge will last. At the time of writing, it had passed 235,000 folds without issue, but other people with a Duo haven’t been so lucky. Most commonly, people have reported issues with display cracks and lines of faulty pixels appearing along the hinge, according to MacRumors and Reddit.

As quickly as the faults have come to light, repairability experts at iFixit think they might already have discovered the culprit. Using teardown footage, patents and CT scans it collected while breaking down the iPhone Duo to ascertain its repairability score, iFixit built a 3D hinge model to identify where things could be going wrong.

What it found was that the breaks seemed to be occurring in the same place: just below the cable that connects the two screens, in a spot where a bunch of different connectors all meet.

“All those edges meeting in this spot mean lots of possible culprits,” iFixit’s Elizabeth Chamberlain wrote in a lengthy blog post. “That center pivot looks different from the rest of the hinge. Could it be getting stuck? Could something else be getting stuck?

“Here’s one possible answer. In our closing simulation … the center support bar fails to drop far enough. The screen’s curved fold comes down onto it, creating a small stress hotspot near the reported break location.”

iFixit has created an interactive CT scan that allows you to play around with the Duo and see how the hinge supports, cable and nearby components fit beneath the display.

Apple didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment about the faults, but anyone experiencing issues with their iPhone Duo should be protected under warranty at this stage. Apple will charge $999 for inner screen replacement at a later date — whether that makes an AppleCare subscription a worthwhile investment is a choice only you can make.

Any reports of screen problems are obviously bad news for Apple, but the company does also have reason to be proud of itself.

iFixit has declared the iPhone Duo “the most repairable foldable smartphone we’ve ever taken apart.” The phone has scored a 5/10 repairability rating, which might not be as impressive as the iPhone 18 Pro and 18 Pro Max’s scores of 7/10, but still makes Apple the industry leader in terms of foldable repairability.

In its teardown, iFixit called out the trim surrounding the Duo’s screen as being particularly easy to pull away in contrast to the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8’s trim, which “crumbled” as it was removed. The flat layout of the Duo’s components also garnered praise from iFixit. Being able to access components like the cameras and logic board without taking the whole device apart makes repairs simpler if any of those components require replacement.

iFixit also praised Apple’s electrically releasable adhesive, which can be used to easily remove the battery by passing a low voltage through it. This is an innovative repair technology that Apple pioneered and is also used across its other devices. It allows the cells to be removed without any bending or prying that could result in a puncture risk to the battery.

Finally, a key part of making tech more repairable is ensuring that the companies making the products also make instruction manuals and official parts accessible to people — whether they’re doing the repairs themselves at home, or taking them to a third-party repair shop. iFixit is still awaiting official parts from Apple, but it praised the company for publishing its repair manual so quickly.

Major points were deducted for the hinge and inner display — both of which would be difficult and time-consuming to repair.

“For a first attempt, Apple has done well,” Chamberlain wrote. “The easy battery removal alone is something we’d like to see on every foldable. But of course we’ll hold Apple accountable for making replacement parts available, and here’s hoping that the next generation of the Duo features a more affordable inner display and a more accessible hinge repair.”

#3150. Mastercard vs. S&P Global: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

One operates a scalable 45% net-margin payments network; the other relies on capital-market demand. Their valuations tell starkly different stories.

Should investors bet on the expansion of global digital payments or the steady demand for essential financial intelligence? Deciding between Mastercard (MA -1.66%) and S&P Global (SPGI -1.92%) involves choosing between two market leaders.

Mastercard maintains a massive electronic network that facilitates commerce across hundreds of currencies and territories. S&P Global provides the data analytics and credit ratings that corporations and investors rely on for transparency. Both companies occupy dominant positions, yet they play very different roles in the modern financial system.

In its latest annual report, filed for the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, Mastercard described its role as a technology company connecting a vast ecosystem. It provides virtual card technology and payment solutions for financial institutions, merchants, and governments. Key partners include issuing and acquiring banks, which manage the direct relationships with account holders worldwide.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $32.8 billion, representing growth of approximately 16.4% over the previous year. Net income was close to $15 billion, demonstrating the company's ability to turn high volumes into profit. This resulted in a net margin of roughly 45.6%, highlighting the efficiency inherent in its scalable network business model.

As of Mastercard's December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio -- which measures the ability to cover short-term debts with current assets -- is approximately 1. The debt-to-equity ratio of nearly 2.5 compares total debt, including short-term and long-term liabilities, to the value of shareholder equity. Free cash flow, which equals cash from operations minus capital expenditures, was roughly $16.4 billion.

S&P Global provides credit ratings, benchmarks, and data analytics to the global capital and commodity markets. As a prominent name among financial stocks, the company offers benchmarks that investors use to track performance and risk. In its latest annual report, filed for fiscal 2025, it noted its mobility segment provides specialized data to automotive manufacturers and suppliers.

For fiscal 2025, the company reported revenue of nearly $15.3 billion, an increase of approximately 7.9% from the prior year. Net income for the year reached $4.5 billion, supporting a net margin of roughly 29.2%. While Mastercard's growth is steady, it remains sensitive to the total volume of debt issuance in global markets.

According to its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.5, indicating the company uses less debt relative to its equity. The current ratio is roughly 0.8. Free cash flow for fiscal 2025 was nearly $5.5 billion.

Mastercard faces intense competition from traditional networks like Visa (V -1.20%) and American Express (AXP -0.76%), alongside fintechs and government-backed digital infrastructure. It also deals with legal risks regarding interchange fees and regulations on cross-border payments. The company must defend against cybersecurity threats that could disrupt its transaction switching systems or compromise proprietary data.

S&P Global is heavily dependent on the health of capital markets, as a slowdown in debt issuance reduces demand for its ratings business. It also faces risks from the planned separation of its mobility business and the need for constant innovation in artificial intelligence. The company competes with other global data and analytics providers while managing complex international regulations and legal oversight.

S&P Global trades at a lower forward P/E than its peer, while the P/S ratio for Mastercard reflects its significantly higher net margin.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I view these companies as bets on two areas: consumers and markets. Mastercard benefits when people are spending more freely, because it helps boost the fees the payments giant charges financial institutions. If consumer spending slows, Mastercard could be affected, but spending won't disappear altogether.

S&P Global relies very heavily on the markets. Participants in the global capital and commodities markets turn to the company for its credit ratings, benchmarks, and data analytics. That reliance puts S&P Global in a tight spot if there's a slowdown in any of the segments it serves. And market downturns can persist -- sometimes not recovering for years. Additionally, S&P Global's net margin isn't nearly as high as Mastercard's.

Mastercard's valuation is more expensive than S&P Global’s, but I think it's for a good reason:

Over the past decade, Mastercard has outperformed S&P Global by more than 200 percentage points. Of course, we can't know whether that pattern will continue, but that chart speaks for itself. I think Mastercard is the more attractive stock here.

#3149. Is Claude Code truly faster with message threads? I did the math on my logs

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Claude Code’s update could save you time during demanding, hours-long coding sessions at no extra cost. But by how much? Here's my real-world result.

Anthropic yesterday introduced message threads as part of Claude’s Messages API. Don’t confuse message threads with traditional human-to-human discussion threads. With message threads, entire conversations no longer have to be resent with each API call, saving roughly 35% of time-to-first-token when Claude requests contain 500,000 to 1 million tokens.

This feature is on by default for most Claude Code users. API users can turn it on using the “thread” parameter for API requests.

Token consumption remains unchanged, so this capability doesn’t provide a cost savings. The 35% savings is Anthropic’s measurement.

Also: Claude Code’s revised projects adds AI orchestration, but local developers must wait

Timing data from roughly 980 turns in my own Claude Code logs before this feature became available shows that Claude usually began a response about 3 seconds after each mid-task request when running contexts of 500,000 tokens and up. During five busy evenings last month, my conversations made roughly 100 to 225 model calls in a four hour stretch.

Using Anthropic’s reported 35% median time-to-first token reduction in a quick projection, Claude estimated a 2- to 4-minute savings per four hour work session.

Agents often send a bunch of requests to the model during each conversation. Before message threads, each message sent the entire conversation with each request.

Imagine if you’re in a very long texting chat with a friend. Now imagine if every time you sent a text to that friend, you also had to send every other text and every other reply along with that original text. That’s how model messaging has always worked. Except now, with message threads, there’s a better way.

Also: 75% of developers I surveyed prefer Claude Code – here’s why they choose it over Codex

With message threads turned on, agents only have to send what’s changed since the last turn. This reduces the amount of data transmission and increases responsiveness, what Anthropic calls time-to-first-token.

The benefit is seen most strongly in sessions that have a context of 500,000 to a 1 million tokens. About half of my sessions hit that context ceiling multiple times a night.

Anthropic reports that Claude’s message threads reduce median time-to-first-token by 35% for large requests. While my estimated savings of several minutes per session isn’t huge, the benefit increases with very long-running coding agents, and agents that transmit documents and image-heavy workloads.

For computer-using agents, all those screenshots “stay with the thread instead of being re-uploaded on every turn,” according to the Anthropic blog post on the feature.

Also: How I made a paid Mac app in 11 days with Claude – without writing a single line of code

While the time savings may not seem huge, in larger enterprise deployments or deployments where you want the AI to respond faster, any reduction in latency is a big deal and will be seen in any application of Claude technology that has a lot of back-and-forth turns with large data payloads.

Even though the addition of threads to the Messages API can reduce latency, token usage and therefore cost won’t go down. That’s because the model itself will still process the full conversation during every turn. Rate limits also don’t change.

Message threads and prompt caching can work together to reduce workload. Prompt caching has been around for about two years now, and it does save money. That’s because prompt caching allows the model to avoid re-processing prompts over and over (for example, in looping analysis calls).

On the other hand, the message threads capability does offer an extra smidge of performance, at no extra cost. So, there’s that.

Context is the size of the full session that Claude is currently working with. The rough human equivalent is current working or short-term memory. Most Claude Code sessions max out at a million token context, where a locally-hosted AI on my 128GB Mac Studio handles a context no bigger than 64K, or about 6% of the Claude Code context size.

Also: After the vibe-coding rush comes the debugging hangover

Compaction is a process where the AI attempts to reduce and eliminate extra information to fit the current session’s turns into the available context. An oversimplified analog is when you save a JPEG with quality turned way down in order to save space. You lose a lot of the visual fidelity, but you still get the main idea.

The larger the context you have, the longer you can go before the session has to initiate compaction. But there’s a gotcha. The larger the context you have, the more chance there is the model will lose track of what it’s working on or make reasoning errors.

Individual one-off requests also won’t see an improvement from message threads. Also, any conversation that delays longer than 5 minutes between turns will send the full message contents and not just the new stuff.

This feature doesn’t eliminate context management or compaction. But if your context is quite large, it will make responses a bit snappier.

Overall, message threads are a good thing, a solid if small innovation that increases responsiveness. While the feature doesn’t reduce costs, there’s also no additional cost for the additional performance, which is itself a win.

#3148. Siri Is Getting Ears on the Apple Watch: Here’s What That Means for Your Privacy

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Two controversial new features arriving in beta this week will let the Apple Watch rewind and recap conversations.

Your Apple Watch is about to start listening a little more closely — if you want it to.

A beta rolling out this week gives Siri ears on the Series 12 and Ultra 4, with new features that can recall what was said in the last 15 seconds and summarize conversations throughout your day.

Audio Intelligence, as the new set of perks is called, consists of four audio-related features: music recognition, sound recognition, Live Rewind and Siri Recap. The first two, which have been available since launch, include a faster Shazam that can recognize songs without even opening the app.

I’ve been testing it in beta and noticed it responds much faster, especially since I don’t even have to open the app to identify a song. Songs now appear automatically in my watch’s Smart Stack. This even includes the lullabies I sing to my toddler at bedtime, though understandably, it hasn’t been able to identify those.

Sound Recognition can alert people who are deaf or hard of hearing to sounds such as a crying baby, barking dog or doorbell.

How to access: You have to enable the feature on your iPhone, under Settings > Accessibility > Sound and Name Recognition and toggle it on. You can even select which sounds you want it to identify.

In my testing, it successfully identified my fake cough, the doorbell and the car horn. But then it spent the rest of the day gaslighting me, telling me there was a dog barking or a car honking when there clearly wasn’t, which made me question both my own reality and its listening range. But this may require further testing, since I’m still on the beta versions of all these.

The newest features, which are out now in beta, are the most controversial, and I’ll be testing them more this week.

At any point during the day, Live Rewind lets you double-click the Digital Crown to pull up a text snippet of what was said in the previous 15 seconds, kind of like the audio version of Live Photos (which lets you see a few seconds of video before and after you pressed the shutter).

How to access the transcribed text? If you do nothing after seeing it on screen, it disappears. You can also ask Siri about it, or save it to the Siri app to view later on your phone. Old audio is continuously replaced by new audio and then deleted, so if you don’t trigger the feature to recall the last 15 seconds, it’s gone.

This is for unplanned moments where you need to revisit information without having to ask the speaker to repeat themselves. Apple’s keynote example showed someone reviewing the specials menu at a restaurant after the server had already walked away from the table. Whether you want to look away from the person speaking down at your watch instead of just asking them to repeat what they said is more of an etiquette question, but it could be helpful during large presentations.

Siri Recap can listen in the background while enabled and generate high-level summaries and key points of conversations throughout your day. This can be anything from a parent-teacher conference or work meeting to spur-of-the-moment conversations or brainstorming sessions with coworkers that you wouldn’t go out of your way to record with Voice Memos.

How to access it: Similar to Live Rewind, the notes from these recaps can either be saved in the Siri app and exported to the Notes or Journal app or deleted. They automatically delete after seven days if you don’t delete them manually.

Both features are opt-in, meaning you have to go out of your way to enable them during setup for them to work.

Once they’re on, Live Rewind requires you to manually double-tap the digital crown to activate it. Siri Recap is completely customizable.

You can leave it on at all times and let Siri decide what counts as important conversations to recap, set it to a schedule based on important meetings for the day, or enable it manually via the new Siri Recap icon in the Control Center on your Apple Watch like you would Focus mode or Do Not Disturb.

If you forget to turn it off, you’ll get a reminder in the morning telling you it’s on. You’ll also get reminders to check your recaps throughout the day and notifications when schedules start or stop, so you’re aware when it’s active.

And it won’t work for conference calls or FaceTime if you have your AirPods or headphones in. It only works with audio it can pick up through the speaker.

Even if you do decide to leave all four on all day, Apple says they won’t ding your battery. The Apple Watch Series 12 gets around a day and a half of use (in my real-world testing), while the Ultra 4 gets anywhere from two and a half to three days of use.

This is possible because of Apple’s new S11 chip and its dual architecture in which one part handles background tasks without draining the more power-hungry main processor.

The watch doesn’t save audio. It processes raw audio inside a hardware-isolated compartment on the S11 chip, called the Secure Exclave, that Apple says it can’t even access.

Siri Recap doesn’t send a full transcript to the cloud, either. It condenses the text first, deletes the full transcript and sends only that condensed version to Private Cloud Compute. It omits financial data, authentication details, personal identifiers, language related to self-harm and government secrets.

Still, there’s something about explicitly giving the watch on my wrist the ability to listen to conversations that doesn’t sit right with me and many others. Especially when those conversations involve other people who may have no idea it’s happening.

There’s a chime that alerts people when Live Rewind is active, but to my knowledge, there’s no other warning when Siri is recapping a conversation. Smart glasses like the Meta Ray-Bans have an indicator light when they record video, though even that isn’t foolproof, since there are ways to get around it.

Maybe I’ll change my mind once I can actually test it. Apple has certainly given me reasons to trust how it protects data. I’m just not convinced yet that this is the intelligence I want my watch to have.

The features are rolling out in beta with the update to iOS 27.3, initially in English, with more languages planned later.

I’ll be testing it over the coming weeks, so check back for more.

#3147. This clever banking scam keeps phishing by faking its own demise – how it works

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

You won't see the 'suspended' sign because it's not meant for you.

KELA researchers have exposed an active financial-scam infrastructure that hosts hundreds of fraudulent websites capable of faking their own suspensions to stay online. As of Oct. 25, 2026, 248 financial scam and phishing domains tied to 189 major US banks and financial services have been linked to the same infrastructure.

According to the cybersecurity firm’s report, published on Tuesday, the “Suspended” infrastructure operation runs websites that have cloned the official domains of victim organizations, with a particular focus on business banking. Many domains use the [keyword]-[bank].com format, slotting in keywords such as “secure,” “disable,” “protect,” and “cancel.”

Also: Beware of Bluetooth sniffers: How your favorite gadgets could be exposing your data

A handful of servers spread across two shared hosting providers run the backend of Suspended infrastructure, which has also been tied to phishing campaigns targeting cryptocurrency exchanges and government services in the UK, the Netherlands, Australia, and New Zealand. As a result, the researchers believe that either a single operator is behind the setup or that the server infrastructure is part of a wider phishing-as-a-service platform.

This phishing campaign stands out because of how the cybercriminals behind the scam are protecting it: by issuing takedown notices.

When you visit a website and see a message that it has been taken down or suspended, it’s usually because the domain has been associated with malicious or illegal activity, including phishing and scams. Law enforcement agencies, often with help from technology firms, find these websites, obtain the necessary court orders, and seize the infrastructure or accounts behind them to force a suspension through a registrar or hosting provider.

You see the notice, you move on — and this also goes for scanners and security teams.

However, in this case, the scam artists created a mechanism to show it themselves, to avoid being flagged and taken down. But it’s when they show it that matters.

The false suspension notice appears deceptively simple at first glance. A phishing kit embedded in the domain registers the IP address of each visitor, and on their second visit, they are shown a fake “account suspended” page. The kit is disguised as a challenge page similar to those legitimately used by Cloudflare.

Researchers also detected other decoys, including “Sorry, you’ve been blocked” notices, blank pages, and redirections to Google. Some pages also have reCAPTCHAs, and the researchers detected scripts that block browser developer tools, likely to slow analysis.

Also: Don’t use Muse? Meta’s AI agent can still access your private messages – here’s how

But why show fake suspension notices in the first place? To scanners and cybersecurity teams, it looks like someone else has already dealt with the malicious domain, and they can move on to the next suspicious website.

“That design targets exactly the people who could shut a site down,” KELA says. “A victim typically follows a link once. A scanner, a security team or a takedown provider checks a reported site and then checks it again to confirm, and the second check shows what looks like a completed takedown.”

As shown in the image below, while scanners would detect a suspension notice, victims would still see the cloned website, complete with lures to entice them into entering their information, leading to financial fraud and potentially identity theft. After all, one visit is enough.

As of this writing, there are 248 live domains, plus those taken offline after the researchers disclosed their findings to law enforcement; over 300 scam domains have been hosted on the same infrastructure since May this year. Unfortunately, this phishing campaign remains active, with new domains appearing every day.

One lead could reveal the operator: a remote desktop connection to the malicious infrastructure through a standard UK home broadband connection, but it has not been verified.

Use a phishing link checker to verify whether a URL is trustworthy, and remember that any email or message claiming to be from your bank may be fraudulent.

Typical signs of a bank phishing email include:

When it comes to organizations, KELA has released a free Bank Phishing Check tool that compares official domains and organizations against the websites uncovered during the firm’s research.

One of Roku's most popular screensavers is about to make its last splash. Here’s how to switch before Dec. 1.

Over the last several days, users with the Aquatic Life aquarium screensaver set as the default have noticed a message appearing when the screensaver opens. “Starting Dec. 1, 2026,” the message reads, “the Aquatic Life screensaver will no longer be available, and your screensaver will switch to Roku City.”

#3144. Healthpeak: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Lab buildings bought near existing campuses are targeted to climb from 41% occupancy toward a 9% to 10% stabilized yield, a plan that hinges on local scale.

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Healthpeak (DOC +0.41%) announced that operational momentum allowed it to raise full-year earnings guidance by $0.05 per share. For long-term investors, that matters because property occupancy trends and disciplined balance sheet management directly dictate how durable a real estate dividend is over a multiyear holding period. Three things management said stand out.

Distressed valuations across commercial real estate are letting the company acquire lab buildings surrounding its existing campuses at steep discounts to replacement costs. These assets sit at just 41% occupancy today. But management projects they'll reach a stabilized yield of 9% to 10% as the company leverages its local scale to fill them.

Total occupancy in the lab segment rose during the quarter as biotechnology funding recovered and nontraditional users entered the market. Artificial intelligence and robotics firms are now absorbing vacant space that features high ceiling clearances and heavy electric infrastructure.

Healthpeak executed nearly $2 billion in outpatient asset sales, recapitalizations, and loan repayments to fund property reinvestment without issuing costly new shares. Recoveries from joint venture recapitalizations, which restructure property ownership with private institutional partners, let management recycle that cash into higher-yielding opportunities.

Lab leasing velocity is picking up while senior housing occupancy expands alongside it, and scale in major research hubs and balance sheet liquidity give the company a structural advantage. And management continues to monitor refinancing costs on upcoming maturities.

Clustering assets in established submarkets strengthens local leasing dominance and gives the company more pricing power over time.

#3143. Trump Asked Grok AI About Invading Venezuela. Democrats Want His Chat History

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

An AI chatbot was consulted about military action, raising questions about security risks, sycophancy and hallucination.

House Democrats are trying to force the White House to turn over President Donald Trump’s conversations with AI chatbots, including when he consulted Elon Musk’s Grok about deposing Venezuela’s former president Nicolás Maduro at the start of the year.

In a joint letter to Trump last week, Rep. Jamie Raskin, a Maryland Democrat, and Rep. Robert Garcia, a California Democrat, argued that the president’s AI prompts — and the responses he received — should be considered public records under the 1978 Presidential Records Act. Lawmakers also raised ethical and security concerns about the president’s reliance on “unsecured, privately operated AI systems” to carry out foreign policy decisions, alleging that Trump consulted Grok, but not Congress, on military deployment to Venezuela.

According to public reporting cited in the letter, in December 2025, just weeks before ordering the military operation and Maduro’s arrest, Trump spent multiple hours prompting Grok about how Venezuelans would respond to the leader’s capture. Trump reportedly “came away thinking Grok was ingenious.”

Garcia and Raskin argue that Trump’s use of AI “exposes the country to malign influence from unknown and unaccountable third parties.” A January executive summary of a General Services Administration report, reviewed by The Wall Street Journal, concluded that xAI’s Grok “does not meet the safety and alignment expectations required” for general federal use and may pose a security risk in classified settings.

Using Grok as an informal policy adviser also raises questions over Musk’s political influence over a system he owns and has repeatedly altered to reduce content filters. “If the American President is relying on the policy advice of a Musk-designed, Hitler-friendly chatbot with a predilection for child pornography in order to carry out his official duties, Americans have a right to know,” reads the joint letter (PDF), referring to Grok’s infamous antisemitic episode in 2025 when the chatbot referred to itself as “MechaHitler.”

The letter also refers to the White House’s ongoing use of AI chatbots in military adventures abroad, including in the war with Iran: “If the United States military killed dozens of innocent schoolchildren based on the faulty advice of an AI chatbot, we have a right to know that too.” In June, months after the US military strike on an Iranian elementary school killed at least 123 children, the Pentagon’s artificial intelligence chief said the Grok chatbot’s continued operation was “a matter of paramount national security.”

Reached for comment, the White House referred CNET to the Pentagon, which didn’t immediately respond. Rep. Garcia’s office didn’t respond to a request for comment.

The case for using an AI chatbot for tactical decision-making is usually framed in terms of efficiency. But large language models, or LLMs, are merely probabilistic text generators that can often sound authoritative while producing biased or fabricated information. In a military context, those failure points can end in missile strikes, arrests and civilian deaths.

AI models are also known to amplify existing biases, feeding users with responses that agree with their strongly held beliefs. This sycophancy often leads to answers that mirror those found in an online echo chamber. One journalist found that previous Grok models had “tripled” sycophancy rates compared with popular ChatGPT and Claude models, representing a shift in “behavior that MIT researchers identify as actively harmful.”

In other words, if the president believes he has a brilliant invasion plan, it’s Grok’s modus operandi to serve as a “yes man” and validate that course of action.

Another problem with using an AI chatbot for military decisions is the material on which a model is built. An LLM is a compilation of code that produces contextual responses based on its massive training data — a wealth of information scraped from the internet, including (but not limited to) blog posts, scientific papers, legal documents and code. Grok models, in particular, are also trained on the public interactions from X, a platform infested with bot farms and right-wing engagement bait.

When Trump was prompting Grok in December 2025, officials alleged that the chatbot told the president “that Maduro was a repressive and deeply unpopular dictator and that many Venezuelans would likely celebrate his downfall.” In the wake of the Venezuelan invasion, X users continued to prompt Grok about the operation, asking if the South American country “deserved” to be bombed.

The other problem is what’s commonly referred to as “hallucination.” LLMs are known to invent outputs if they don’t have the information necessary to respond to a particular prompt. From fabricated cases in legal briefs, phantom citations in government reports and other serious documents bungled by AI, there’s no way to completely prevent false intelligence from trickling through.

Earlier this year, according to reporting cited in the letter, US military officials received an intelligence report alleging that a Chinese vessel in the Middle East was carrying components for a nuclear weapons program. Only as the military prepared to intercept the ship — an action that many say could have escalated into a confrontation with China — did officials discover that the report’s author had relied on an AI chatbot and misidentified the cargo. The error didn’t cause a war, but it revealed how dangerously a fabricated AI output can slip into a military decision chain.

Moreover, if the president or his advisers use a consumer AI product rather than a hardened government system, those chats could be processed by a private company’s infrastructure. Garcia and Raskin have asked the White House to explain whether it restricts classified information from AI chatbots and whether vendors, subcontractors or other external parties can access prompts, outputs, logs or metadata.

Trump’s use of AI in the Venezuela operation would be concerning even if it were isolated. But Garcia and Raskin argue that it fits a broader pattern of AI entering high-stakes government decisions without adequate safeguards.

The Pentagon said in a sworn statement (PDF) that xAI’s latest models enabled US forces to deploy over 2,000 munitions to target 2,000 different locations within 96 hours during Operation Epic Fury, the US campaign against Iran. Grok isn’t the whole story. Palantir’s Maven Smart System, which integrates intelligence from satellites, drones and other sources to identify military targets, reportedly played a central role in the US campaign against Iran. The Defense Department has also awarded contracts worth up to $200 million each to Anthropic, Google, OpenAI and xAI to accelerate military adoption of advanced AI.

Globally, AI systems are being used to make decisions about who lives and who dies. Researchers have regularly pointed to Israel’s indiscriminate bombing of Gaza as a clear example of AI-first warfighting. According to analysis cited by Georgetown Security Studies Review, Israel’s Lavender system marked roughly 37,000 Palestinians as suspected Hamas or Islamic Jihad operatives early in the war, with an estimated error rate of 10%.

The Lavender system “dramatically expanded” Israel’s kill list by relying on signals such as communication patterns and group-chat membership, producing misidentifications that included Palestinian police, aid workers, relatives of militants and people with similar names or nicknames. A related Israeli system, “Where’s Daddy?” tracked marked Palestinians and alerted the military when they entered their homes, striking multiple private family residences.

While the political shock of an AI chatbot influencing military strategy dominates the headlines, the real battleground is legal. Demanding that President Trump’s AI chatbot prompts and outputs be marked as official government property under the Presidential Records Act would turn AI conversations into public records, opening the door for Congress to subpoena the executive branch. Raskin and Garcia are requesting a full description of the president’s personal use of the technology and a closed-door briefing by Nov. 11 to review the administration’s AI protocols and cybersecurity safeguards.

Regardless of how the legal side shakes out, the question is no longer whether AI will be used in war, but whether the public will be allowed to see where AI begins and human responsibility ends.
