#3142. Windows 11 can run better on PCs with 8GB now, Microsoft says – here’s why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Windows > Windows 11

The latest updates to Windows 11 offer improvements in speed, performance, and memory management.

The more memory your Windows PC has, the faster it should perform, especially when juggling multiple apps, files, and other data. My laptop has 32GB of RAM, while my desktop has a hefty 64GB, and both are absolutely zippy. Does that mean computers with only 8GB of memory, or even less, are also-rans? Nope, not according to Microsoft.

Also: How much RAM does your PC really need?

In a new blog post published Tuesday, Microsoft touted the gains in speed, performance, and memory management in Windows 11 achieved by the latest updates. These especially impact PCs with just 8GB of RAM, which can now run multiple apps and files faster and more efficiently than before.

To reach this goal, the company said it had three notions in mind.

The first was to make better use of the existing memory on each Windows 11 PC. The second was to protect the tasks that need to feel instant even as more memory gets used. And the third was to ensure that key apps and Windows require less memory. Yes, a computer with 16GB, 32GB, or 64GB of RAM can better handle more intensive activities. But Microsoft wanted to make sure that systems with 8GB could also keep pace.

Based on its own lab tests of PCs with 8GB of RAM, the company claims that Windows is now 20% more responsive and that multitasking is 40% faster than before the latest updates. Here’s how.

As more memory gets allocated, Windows must move inactive apps and files from RAM to the hard drive. When you need to access those apps and files again, you have to wait for Windows to move them from disk storage back to RAM. Normally, PCs with less memory have to do a lot of this disk swapping.

One way Windows eases the pain of this process is through compression. Here, the operating system shrinks less-active data into a smaller format so it can remain in memory. Though Windows has used compression for many years, Microsoft has fine-tuned the technology to determine how much to compress, when to move data from RAM to your drive, and when to fetch it back, especially on PCs with 8GB of memory.

Also: 5 apps I always install on new Windows PCs – most are free

As such, Windows can now keep up to 2GB more of your active data in memory rather than moving it to storage, Microsoft said. An inactive app, file, or browser that you leave open is less likely to have to reload from your hard drive when you return to it.

Opening the Start menu, launching File Explorer, right-clicking a folder or file, and searching for an item are all tasks that need to feel instant; otherwise, Windows itself feels sluggish. To achieve this on PCs with less RAM, Windows tries to prioritize certain actions you need to accomplish while backing off background tasks like search indexing, Microsoft said.

Here’s one example. After you update your computer and reboot, Windows has some housekeeping to do. But any setup work that occurs shouldn’t affect how long it takes to open an app or file.

Also: How to upgrade your ‘incompatible’ Windows 10 PC to Windows 11 – for free

The latest version of Windows 11 speeds up these types of tasks and interactions by up to 20% compared with the January 2026 version, according to Microsoft.

However, multitasking places the greatest strain on a PC with only 8GB of RAM. To analyze this, Microsoft said it conducted two tests on the same PC with 8GB of memory. One test measured the current version of Windows 11, while the other gauged the January 2026 flavor. Here, the testers opened 20 tabs in Microsoft Edge, kicked off a 4K video, and launched large files in Word, PowerPoint, and Excel, one after another. The current version of Windows is up to 40% faster than its predecessor, the company said.

To reduce the need for RAM among Windows and certain key apps, Microsoft said that it has improved how memory is managed and when it’s used.

As one example, memory is allocated to Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook more efficiently, resulting in a 360 MB savings in RAM. This maneuver also extends to third-party apps such as WhatsApp, which has reduced its memory requirements.

Also: 11 Windows apps I replace on every new PC (and the ones I keep)

On PCs with 8GB of memory, the Widgets app loads only when you open it and frees up about 200 MB of RAM when you close it. In Microsoft Edge, inactive tabs now go to sleep after 15 minutes, down from two hours previously, reducing overall memory allocation.

Across Windows, Microsoft 365, and Edge, those improvements reduced the growth in required memory by up to 900 MB, Microsoft said.

How does Microsoft test these scenarios to see whether the latest Windows updates and fixes are helping PCs with less memory?

“We test those fixes across a broader collection of over 20,000 PCs in our labs, including current 8 GB models, using automated workloads based on activities such as browsing, working in Microsoft 365 apps, joining video calls, and downloading files in the background and even using high-speed cameras to measure the time from an action, such as opening Start or launching an app, to the moment the result appears on screen,” the company explained.

Also: Why your RAM options cost 4X more now than last year

The ability to use Windows without much RAM on your PC is critical these days in light of the “RAMageddon” crisis. With AI companies scooping up massive amounts of memory and flash storage chips, remaining supply is limited, and the prices have surged. The more memory you add to a new or existing PC, the more you’re going to pay. That’s always been true, but now more than ever.

Of course, memory is only one part of the picture. Your PC’s processor and other hardware also play an important role. Here, Microsoft found that a 2026 Dell XPS 13 with 8GB of RAM showed 94% faster multi-core CPU performance than a 2021 8 GB model. But the same 2026 8GB PC performed on par with a 2021 16 GB system. This shows that memory does matter for multitasking, but that the processor also impacts performance.

Whether your Windows 11 PC has 8GB of RAM, less, or more, how can you take advantage of the latest enhancements? For that, you’ll want to make sure that you’ve updated to the newest version of Windows. That would be Windows 11 26H2, build 26300.9818 or later, according to Microsoft. But that includes the latest optional preview build released in late October. If you don’t want to install that one, you’ll be fine with build 26300.9711 for now.

Also: The best laptops: Expert tested and reviewed

To check, head to Settings, select System, and then click About. The section for Windows Info shows the current version and build. If you need to update, stay in Settings and go to Windows Update. Assuming your PC qualifies, you should find the latest Windows 11 26H2 build ready to install.

Beyond updating Windows, you also want to keep your apps current. If you use Microsoft 365, open any app in the suite, click the File menu, select Account, click Update Options, then select Update Now.

With most other apps, you have to update them manually or individually. To ease the process, consider a good software updater program. These will automatically scan most of your installed applications to identify which need updating, then download and install the updates for you.

#3140. I tried Sonos’ latest app features – these 2 small upgrades fix big annoyances

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Home Entertainment > Speakers

New Sonos software and firmware updates are rolling out. Here's what you can expect.

Sonos’ promise to release its AI-upgraded voice assistant is nearing its “Fall 2026” deadline, and before fans can use the highly anticipated update, the company continues to make small improvements to its app and player features.

Also: Sonos Ace Ultra vs. Sonos Ace: Who should upgrade

This time, Sonos added System Health, a new in-app page to access a more comprehensive view of your system(s), though it’s only available on iOS for now. However, a new blog post states that iOS and Android users can expect volume presets, more interaction with the app miniplayer, and spoken voice prompts for the Ace Ultra headphones.

I poked around in the app and tried some of the latest features. Here’s what to expect.

If your system consists of multiple speakers, a mix of modern and legacy speakers, or multiple systems, you’re bound to experience a hiccup and be unsure which speaker is causing the issue.

It usually takes me some time to Google my issue and scroll through the Sonos Community forum, Reddit, or the official Sonos help documents to find suggestions and troubleshoot it. Sonos’ new System Health feature provides an overview of your systems, showing your network status and name, as well as the number of speakers and rooms in each system.

Also: I used Sonos’s new home theater feature – it’s a competition killer

According to Sonos’ blog post, System Health can detect a Double Network Address issue, which usually occurs when you introduce a new router to your home or plug a mesh system into your ISP-provided router. The result is two home networks, and your Sonos speakers will split their connection between them, flagging “System not found” errors, missing speakers, or connection dropouts.

System Health is in the Settings tab, under the Help Center menu.

Once you tap into your system, you can see all of its speakers, with unconnected speakers bearing a red “Not connected” badge, and wireless speakers showing a black “Powered off” badge when they’re off.

Then, you can see a speaker’s connection information, including status, Wi-Fi signal strength, signal/noise ratio, frequency, IP address, and more. You can also find its volume output, Trueplay setup status, line-in connection, voice control status, serial number, hardware model and version, and which Sonos platform it operates on.

System Health can also show you your account details, Sonos ID, Controller ID, and your current app build version. System Health comes after Sonos updated the System Updates tab, which now displays an overview of your app, system, and headphone update status. This page also links to the Sonos release notes.

Sonos’ blog post states that this feature is new and still open to change, especially if the Sonos community has a particularly overwhelming response to it. The blog post also disclaims that this feature is a first look, not a deep dive.

However, after a brief first use, I see it as a great way to quickly access all the details you need about your systems and speakers, especially if you’re doing some rewiring, moving a speaker to a new room, or general troubleshooting.

System Health is rolling out to iOS users first. I updated my Sonos app in the App Store and was able to access it, but Sonos’ blog post states that this update is a phased rollout.

Updates to the Sonos app and its player are a long time coming. After receiving several new ways to control your speakers’ volume (I think we’re up to three), Sonos is realizing that granular volume control is a high priority for many Sonos users, including me.

The in-app volume dial is cool, and the interactive volume scrubber is a nice touch, but there’s nothing more jarring than wanting to play some tunes on my kitchen counter speaker, only for it to blast at 80% from when my husband was watching football the night before.

Sonos’ latest big update to volume this time around is Presets, a way to start playback in a specific room at a set volume. I haven’t found this feature in my app’s update yet, but I think it’s because my hardware needs an update. Presets will be available on iOS and Android.

Fortunately, I did use another update to the Sonos miniplayer: swipe to skip. What a teeny, tiny but useful feature. It works just like the miniplayer in Apple Music and Spotify; when the app is open, you can swipe left to skip to the next song.

One major caveat: swiping right to replay a track doesn’t work — at least not yet. Swiping both ways seems like a given to me, so it feels odd that you can’t. Still, being able to swipe the miniplayer to the next track is better than always having the main player open to toggle tracks. This feature is also coming to iOS and Android.

Sonos’ miniplayer is getting so much better; you can swipe to the next track and easily control volume from it, even when you have the Ace or Ace Ultra connected (I tried). The in-app volume dial also works with Sonos headphones.

Also: Sonos Play vs. Sonos Move 2: Why I’d go with the $200 cheaper Bluetooth speaker

Speaking of the Ace Ultra, a firmware update is available. A new Vocal Guidance feature can announce the battery percentage when you power on the headphones, when the audio mode changes from ANC to Transparency, and which device your headphones are connected to via Bluetooth.

It feels like this update is the final stretch before Sonos 27voice arrives, as the release notes suggest. It appears Sonos is laying the groundwork for speakers to support the AI-powered voice assistant in Early Access. Once it’s here, I’ll test it as soon as I can.

#3138. Apple Is Putting an Age Limit on Falling in Love With AI

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Dating and AI companion apps have new age gate restrictions, while apps that share your location will have to disclose it.

Practicing flirting using Replika's AI system.

Falling in love with artificial intelligence raises a lot of questions about loneliness, human connection and what the heck we’re doing as a species. Apple has a more immediate concern: Are you at least 18?

The company is tightening its App Store rules, requiring dating apps and AI companions that offer romantic or sexual interactions to carry an 18-and-over age rating. The change applies whether you’re looking for love with another human being or flirting with a chatbot that never leaves you on read.

In a notice to developers on Monday, Apple said it will introduce two new content descriptors on Nov. 16: “Dating and Relationships” and “Shared Location.” Developers will have to disclose whether their apps offer dating features, including romantic or sexual interactions with virtual companions, or whether they allow users to share their real-world locations with others.

Location-sharing features may also affect age ratings, depending on the country. In Brazil, for example, apps that allow users to share their locations must carry a minimum age rating of 16, while dating apps require an 18-and-over rating. Developers have until Jan. 12, 2027, to complete the new disclosures for all their apps.

Most major dating apps, including Tinder and Hinge, already require users to be at least old enough to vote (in the US, that is). The more interesting part is how Apple’s rules could affect AI companions, which have become increasingly popular.

Last year, Character.AI, a platform where users can create and interact with fictional AI personalities, removed open-ended chats for users under 18. The company continued offering other features to younger users, but the decision came amid growing concerns about the emotional and psychological risks of forming relationships with AI.

A 2026 Common Sense Media report, which surveyed more than 1,000 children ages 9 to 17, found that many were already treating AI as a confidant. An earlier 2025 study found that nearly three-quarters of teens had used AI companions, with a third choosing chatbots over humans for serious conversations. The nonprofit has warned that such relationships can pose risks to minors, particularly around emotional attachment and dependency.

Apple’s announcement also follows the company’s expanded parental controls in iOS 27, which gave parents more control over their children’s apps, browsing and screen time. The update introduced features that let parents approve websites before their children visit them, set time limits for app categories and restrict access to potentially harmful content.

However, an “18-plus” rating doesn’t automatically mean everyone has to verify their age. The labels work with parental restrictions, while Apple may require users to confirm they’re adults before downloading those adult apps in certain regions.

So the new rules won’t necessarily stop teenagers from finding a digital soulmate. They should, however, make it harder for apps offering romantic AI relationships to slip past age-based content restrictions — regardless of whether a chatbot says you’re mature for your age.

#3134. AI Data Centers Have a PR Problem. OpenAI Is Proposing a Rebrand

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

As part of the company's post-midterms marketing campaign, data centers could be called “AI compute campuses.”

OpenAI is shifting its marketing strategy after the US midterm elections conclude this week, proposing to rebrand its data centers as “AI compute campuses.” The creator of ChatGPT is preparing a public relations campaign that could include regulatory lobbying and efforts to rehabilitate AI’s image, according to Axios.

The push coincides with a potential blue wave in Congress. OpenAI Global Affairs Officer Chris Lehane is reportedly trying to steer the AI policy debate and to send a message that “AI should be built by and for people,” as Axios reports.

The effort is a response to an increasingly enraged electorate, as backlash to AI data center construction becomes a unifying issue across party lines. A July Gallup poll found that as many as 70% of US adults are opposed to local data center construction, including Democrats, Republicans and independents.

Supporters of AI data centers often argue that the construction and operation of these massive AI server farms bring job growth to local communities. But studies show that employment is limited, and in some cases temporary. Residents continue to rally against the environmental impacts of water contamination, the burning of fossil fuels and noise pollution.

Lehane told Axios that OpenAI views the conversation as healthy and that “communities should hold companies responsible and accountable so they really and truly get a transformative benefit from these projects.” The company is trying to reframe AI adoption as a means to access economic opportunities.

OpenAI’s new PR campaign aligns with Silicon Valley’s effort to repackage the controversial technology so it can move full speed ahead with building more data centers, which the White House has touted as vital engines for the US economy. President Donald Trump’s recent move to rename AI as “super intelligence” was another bid to dress up the capabilities of frontier labs, which have been under increased financial scrutiny by investors.

A representative from OpenAI didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

OpenAI’s post-midterms marketing package includes implementing speed limits and mandatory safeguards for AI model development, largely echoing Anthropic’s “pacing” talking points. The company is also trying to shape the international standards that dictate the technology’s development.

Up to now, a lack of regulatory oversight has left top AI labs largely unchecked, leading to incidents where frontier models slipped past flimsy safety guardrails and caused real-world disruptions across the web.

OpenAI’s lobbying strategy for an alleged “human-centered” path based on “trust” fits squarely in the company’s data center rebrand — all calculated to help deflect public criticism. If the company publicly advocates slowing its own AI development, it can be more effective in what critics call “regulatory capture,” a tactic that gives large AI companies significant advantages.

In other words, if OpenAI helps write the rules meant to govern the industry, it can shape regulations to protect its market dominance. If frontier labs are considered the experts in constraining the industry, they can create a legal moat around themselves, making it more difficult for smaller competitors to enter the AI industry, while protecting their bottom lines from any real damage.

The Trump administration has been a major boon to the “Magnificent Seven,” the group of major tech companies that heavily influence the S&P 500 and global financial markets. After cozying up to the White House during the past two years, the most influential figures in Silicon Valley have been rewarded with a lax regulatory environment and expensive federal contracts.

Many of the biggest tech and AI companies donated millions of dollars to the president’s inaugural fund and, more recently, to his $330 million White House ballroom project. (Apple and Nvidia were among the top contributors, at $25 million each.)

As it stands, Trump is working directly with AI companies to define security threats and respond on their own terms. If the political tide turns in DC, however, it would make sense for Big Tech to make contingency plans for operating in a potentially less favorable environment. Democratic politicians have generally eyed the AI gold rush with greater outward skepticism and scrutiny, with some members of Congress even backing a resolution opposing AI company bailouts in the event of a financial bubble popping.

Lehane told Axios he believes AI will be the primary issue voters care about in the next presidential election cycle, and that the midterms are a “bit of a movie trailer for what you’re going to see in 2028.” If nothing else, OpenAI is angling to make new political alliances before that time comes.

#3133. SUSE lays off 1 in 10 workers – citing AI and automation

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Squarespace also announced layoffs this week, but explicit ties to AI remain inconsistent.

According to exclusive reporting by The Register, enterprise Linux provider SUSE will lay off one in 10 of its nearly 2,000-person workforce due to “automation and AI,” CEO Dirk-Peter van Leeuwen told staff via email.

Also: How many jobs is your state losing to AI?

Van Leeuwen noted that AI is rapidly changing how software companies function.

“AI capabilities are advancing exponentially, forcing us to radically reimagine our work,” he said, adding that “the organizational design and cost base that SUSE is built on today will not carry us forward successfully.”

He did not specify which roles would be terminated, but The Register reports that staff will be notified by Nov. 4.

AI is impacting many parts of the tech industry, including open source. Just last month, Linus Torvalds announced he had used Claude Code to write a Linux patch, which Linux enthusiasts read as an evolution of his stance on AI.

By some reports, businesses are finally seeing some ROI from AI after a few years of uncertainty. But that remains localized, ironically, to tech itself. As one economist recently noted, AI has primarily increased revenue per employee at major tech companies, while leaving most other companies unchanged.

The trade-off for that growth is, at least in some cases, layoffs. But layoffs credited to AI in some way don’t mean that human roles are always being replaced. Last month, Microsoft announced it would buy out 60,000 of its workers — some of which are in its Copilot division — to prioritize AI efforts, though it’s also likely the company is reacting to spending a significant amount of money on AI infrastructure.

Jobloss.ai’s layoff tracker shows primarily tech companies — including Uber, Salesforce, and GitLab — in its recent entries, but job loss attribution to AI is still mixed, and the number of roles cut isn’t always jarringly huge.

It’s also hard to tell whether a layoff strategically frames pre-AI plans as being due to AI capabilities or is actually a result of them. That question circulated around Block’s major layoffs earlier this year. Squarespace also announced layoffs of 19% of its staff yesterday, though company communications don’t explicitly name AI. Still, vibecoding has impacted website builders overall, though Squarespace itself has invested in AI offerings.

Also: Why replacing staff with AI backfires

Van Leeuwen’s email to staff acknowledged where SUSE has already begun to change, though it’s unclear whether he meant by adopting AI tools internally.

“Across SUSE, modern tooling is already changing our daily workflows, enabling teams to move faster with less operational overhead and flatter reporting lines. The requirement for some existing roles has greatly diminished. Other roles will evolve over the next few years,” he said.

While several tech companies have announced highly visible layoffs this year that they’ve also framed as related to AI, economists agree that the massive cross-industry layoff wave many fear has yet to arrive. Gartner even predicts that companies will actually rehire 30% of the employees they tried to replace with AI — and at a higher cost — by 2029 if they don’t “overhaul workforce planning.”

Still, AI investments, vibecoding, and shifting priorities are changing the software scene overall.

#3132. Google Is Forcing Schools to Choose Between Its Search Engine and AI

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Experts weigh in on the promises and pitfalls of Google’s new policy, which leaves schools unable to use Search unless they opt into its AI tools.

Google will automatically turn off its AI products for K-12 student accounts, requiring school administrators to manually opt in before students can use the technology, the company announced in a blog post on Tuesday. But there’s a big catch: If schools choose not to enable AI, students won’t be able to access Google Search.

In August, the company’s AI products were automatically enabled in Google Classroom, Google’s free learning management system for students of all ages. Tuesday’s announcement reverses that decision.

In October, tech and child-safety organizations wrote an open letter to 150 US school districts and their leadership, requesting that districts disable Google Gemini AI on school devices, concluding that “children are at risk of being harmed.”

“After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom and Google Search,” wrote Chris Phillips, Google’s vice president and general manager of education.

For student accounts with Google Search enabled, Gemini will feature a new Guided Learning study tool. The interactive feature helps students solve homework problems step by step rather than providing direct answers. Later this month, at select US schools, Guided Learning will launch as a pilot program to collect feedback before rolling out to more schools.

According to a 2024 Google blog post, over 150 million teachers and students worldwide use Google Classroom and Assignments, the company’s assignment distribution and grading app.

A 2025 New York Times survey sent to the 20 largest US school districts found that Google’s Chromebooks, which start at $179, are the most common device in classrooms, with about 80% of teachers reporting that their school district provided Chromebooks for students.

The default search engine on Chromebooks is Google Search.

New York City Public Schools, the largest US public school district, is a Google client. When asked for comment, a NYC Public Schools spokesperson pointed to NYC Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s X post.

“Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools,” Mamdani wrote in the Tuesday post. “No corporation should have the power to dictate how we teach our children. Our public schools exist to serve students, not to grow the profit margins of Big Tech.”

The second largest US district, the Los Angeles Unified School District, is also a Google client.

A Los Angeles Unified spokesperson told CNET in an email that the organization is reviewing Google’s announcement and its potential implications for schools, educators and students. The district believes emerging technologies, including AI, have the potential to enhance teaching and learning when introduced thoughtfully, responsibly and with clear safeguards, the spokesperson said.

Ying Xu, an assistant professor at the Harvard Graduate School of Education and founder and director of the Harvard Child-Centered AI Lab, told CNET in an email that Google’s Guided Learning tool could buffer some of the negative consequences of using general-purpose AI tools.

“But to me, the deeper question is: Do students still need to learn how to search for information using traditional search engines?” Xu said.

In her work, Xu found that seeking information using traditional search engines, as opposed to AI models, involves different processes, ranging from how students formulate queries to how they evaluate information to how they integrate it from multiple sources.

“I’m a bit concerned that, even with Guided Learning, students may be losing opportunities to practice these important information-seeking skills,” said Xu. “One concern I have with Google’s announcement is whether schools will still have the option to support traditional information seeking without AI features.”

Until recently, the conversation focused on whether AI should be allowed in schools at all. Now, Google’s announcement is forcing schools to decide which specific AI features to use and under what conditions, without the support needed to make these decisions.

“I do think there’s a risk of widening inequalities here, as schools with more resources and technical expertise may be better positioned to navigate these decisions, while others may have fewer options,” Xu said.

However, she doesn’t think that moving away from Google entirely is necessarily the solution, especially for districts that already rely on its systems.

Instead, Xu thinks the type of support available to schools as they navigate AI-related decisions will be crucial.

“With or without Google’s decision about search functionality, the responsibility falls on schools to protect students’ right to learn from human teachers and peers in human-led classrooms,” said Jennifer L. Steele, a professor at American University’s Baker School of Education. “Tools like Gemini Guided Learning, which uses AI to walk students through difficult questions, are not the answer.”

When schools and teachers rely on tech companies to teach students how to use their AI tools and understand their strengths and weaknesses, that’s when problems occur, she said.

“If schools take that responsibility seriously, then how Google configures its apps won’t really matter,” Steele said.

When asked for a response to the backlash surrounding the decision to turn off its search engine for schools that reject its AI, a Google spokesperson pointed to the company’s X post stating that schools can choose to turn on Gemini, Gemini Notebook or Search individually.

However, that post doesn’t address the fact that AI Mode and AI Overviews will still be enabled alongside Google Search. So if schools don’t want AI enabled for students at all, they will still lose the ability to use the search engine.

A Google spokesperson didn’t immediately respond to a request for further comment.

Tracy Pizzo Frey, a former Google managing director, wrote on LinkedIn that Google could allow schools to turn off AI Overviews, but instead is choosing to take search away from students.

Search engine DuckDuckGo, for instance, has noai.duckduckgo.com, its AI-free search engine that automatically disables all AI features and filters out AI-generated images.

“AI overviews is a search module like maps, flights or weather,” a DuckDuckGo spokesperson said in an email. “Google can determine when it shows up and when it doesn’t.”

Experts caution that educators can’t count on tech platforms to police themselves. Because AI tools are now permanent fixtures, schools must shape their own path forward.

The primary hurdle is an uneven playing field, where wealthier districts have the resources to adapt, leaving underfunded systems struggling to keep up.

#3131. Your Apple Watch has 2 new controversial listening features – but you have to opt in

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind are finally here, two months after Apple unveiled the always-listening Apple Watch features. Here's how they work.

Siri Recap and Live Rewind, Apple’s two controversial, always-listening Audio Intelligence features, are now available on the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4 with the iOS 27.3 developer beta. Both features are opt-in.

Apple unveiled Siri Recap and Live Rewind in September alongside its Series 12 and Ultra 4 smartwatches, but it waited to release the features until after the smartwatches launched. Siri Recap provides a high-level summary of meetings, while Live Rewind continuously buffers audio so the watch wearer can pull up a transcript of the last 15 seconds.

Also: Apple’s Audio Intelligence is priming us for its real AI wearable future

To try both features today, update your iPhone to the iOS 27.3 developer beta, and then update your Apple Watch.

Within the Siri app, users will find a new section called “Recaps,” where both the Siri Recaps and Live Rewinds live.

Siri Recap isn’t listening for nitty-gritty details within a meeting or recording a transcript’s worth of text. It’s more geared toward a conversation’s key takeaways, like important dates, times, prices, and action items. A demo of the feature showed an off-the-cuff conversation between a parent and a child discussing college application strategies. Siri Recap took notes on the high school activities the student could pursue to include in their application, the topics they could write about in their essay, and the colleges they could consider applying to.

After a conversation, a user can save the meeting notes in the Siri app or export them to Notes or Journal. If they don’t save the conversation, it’s automatically deleted after seven days.

Apple designed the feature to exclude certain information from the Siri Recap. It includes a person’s name only if they introduce themselves or if their name is regularly brought up in conversation. Otherwise, it avoids identifying speakers. It also scrubs the audio of language that promotes self-harm, financial and authentication data, and personal identifiers.

You can enable or disable Recaps at any time through Control Center. You can create something akin to a Focus mode to automatically enable the feature once your Apple Watch detects you’ve reached your office or college lecture. Each morning, Siri Recap sends a notification to alert you that the feature is on. At the end of each day, you get a reminder to check your daily recaps in the Siri app.

The feature constantly assesses whether a conversation is relevant, optimizing its detection based on the conversation’s context, proximity, and length. It pauses summarization when a user is on a phone or FaceTime call, plays music, or uses earbuds. It won’t work when Bluetooth devices like AirPods are in use.

Double-press the Digital Crown to see a transcript of the past 15 seconds of conversation. The Apple Watch chimes and displays an animation to indicate it’s in use, then shows the transcript for 30 seconds.

Also: Is cellular on Apple Watch worth it? 4 ways people use it – and why I opt out

Live Rewind is built for those head-in-the-clouds moments when you miss a tidbit of information and can’t go back and ask what a speaker said. I think about how useful this could be in a college lecture or boardroom setting, where dozens of people’s conversations and questions prevent someone from asking a clarifying question about what was discussed.

The user can send the snippet to the Siri app to save it or ask about it (if they want to look up the definition of a mentioned term or get more information on the history of a mentioned topic, for example). If the user doesn’t act on it, the snippet is deleted. Any snippet you save is encrypted through iCloud. The audio buffer updates continuously, and unused audio is automatically deleted. It’s processed on the device and not sent to Apple.

Think of the Secure Exclave as a private island on the S11 chip where the Apple Watch Series 12 and Ultra 4 process audio. A secure audio buffer processes the raw audio, and a secure pairing mechanism privately enables the connection between a paired Apple Watch and iPhone.

Also: The Apple Watch Ultra 4 just hit its lowest price ever ahead of Black Friday

For Siri Recap, the S11 chip listens to detect whether the watch wearer is part of a current conversation. Once detected, audio enters the Secure Exclave, where it’s encrypted and sent to the Secure Exclave on the iPhone. The iPhone turns that audio into condensed text, essentially editing the conversation to remove filler words or irrelevant information, and then it’s sent to private cloud compute. Private cloud compute creates a recap, delivered to your iPhone’s Siri app.

This process ensures that no one, including Apple, can access the audio data.

Don’t want your Apple Watch to listen to you? Siri Recap and Live Rewind are off by default, according to Apple.

If your Apple Watch prompts you to enable Audio Intelligence during setup, you can choose which features to turn on.

On its support page, Apple said you can “turn on Siri Recap in the Watch app on iPhone.” Once it’s enabled, you can “turn it on or off at any time from Control Center.” To enable Live Rewind, Apple said to “open the Watch app, tap Siri, then tap Live Rewind and turn on ‘Double Click Digital Crown.’”

I haven’t tested these controls myself. I’d expect you can follow the same steps to switch Live Rewind off, but I haven’t confirmed that yet.

#3130. The Austin Powers steamroller: How to trade CoreWeave's slow moving crisis

In Austin Powers: International Man of Mystery, a Virtuocon guard famously stands in front of a commandeered steamroller, screaming "No!!" three times before being run over. The joke, of course, is that the hazard moved so slowly he had ample time to step out of the way.

For months, equity traders holding CoreWeave (CRWV) have played that guard. Despite credit markets flashing warnings, equity holders essentially just stood there. Now, after a shocking 27.5% drop from last Friday's close to this Thursday morning's 52-week lows, followed by a whiplash 17% rebound, equity investors, if not squashed like Dr. Evil's henchman, are dazed and confused. Shares are down another 5% today.

With earnings just a few weeks away, massive leverage, a heavily split sell-side, and 15.4% short interest, the stock remains primed for explosive volatility.

The hazard approaching CRWV equity wasn't hidden. The AI cloud infrastructure platform essentially didn't exist five years ago and has accumulated $51.6 billion in debt (~$46 billion net of cash) against a backdrop of zero historical profitability. That was well known, but the CEO's comments that the company aspired to be investment grade so it could borrow "unlimited sums", even as investors already anticipated nearly another $60 billion in debt in the next 18months, spooked folks. (Trick or Treat!) Especially since the debt issued only in June had already fallen 20% since.

From an options trader's point of view, it was hard to reconcile deteriorating credit and rising leverage with falling options premia; as recently as last week, they were in the 6th percentile, 1.5 standard deviations below the one-year mean. At 72%, options weren't cheap in absolute terms, but given everything going on, they should have been higher. The stock also looked somewhat complacent, with the equity chopping around historical support levels dating back to May 2025. The credit side was on tilt:

Because CRWV is levered by more than 2:1, a 10% move in enterprise value translates to a nearly 22% swing in equity value.

Despite the dire credit picture, equity sentiment remains fiercely divided, driving this week's 17% bounce from the lows. For example, HSBC maintains a Sell rating with a $39 price target, essentially calling for the stock to be cut in half. Conversely, banks like BNP and Cantor Fitzgerald (my former employer) suggest these fears are overblown and the stock should more than double from here.

It's not just bankers defending the company either. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang downplayed systemic concerns, and ARK Investment stepped in to buy 192,000 shares. BofA argues that CRWV's ability to bring active power online at scale, combined with higher pricing from Nvidia's upcoming Vera Rubin deployments, will drive faster-than-expected revenue growth. They described execution risk as a bigger concern than financing; I'm not sure those who bought the 2032 8 1/2s at par would agree.

With CRWV's credit heavily distressed but its equity heavily defended by high-profile dip-buyers, going outright long or short into earnings carries excessive directional risk. A November/January Call Diagonal, currently trading for just under $6 per spread. Almost 9% of the current stock price. Normally, that's a lot, but given this stock has fallen 27.5% and then gained 17% in the past 5 sessions, it isn't.

#3129. Why SK Hynix Plunged in October

Outstanding earnings and soaring profits weren't enough to defy last month's AI stock selloff.

Shares of South Korean memory giant SK Hynix (SKHY +2.08%) fell 20.2% in October, according to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence.

While SK Hynix actually reported strong numbers at the end of the month, this wasn't enough to offset the big downturn in AI stocks, especially semiconductor stocks related to the AI build-out.

SK Hynix tends to be highly volatile within the already volatile semiconductor sector. October was an especially bad month, with virtually all AI stocks selling off violently in the first part of the month.

This was due to several factors. The first, and likely most prominent, factor was the leaked third-quarter revenue number from the leading AI lab Anthropic ahead of its IPO. Although the leaked $16 billion-plus revenue figure represented staggering growth over the prior year, investors might have hoped for more, given numbers that had already leaked in August.

The thinking goes that if Anthropic or its competitors don't make enough revenue, they won't be able to cover their large computing commitments, which could slow the AI build-out.

Add in the uncertainty over the midterm elections, especially state-level races in states where many data centers are being built, as well as rumors that the U.S. may renew military operations in Iran after the midterms, and artificial intelligence stocks saw an across-the-board sell-off to start the month.

The sell-off occurred even in stocks of companies that are doing well. That includes SK Hynix, which posted eye-popping results later in the month. Even so, it wasn't enough to claw back the big decline earlier in October.

In the third quarter, SK Hynix saw revenue skyrocket 328% to 104.6 trillion Korean Won, while earnings per share rallied 205% to 54,454.00 Korean Won per share. However, operating profit grew much more, by 632%. Operating margin came in at a whopping 80%.

SK Hynix is leading the AI memory boom, driving an unprecedented up cycle. In fact, SK Hynix only trades at 3.5 times its 2027 earnings estimate. That's stunningly cheap. Additionally, management will pay a large dividend of 20,000 Won per share to shareholders at the end of this year. That's a 1.5% dividend yield at current stock levels.

However, it appears the sustainability of those earnings is being closely scrutinized. Given how much revenue and earnings have accelerated this year, factors such as the AI build-out's financing availability, the anti-data-center sentiment in the U.S., and the aforementioned concern over AI lab revenue are all contributing to concerns over how long the boom can last.

If SK Hynix can just maintain its current level of earnings power, the stock is now remarkably cheap. However, one could say the same about many AI-related stocks right now.

The fate of these stocks depends on what happens with the data center build-out, not during this earnings season, but in 2028 and beyond. Therefore, investors won't know the answer to their SK bet for some time.

Billy Duberstein and/or his clients have positions in SK Hynix. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The iPhone Duo likely won't make it into Santa's sleigh this year. Here's what Apple had to say about it.

Apple is unsure when it will have more of its foldable iPhone Duo in stock for customers to purchase ahead of Black Friday and the holiday shopping season.

During Apple’s earnings call on Monday, Nov. 2, Apple’s chief operating officer, Sabih Khan, told investors that the company couldn’t say when supply of its new iPhone Duo would catch up with current demand.

Also: iPhone Duo hands-on: Some magical touches

“iPhone Duo is constrained as of today … We’re working hard to get this revolutionary new product into the hands of our customers as quickly as possible. But at this time, it’s really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance,” Khan told investors.

The stock and shipping delays were among the broader concerns on an earnings call riddled with questions about rising memory chip costs driven by the AI industry, according to reports from TechCrunch.

Also: iPhone Duo shortage watch: Changing wait times, buying limits, launch-day odds

On the call, Khan noted that Apple has had to increase prices on numerous products, though he said those decisions were made rather “reluctantly.”

As Apple works to iron out its kinks, customers may be looking elsewhere. Reports from Korea indicate that Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 sales jumped 20% in the week after the iPhone Duo became available, and recent reports of cracked displays are another strike for Apple.

Also: Want an iPhone Duo before 2027? Try this

#3127. Shooting Video With Honor’s New Magic 9 Pro Max Lets You Feel Like a Movie Director

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Phone-maker Honor partners with the iconic cinema camera maker ARRI to enhance the look and color of videos recorded on its Magic 9 Pro Max.

While standing on the roof of the Griffith Observatory in Los Angeles for the first time, I pulled out my phone to commemorate the moment by filming a video that pans toward the Hollywood sign. And then I did it again, using a special cinema mode created by the legendary movie camera company ARRI to give it some old Hollywood flair. The second video had a grainier look and could have been shot in the 1960s, with the sky appearing teal.

This might look like a filter, but it’s actually ARRI’s Nostalgia look — one of many color configurations available on the company’s cinema cameras, which start at $80,000. I’m using it on a smartphone from the Chinese company Honor.

This Honor Magic 9 Pro Max, announced Wednesday, looks a lot like an iPhone 18 Pro Max, with a similar unibody metal design and two-tone back glass. I used the phone in a comforting moss green, but it also comes in shadow black and glacier silver.

Honor’s focus on video production underscores the increasing trend of smartphone use in studio environments. And the company is banking on its partnership with ARRI to help its Magic 9 Pro Max stand out among a number of phones with their own Hollywood pedigree — including the iPhone.

Even if you aren’t familiar with ARRI, you’ll likely know movies filmed with the company’s cameras, such as 2022’s Everything Everywhere All at Once and 1947’s Dark Passage, starring Humphrey Bogart and Lauren Bacall. ARRI’s cameras are designed for movie sets and priced accordingly, costing $65,000 to upward of $95,000 new. Instead of creating a cheaper camera, ARRI is bringing its color technology to the Magic 9 Pro Max’s camera app, which includes a dedicated ARRI Cinema mode.

This cinema mode uses the phone’s 200-megapixel wide and 200-megapixel telephoto cameras and lets you capture video with a wider 2.39:1 aspect ratio — similar to what’s used in theatrical releases. Cinema geeks may be particularly interested in the mode’s access to ARRI’s Look Library and its ability to install LUT files. Some are preloaded, while others can be downloaded from Honor’s and ARRI’s websites.

To put it as simply as I can, Looks and LUT files allow you to record video with a variety of cinema-like looks, including settings that can closely mimic an ARRI Alexa camera.

During a media demonstration at ARRI’s Los Angeles facility, two different film sets were recorded by both an Alexa camera and a Magic 9 Pro Max with similar looks and LUT settings. Both sets had ideal lighting, but the recorded footage showed similar color shading and detail. An ARRI rep pointed out that you have to look at the fine details, like the shadows of a bookshelf, to see the differences between the phone and cinema camera. Clearly, the Alexa camera was far better at capturing the scene, as it should.

Using the Magic 9 Pro Max, I filmed my own faux rainy-evening scene featuring dark green walls, a brown set and two actors speaking to each other. While I do like the set’s movie-like look, I was more impressed when I shuffled through the different ARRI Looks while watching the sunset at Griffith Observatory. Like the iPhone’s Photographic Styles for still images, ARRI Looks offer plenty of choices for video, including several black-and-white options. It’s fun to know that they’re the same options available to camera teams using ARRI’s full-fledged cinema cameras.

Honor hasn’t shared the Magic 9 Pro Max’s price or release date, but it won’t be coming to the US. However, it does have quite the spec list beyond its ARRI Cinema mode, including a 6.8-inch display, an 8,000-mAh battery with 100-watt wired charging and 80-watt wireless charging. The phone’s powered by the new Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 processor.

A 50-megapixel ultrawide camera joins the wide and telephoto cameras, but it doesn’t support the ARRI Cinema mode. The 55-megapixel front-facing camera uses a square sensor, like Center Stage on recent iPhones, allowing vertical selfies whether you’re holding the phone vertically or horizontally. Honor said this is a first for Android. I’m hopeful that more Android phone companies will bring this feature to their front-facing cameras in 2027.

The phone also ships with Android 17 and Honor’s MagicOS 11 overlay (it looks a lot like iOS 27), and joins other Android phones with support for Apple’s AirDrop via Quick Share.

As it stands, Apple’s iPhone 18 Pro Max is being used in some baseball broadcasts, and the 2025 film 28 Years Later was shot primarily using the iPhone 15 Pro Max. Honor’s partnership with ARRI might lead to more cinema-quality features on smartphones, especially if more productions decide the pocket-sized camera is the right way to tell more big-screen stories.

Editors’ note: Mike Sorrentino’s travel costs for the Honor event were covered by Honor. The judgments and opinions of CNET are our own.

#3126. Want a Challenge? Try These 10 Wordle-Like Word and Puzzle Games Now

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Many of these games are free, and you can play them as many times as you want.

The popular word game Wordle is five years old, and it’s still popular. Josh Wardle launched the game in 2021 before The New York Times bought the game the following year. Since then, millions of people are estimated to play the game each day. If you want help with the daily game, CNET’s Gael Cooper has loads of tips and tricks to help you tackle each NYT Wordle puzzle, as well as solutions to each one.

Once you finish your Wordle, you’ll have to be patient and wait until the next day for the next puzzle. Thankfully, there are plenty of other word and puzzle games to choose from if you’re still craving a challenge. And some of these games let you play as many times as you want in a day.

Here are 10 other puzzle games you can play now for a fun challenge.

Another New York Times-owned puzzle, Connections is a tricky word game. “Players must select four groups of four words without making more than four mistakes,” the New York Times explained on X. There are also four color-coded difficulty levels for each game; yellow is the easiest, then green, then blue and finally purple. The game is also similar to the BBC quiz show Only Connect, and the show’s host took to X to point out the connection. See what I did there?

You can play Connections on any web browser, but you need a New York Times subscription (which starts at $1 a week) to play.

Strands is another New York Times-owned puzzle but this game resembles a word search more so than Wordle and Connections. This game presents a theme every day to help you find words in a grid. In Strands words can appear forward, backward, top-to-bottom or any number of ways in a traditional word search, and words can also form in the shape of an “L” or have a zigzag in them. When you find a word, tap the first letter and drag your finger to the other letters. Every letter in the puzzle is used, so if you still have letters that aren’t connected to words, you aren’t finished yet.

You can play Strands on any web browser but you need a New York Times subscription (again, $1 a week) to play.

You can play Quartiles on an iPhone or iPad but you need an Apple News subscription (which starts at $13 a month) to play.

Quordle has you solve four word puzzles at once, which sounds daunting.

Are you up for a challenge? If you love Wordle and want puzzle games that take more brain power, you’ll want to check out either Dordle, Quordle, Octordle or Sedecordle. Each of these word games resembles Wordle, but they add more rows, columns and words to solve. Each game requires you to simultaneously solve a different number of words at once: Dordle has you solving two words, Quordle four at once, Octordle eight at once and Sedecordle a whopping 16. Good luck.

You can play Dordle, Quordle, Octordle or Sedecordle on any web browser.

“Lewdle is a game about rude words,” this game’s content advisory reads. “If you’re likely to be offended by the use of profanity, vulgarity or obscenity, it likely isn’t for you.” Translation: It’s Wordle but with bad words. The words range from mild — like poopy — to words that would make a sailor blush. Thankfully, despite this game’s content warning, slurs are not included. Like Wordle, gray, yellow and green blocks are used in the same way and there’s only one puzzle per day. So go forth and let the bad words flow!

You can play Lewdle on any web browser. You can also download this game from Apple’s App Store or the Google Play store.

Not off to a great start with this Antiwordle puzzle.

Tired of seeing those gray, yellow and green blocks plastered all over your social media feed? Give Antiwordle a try. While Wordle wants you to guess a word in as few tries as possible, Antiwordle wants you to avoid the word by guessing as many times as possible. When you guess, letters will turn gray, yellow or red. Gray means the letter isn’t in the word and can’t be used again, yellow means the letter is in the word and must be included in each subsequent guess and red means the letter is in the exact position within the word and is locked in place. If you can use every letter on the keyboard without getting the word correct, you win. Honestly, I’ve found this version of Wordle to be much harder than the original.

You can play Antiwordle on any web browser.

Absurdle bills itself as the “adversarial version” of Wordle. While Wordle nudges you in the right direction with each guess, Absurdle is trying to avoid giving you the correct answer. According to the game’s website, “With each guess, Absurdle reveals as little information as possible, changing the secret word if need be.” Absurdle doesn’t pick a word at the beginning of the game for the player to guess. Instead, it uses the player’s guesses to narrow its list of words down in an effort to make the game go as long as possible. The final word might not even include a yellow letter from one of your earlier guesses either. You can guess as many times as you want, which is helpful, and the best score you can get is four. Have fun!

You can play Absurdle on any web browser.

For more word game fun, check out CNET’s Wordle tips and everything you need to know about the word game. You can also check out what to know about the other New York Times-owned games, Connections and Strands.

#3125. Semiconductor Sell-Off: The Best Chip Stocks to Buy Right Now Before They Skyrocket Once Again

Semiconductor stocks can emerge stronger from the recent correction in the sector, suggesting now is a good time to buy top names trading at attractive valuations.

Semiconductor stocks witnessed a brutal sell-off on Friday, Oct. 30, after Intel's guidance came in well below expectations due to a declining personal computer (PC) market.

The PHLX Semiconductor Sector index fell 7% in a single session. Shares of Nvidia (NVDA +2.29%) were down more than 4% on Oct. 30, while other popular semiconductor stocks, such as Broadcom (AVGO +3.07%) and Micron Technology (MU +2.58%), also pulled back substantially.

However, I think that the sell-off in this sector is overdone. That's because chips play a central role in various applications, including artificial intelligence (AI), smartphones, computers, vehicles, and factories. Bank of America estimates that the U.S. semiconductor industry's revenue alone could reach $3.2 trillion in 2030, up from an estimated $1.7 trillion this year.

As such, now is a good time to buy some top names in this sector following the recent pullback.

Nvidia and Broadcom are among the top two semiconductor companies capitalizing on the AI-fueled chip demand. Both companies have been enjoying solid growth lately, and the good news is that they expect to sustain their momentum going forward.

Nvidia management, for instance, noted on the August earnings call that it expects revenue to increase by 70% in fiscal 2028. Nvidia CFO Colette Kress noted that "this is a supply-constrained outlook," suggesting that the company could clock faster growth if it can fulfill more demand. Nvidia's guidance is quite impressive, given that its top line is expected to jump 90% in fiscal 2027 (ending in January next year) to nearly $412 billion.

Even better, the company's earnings per share (EPS) are anticipated to jump by 95% in fiscal 2027, followed by a 71% increase in fiscal 2028. Meanwhile, Broadcom expects its AI revenue to double over the next couple of fiscal years. The incredible demand for Broadcom's custom AI processors and networking chips will push its AI semiconductor revenue to $58 billion in the recently concluded fiscal 2026, up by 186% from the prior year.

The company forecasts $230 billion in AI semiconductor revenue in fiscal 2028, nearly 4x the AI revenue it is poised to generate this year. So, it is easy to see why analysts are anticipating both Nvidia and Broadcom to clock healthy EPS growth over the long run.

AVGO EPS LT Growth Estimates data by YCharts

Moreover, both stocks are attractively valued following their recent slides, especially when their growth potential is considered. Nvidia has a price/earnings-to-growth (PEG) ratio of just 0.27, while Broadcom's PEG ratio is 0.99, according to Morningstar. The PEG ratio is a forward-looking valuation metric that accounts for a company's projected annual earnings growth over the next five years.

A reading below 1 suggests the stock is undervalued relative to its growth potential. So, investors are getting a good deal on these AI stocks that could make them richer in the future. Nvidia's 12-month median price target of $320 implies a 58% jump, while Broadcom's target of $530 suggests 64% gains. As such, buying these top chip stocks right now could prove profitable.

Micron Technology has been seeing exponential growth in revenue and earnings due to the terrific demand for memory chips and the accompanying supply shortage.

The good news is that the favorable memory market dynamics fueling Micron's growth are sustainable. Micron's peer SK Hynix recently noted that the memory shortage won't be going away until 2030, driven by robust demand from AI data centers and emerging AI applications such as humanoids. As a result, SK Hynix estimates that memory demand will continue to outpace supply until the end of the decade.

This is great news for Micron stock investors, especially given that it trades at an attractive 12 times earnings. Micron's 24% share of the dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) market and 15% share of NAND flash storage memory put it in a terrific position to sustain the healthy growth it has been clocking over the past couple of years.

Not surprisingly, Micron has a 12-month median price target of $1,575, implying 89% upside from current levels. The stock could easily outpace analysts' targets over the coming year as it trades at an extremely attractive valuation even though it is expected to deliver long-term EPS growth of 55%, according to YCharts.

As such, savvy investors can consider using Micron's latest pullback to buy a top growth stock trading at an attractive valuation, as it could make them richer over the long term amid the secular growth of the memory market.

Bank of America is an advertising partner of Motley Fool Money. Harsh Chauhan has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Broadcom, Intel, Micron Technology, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3124. The Switch 2 Zelda: Ocarina of Time Is a Brilliant Remake

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

It’s back, completely refreshed, and an absolute joy to experience again.

It was way back in 1998 when I finished Ocarina of Time. I remember how incredible it all was, not only to play but also to look at. N64 games back then felt stunning — so impressive that I rushed out to make the N64 the first Nintendo home console I ever owned.

Those days are gone, and N64 games now look pretty rough to my eyes. But with the $60 Ocarina of Time remake, exclusive to the Switch 2, Nintendo brings some of that magic back again; I’ve never had more fun replaying a game in my life.

When I first tried the new version of Ocarina for about an hour a few weeks ago, it felt like it was replacing my old memories of playing the original game. More than a week into playing at home, I can confirm that the remake has completely replaced my memories of the original. I’ve revisited the N64 version a few times through the Switch Online N64 app, but it feels so minimal and awkward compared with the remake that now I can’t go back.

But it’s also brought me right back to the feeling I had in 1998. Like in the game itself, I’ve been whisked back in time to re-experience the past — and it’s better than it’s ever been. I’ve been down the Ocarina rerelease road before, when the 3DS version arrived in 2011, but the Switch 2 version is far more impressive. It’s not a new game, but at times it almost feels like one.

Everything in Ocarina of Time has been created fresh again. Like some sort of setbuilder making all the pieces of an old show from memory, it’s astonishing how Ocarina is an old game made with completely redone graphics. It outdoes Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom in visual splendor, with similarly gorgeous open vistas at every turn.

Just as Ocarina of Time inspired Breath of the Wild, it’s Breath of the Wild that’s now reshaped Ocarina — at least, visually. The scope, story and challenges in Ocarina remain just about the same, creating a more intimate experience despite its sense of epic scale. If you’ve never played it before, think of it as a theme park version of the supersized Hyrule in Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom.

But I appreciate its more contained scale even more now. I got lost in Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom, and not always in a good way. To be honest, at times, parts of those games began to feel repetitive. Ocarina of Time’s story is told more directly, with clearly laid-out goals and a faster pace. It feels like a true fusion of the sandbox-like open worlds of recent Zelda games and the intricate, puzzle-driven storylines of older ones.

I’d love this new Ocarina even if it only brought updated graphics, reworked sound and new voice acting, which grew on me. But its control scheme makes the game far more intuitive to play. The old Ocarina didn’t have a jump button: Link just auto-jumped! Here, you can jump anywhere. The old game never had a second analog stick to control your viewing angle: It relied on “Z-targeting” to look ahead or to recenter your focus on objects and enemies. Now, you can look around and Z-target with a trigger button — something that modern Zelda players probably just take for granted.

Some areas are different now: Early on in Kokiri Forest, the way you find the first sword is different. The dialogue has also changed. That’s just the tip of the iceberg when it comes to the many little shifts here, which become hard to notice. At some point, my experience of the new 2026 version simply took over and overwrote all my old, fuzzy memories of the 1998 version. It’s now, once again, the game as it felt to me back then.

Maybe it’s that I’m getting older, or I don’t replay games such as Ocarina of Time year after year like some do, but I realize I forgot a lot of what happens in this game. But I look forward to every night I get a chance to play it again. It’s that good.

I didn’t think that would happen, not with RPGs and Zelda games advancing so much over the years. It’s a testament to Ocarina of Time that its structure and mystery still feel perfectly paced. There are tons of side missions, but the main story arc is strong and continuous. I felt a sense of awe more than once, even when it came to things I knew would happen. The time travel — which I hope doesn’t spoil too much for anyone who’s never played before, though, hey, it is called Ocarina of Time — still feels wondrous.

I was skeptical about Nintendo spending all its resources updating classic games over the last year or so — a trend that started with Starfox, continued with Switch Sports Resort and has returned here. I wanted new games for the Switch 2 — not retreads. But I have to admit that Nintendo has done these remakes with expert care. And in the case of Ocarina of Time, the effort truly feels like it transcends the original. As much as I ended up loving Star Fox again, it never felt as much like a new game as this Ocarina remake does.

Is it a truly new game? No. But it feels like the most singular Zelda game again, taking precedence even over Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom. And with its graphic overhaul, it now feels like a cousin to those games in a new way, a sort of trilogy (but not exactly). I find myself, once again, appreciating how absurd and adorable all the characters are. Zelda games have such a goofy sense of humor, and I feel like Ocarina does more than Breath and Tears do. I’ve gotten a kick out of finding every side character in Hyrule just to see their new faces.

And the dungeons in Ocarina of Time are so good. I want Zelda games to return to the kind of intricate, puzzle-filled dungeons found here.

I’m so stunned by how much I love the new Ocarina that I’m wondering what other magic Nintendo could work on other throwbacks. Do this for Mario 64, or Pilotwings.

The Legend of Zelda: Majora’s Mask is the “other” Zelda game for N64, and it’s even more brilliantly conceived. Remaking it for the Switch 2 now seems like a when — not an if — considering Ocarina’s Hyrule has been completely rebuilt with new graphics. Nintendo brought Ocarina and Majora to 3DS, and the cycle could repeat again here. I’d be all for it.

Could this all be a massive test run for a future Zelda that doesn’t exist yet? It certainly feels like it — but when? The remastered version of Metroid Prime paved the way for Metroid Prime 4’s release. Beyond the battle-focused Hyrule Warriors: Age of Imprisonment, this is the first Zelda game made exclusively with the Switch 2’s hardware in mind — and its lush visuals show it. It’s the best-looking game on the Switch 2 yet, and of course it makes me wonder what a new Zelda game could feel like. Or heck, a new Mario.

This reconstruction of Ocarina of Time feels like it’s strutting, dripping with luxury, full of glorious style, a symphony played with a full orchestra. And somehow, unleashing it all in service of a fantastic remake doesn’t feel excessive. I’m having a blast playing it, but I also want to see what this opens up for the next generation of Nintendo Switch 2 games.

But if you’ve never played a Zelda game before and have a Switch 2, I envy you: The new Ocarina of Time is an incredible place to start.

#3123. SpaceX or Micron: If I Could Only Own One for the Next 5 Years, It Would Be This

Between a newly public rocket company with a volatile stock and a concentrated CEO, and a memory chip maker with $100 billion in contracted demand already locked in, here's why one is the easier buy-and-hold pick.

If I were forced to pick only one of these two stocks to own for the next five years, I'd take Micron Technology (MU +2.58%) over SpaceX (SPCX +3.62%), and not because I doubt what SpaceX has built. Rockets that can land themselves safely for reuse and a satellite internet business with real revenue are genuinely remarkable achievements. My hesitation is about the stock and the man behind it, not the rockets.

SpaceX went public in June at $135 per share, first traded at $150, and popped as high as roughly $226 in the days that followed. Then it tumbled to a low of around $105 and has since round-tripped back to around $134 to $140 in recent sessions, close to 40% below its peak.

The bigger issue for me is control. SpaceX's IPO filing spelled out the nature of a dual-class share structure in which Musk's Class B shares carry 10 votes each, while the Class A shares that regular investors can actually buy carry just one vote each. The result: Musk holds around 82% of the voting power while owning less than half the economic stake in the company. As a shareholder, you're along for the ride, not in the driver's seat, and that ride includes a CEO who's also running Tesla (TSLA -1.32%), X, xAI, and a handful of other ventures simultaneously. I don't think that makes SpaceX a bad business. I think it makes it a harder stock to hold for five years, given how tied it will be to wherever Musk's attention goes next.

Micron just closed its best fiscal year ever. Its fiscal 2026 revenue came in at $133.19 billion, up from $37.38 billion the year before, and the fourth quarter alone brought in a record $54.23 billion.  In the company's Sept. 30 earnings release, CEO Sanjay Mehrotra framed it plainly: "AI is becoming Super Intelligence (SI), and memory enhances this intelligence and the competitiveness of our customers' platforms." Micron also guided for roughly $61.5 billion in revenue for its current quarter, which would be another record.

What I find more convincing than the revenue number is how Micron is locking that sales growth in. The company has signed 16 strategic customer agreements -- multiyear take-or-pay contracts with price floors that bind customers to purchase set volumes of memory whether they need it in the moment or not. Management says those agreements carry a cumulative minimum revenue value of roughly $100 billion, and customers have already put up about $22 billion in cash deposits and related financial commitments to back them. That's not Micron hoping demand holds up; that's customers prepaying to guarantee they get supply at all. Micron also pays a dividend of $0.15 per share quarterly, something SpaceX doesn't offer its shareholders in any form.

Both companies are riding real, durable trends; demand for space infrastructure isn't going away, and neither is demand for AI data center memory. But one of these stocks comes with a CEO who controls the board regardless of what public shareholders think and a chart that has already demonstrated how violently it can move, while the other just posted contracted revenue visibility stretching years into the future, backed by customers putting real cash on the table.

Over the next five years, I'd rather own the business with its hand already forced by binding contracts than the one where a single person's attention, mood, or next venture can move the stock overnight. Micron wins that comparison for me, not really because SpaceX is a bad company, but because it's a much harder stock to hold on to.

Micah Zimmerman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Micron Technology and Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
