#3122. So You Bought an iPhone 18 Pro. Do You Still Need a Real Camera?

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Apple's phone takes beautiful photos, but is it as good as using a Fujifilm X-M5?

Editor at Large; Lead Photographer, Europe

Editor at Large; Lead Photographer, Europe

The iPhone 18 Pro is among the most accomplished phone cameras around, packing an array of sensors and lenses that are all capable of taking stunning images in a variety of conditions. Throw in the new creative tweaks to Apple’s Photographic Styles and the iPhone 18 Pro becomes a formidable partner to serious photographers.

But how does it compare to an actual camera? To see why you’d pick one over the other, I took the iPhone 18 Pro on some photo walks alongside Fujifilm’s X-M5, a compact-sized mirrorless camera with interchangeable lenses and a host of filmic profiles to choose from.

This isn’t designed to be a direct comparison between each specific model — after all, a phone has other advantages beyond a camera, some of which I’ll come to later. I’m not going to pixel peep at ultrafine details nor estimate which has the technically best dynamic range. This is more about which allows you to be more creative and more engaged in your image-taking and what each one offers.

With the iPhone 16 Pro Apple brought its Photographic Styles with the grid-like tool for adjusting the color tones and shadow depth for each of the looks. It was a huge boon to iPhone photographers who want to shoot with a specific look without having to manually edit each image after the fact. Apple took that further on the 18 Pro by bringing in more advanced tools, including the ability to add image grain and bloom to your images and giving manual control over white balance. Finally.

Fujifilm, meanwhile, has won the hearts of millions of photographers the world over with the ability to create filmic styles that can be shared and installed as “recipes” on its cameras. Like Photographic Styles, these looks change everything from color tones to film grain and allow you to shoot with an almost infinite array of filmic styles with no editing required.

In practice, both operate in much the same way. On the iPhone, I erred toward warmer styles such as Amber and Gold, bringing the shadows down and adding some film grain. My Kodak Gold-inspired Fujifilm recipes aimed to achieve a similar effect and while they’re not identical, I can’t say I like either one of them over the other.

I don’t like all of the iPhone’s styles, but there are a lot of Fujifilm recipes I don’t like either, and arguably this just comes down to personal taste anyway. But I have noticed that there’s a lot that Apple can do to improve Styles for photographers.

For one, you can’t save a specific style as a preset to recall later. While you can tell the phone to remember your last-used settings each time you open it, if you change those settings, you’ll need to remember what they originally were if you want to go back. It also means it’s difficult to share those presets with others, whereas Fujifilm camera owners have created a vast community sharing recipes far and wide.

Overall, Fujifilm’s recipes give much finer control over the look of your image and make it easier to recall a certain preset when you want to start shooting away. Both the iPhone and X-M5 (and indeed almost all regular cameras) also allow you to shoot images in raw format too, allowing you to apply your own edits later on in apps like Adobe Lightroom, Snapseed or VSCO.

The iPhone’s size makes it the dream camera for street or travel photography where you may be walking for miles every day and don’t want to be weighed down by equipment. It just slips into your pocket and can be whipped out just as easily when inspiration strikes. While Fujifilm’s X-M5 is small for a camera, with a lens on you’ll struggle to get it into a pocket, meaning you’ll need a dedicated camera bag to carry it around — or have it around your neck on a strap, which could become uncomfortable after a full day out.

That’s doubly the case if you have extra lenses you’d want to take with you, which the iPhone has built in. Even Fujifilm’s endlessly popular fixed-lens X100VI is still big enough that you’ll only manage to get it into a large coat pocket at best.

On the other hand, cameras are built to be ergonomic when taking images. Their larger sizes give you something to grip onto and hold steady. By comparison, holding a skinny iPhone when taking a photo can often feel delicate, especially when you’re trying not to accidentally press on the screen. Personally, I don’t enjoy the feel of taking photos with a phone, and it’s one of the reasons why I’d always reach for a real camera over a phone for a day’s photography.

That’s especially true when it comes to adjusting shooting settings. Changing exposure compensation means multiple taps on the phone’s screen. Ditto if you want to change white balance or shutter speed. Most cameras like the X-M5 or X100VI offer physical controls to instantly change settings when out shooting in the field, often without even needing to look, saving you crucial seconds when capturing the decisive moment.

While Fujifilm’s popular X100VI has a fixed lens offering a constant 35mm focal length, the X-M5 — along with many others in the range — has interchangeable lenses, allowing it to swap between a plethora of lenses from both Fujifilm and a wealth of third parties. Whether you want an ultrawide fisheye lens, a super-long telephoto for wildlife shots or creative lenses like PolarPro’s LightLeaks lenses, there’s an option out there.

But that means spending more money on more gear and then having to carry an increasingly heavy kit bag out on location to use all that fancy new glass. The iPhone has its multiple lenses built right in. Its focal lengths include 13mm, 24mm, 28mm, 35mm, 48mm, 100mm and 200mm, all from one pocket-sized device.

Sure, most of those include some kind of digital cropping or upscaling and the quality isn’t what you’d find from a pro-standard telephoto zoom lens, but for most people who want to take snaps out and about in the street, the iPhone’s lens options essentially put a full kit-bag of gear into your pocket.

If you do want more lens options for your iPhone, there are a range of add-ons you can pick up. I love shooting with Moment’s clip-on lenses, especially the anamorphic lens for video and the macro lens for close-up work. Then there’s PolarPro’s various filters, like the Everyday filter, which is the same one I slapped onto the front of the X-M5.

As Apple’s phones are so popular, accessories for them are the easiest to come by compared to almost any other Android phone. But the same can be said for Fujifilm’s camera — and most other compact cameras around. Alongside the aforementioned armada of lens choices, you can also pick up thumb grips and half cases to customize the camera and things like flashes and tripods for taking your photography further.

The iPhone, of course, has the benefit of also being a smartphone. It’s not just a camera, meaning that when you’re done taking your photos you’ve got the option to instantly edit them in whatever apps you like and share them to Instagram or over WhatsApp without first having to transfer them to your computer.

But for me that’s also one of the biggest downsides of using a phone as a camera. I’ve written about this before, but the problem essentially boils down to wanting to focus on one task at a time. A camera has just one function: taking pictures. I pick it up to do one thing and that’s all I have to pay attention to.

When I’m shooting with my phone, I can be bombarded with incoming notifications, phone calls and other pop-ups, all of which are desperate to take my attention away from the act and craft of shooting photos. I love having a great phone camera with me for those occasions when I want a photo and I’m not carrying a camera, but when I want to be purposeful in my photography and plan to head out to shoot work I’m proud of, it will always be a dedicated camera that I’ll reach for.

Which is best for you depends on two factors: Your budget and how serious you are about your photography. The iPhone is a superb camera phone for out-and-about snaps and while it’s certainly possible to take pro-level photos with it, it’s not always as easy to use — or as inspirational — as using a proper smaller camera like a Fujifilm X-M5, X100VI or other models like a Ricoh GRIV, Leica Q3 43 or even a small mirrorless camera.

Plus, if you’re keen for a great all-round imaging experience and you don’t have the cash for two tech purchases right now, going all in on just an iPhone 18 Pro is a smart move. But if you want to take a more authentic and intentional approach to your photography and feel creatively inspired by holding a physical camera and twiddling real, analog dials will absolutely benefit from looking toward adding a camera like the Fujifilm X-M5 or X100VI to your photography arsenal.

CNET Editor at Large Katie Collins found that when she used a proper camera on a big trip to China instead of just her phone, it rekindled her passion for photography that had long sat dormant.

While smartphone photography has come on in leaps and bounds in the past few years, there’s still nothing that beats that feeling of lifting a real camera to your eye and firing that shutter button.

#3121. Mortgage Rates Today, Thursday, October 29: Kind of a Big Jump

TL;DR: Average mortgage interest rates jumped today.

If you're here looking for lower mortgage interest rates, today is definitely not your day.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.32% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 22 basis points higher than yesterday and 24 basis points higher than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

This comes after the Federal Reserve announced central bankers’ decision to hold the federal funds rate steady yesterday. While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, the federal funds rate is one factor that moves them. Last month, when markets began pricing in a possible federal funds rate increase, mortgage rates spiked.

The next Fed meeting isn’t until December, so any rate movement in the next week will likely stem from other factors, like the upcoming jobs report. For more on that, read on.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Traders expected that the Fed would vote to hold the federal funds rate steady yesterday, so that wasn’t much of a factor for mortgage lenders as they set rates.

In his post-meeting press conference, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said that September’s rate increase is “starting to work some” toward moving inflation down to the Fed's 2% goal, but that central bankers don't have any firm conclusions yet. He also acknowledged that the Fed’s monetary policy actions hadn’t achieved price stability yet, but that they’re “working on it.”

This hints that if inflation data continues to trend upward, central bankers might raise the federal funds rate again at their December 8-9 meeting.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, dropped this morning — a day too late for central bankers to consider it in their decision-making process.

The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on November 25.

Next week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out Friday, Nov.6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build an even stronger case for the Fed to raise the federal funds rate again in December, because it would suggest that the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.82% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

#3120. Apple Wins Big in CNET’s People’s Picks Awards for Its MacBooks

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Two popular non-Apple brands still won awards for their affordability.

Of all of the laptop brands to choose from, CNET readers say Apple’s signature MacBooks really live up to the hype.

We recently surveyed CNET readers as part of our People’s Picks awards to find out what they think about their laptop and the brand behind it to help you make a more sound choice on the laptop you may be considering over the holidays. We listed several well-known names, including Dell, Lenovo — and Apple. We asked about everything that matters most on a daily basis, like battery life, durability and even its performance for handling tasks like gaming and graphics. Out of 10 categories, Apple swept up eight of the awards and claimed the overall winner for CNET’s People’s Picks award.

Here’s what readers say Apple gets right when it comes to its laptops, what the experts think about Apple’s landslide win and other brands worth considering.

Apple wins the overall award in CNET’s People’s Picks, and also takes home the overall satisfaction rating, with 74.2% of Apple users saying they’re extremely satisfied with their laptop. In comparison, 22.5% say they’re satisfied — bringing Apple to a near-perfect overall satisfaction score of 96.7%.

Apple’s overall impression also speaks to other awards it wins from CNET readers, including high ratings for its impressive battery life, display quality and high-quality build. All of those categories and more sum up Apple’s brand loyalty nod, with 68% of MacBook owners saying they would buy the same laptop and model from Apple again.

How much are people paying for MacBooks? CNET found that 43.5% of Apple users paid at least $1,500. Until earlier in 2026, the MacBook Air was Apple’s entry-level laptop, priced at least $1,299. The introduction of the budget-friendly MacBook Neo brought that down to a more reasonable $699. However, despite Apple’s laptops being more expensive than those from other brands, they still ranked high for their value proposition. Part of the reason for that is that MacBooks received high marks for hardware reliability. “Apple’s MacBooks might cost more than the average laptop, but their premium build quality means they’re built for the long haul,” said Matt Elliott, CNET’s laptop editor.

If you plan to have your laptop for a while, you may be impressed by other features that make work easier and faster, like its M-series central processing unit for task management and AI capabilities, great battery life and a one-of-a-kind operating system — currently MacOS 27. All of the features and specifications bundled together make Apple’s perception as a high-end laptop worth the investment for long-term satisfaction.

Apple clinches a near-perfect score for its value perception. According to our survey results, 44% of Apple users said their MacBook has exceptional value. Others say their MacBook is worth what they paid for it (50.9%) — for a total positive score of 94.6%.

Digging deeper into Apple’s cost-to-value rating, 81.8% of Apple owners paid at least $1,000 for their laptops, which may seem costly, but Apple users seem OK with the price. That could be because, even though Apple has only three laptop models in a handful of sizes in its lineup, each one delivers solid performance and storage.

Take Apple’s proprietary processing chips, for example — each upgrade since the M1 release has improved the laptop’s speed, battery life and performance. Now the M6 can handle the MacBook’s AI capabilities, storage and high-resolution graphics. And for all of that power, most MacBooks are relatively thin and lightweight, making them easier for mobility — just a few of the reasons Apple users may be OK with paying more for what they’re getting.

Battery life is a big deal for many laptop buyers, and that’s another area where Apple delivers. Over a quarter of those surveyed (25.7%) said their MacBook’s battery life exceeds 10 hours — which is more than enough for a full day of work in most cases. And 18.7% say their MacBook’s battery lasts between 8 and 10 hours — totaling 44.4% of MacBook users saying their battery lasts 8 hours or more.

Comparatively, non-Apple laptop brands fall short in this category. Only 16.4% of Lenovo users say their laptop’s battery lasts at least 8 hours, and even fewer for Dell (12.7%) and HP (11.8%). If you’re leaning toward a MacBook for its battery life, CNET’s lab test shows that the MacBook Neo lasts 13 hours and 26 minutes for online streaming, whereas the Apple MacBook Air 13-inch M5 (2026 model) lasts 17 hours and 2 minutes.

Apple sweeps the brightness, color accuracy and clarity category with 99.2% of its users either satisfied or extremely satisfied with their laptop’s screen performance. No Apple users reported dissatisfaction.

“Apple has long been a favorite for creative professionals and content creators for good reason: Its MacBook displays are some of the best you’ll find on any laptop,” said Josh Goldman, CNET’s managing editor for computers. “While Apple may use marketing names like Retina and Liquid Retina to describe display features, they do translate into sharp and clear text and images, with excellent brightness, contrast, color gamut coverage and color accuracy.”

The new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR even goes beyond what MacBook users have come to expect. It’s Apple’s first OLED touchscreen laptop and features next-level brightness, color and contrast.

Apple receives high ratings: 80% of MacBook owners say it has premium build quality, 18.2% say it’s solid, and those results don’t surprise us.

And MacBooks seem durable and reliable, with 87% of Apple users reporting no hardware failures on their MacBooks, while 8% say minor software fixes resolved their issues. Only 3% needed minor hardware repairs, and 2.2% needed major repairs — which could be fixes such as a battery or screen replacement.

Our editors say the M5 MacBook Air is thin and lightweight, but still has a well-built aluminum design. And the M5 Pro MacBook Pro is sturdy, too. What sets Apple’s laptop hardware apart could be its aluminum chassis — the casing that holds all of the components together.

“Apple makes three lines of MacBooks — the Neo, Air and Pro — and focuses on making those the best they can possibly be. They are all built to deliver a premium experience using high-quality materials, and that even includes the entry-level MacBook Neo,” said Goldman.

Lastly, Apple uses durable materials, such as the 100% recycled aluminum alloy it says encloses the 14-inch MacBook Pro. And the tech giant has several testing facilities to test MacBook durability.

When it comes to getting your daily tasks done, most CNET readers with MacBooks (70.9%) say Apple’s laptops handle workloads flawlessly. It’s worth noting that 45% use their MacBook for work tasks, but no Apple users reported poor performance.

Here’s why that might be: Apple’s laptops are packed with top-notch performance specs and features, like their powerful processors, unified memory and fast storage. Better processor performance means enhanced graphics, on-device AI capabilities and faster task speeds, all of which add up to being able to finish work, play games or stream videos without issues or lags caused by your laptop’s hardware.

On the software side, MacOS 27 Golden Gate gives you built-in AI features, like Siri AI and Visual Intelligence. And you can organize your Safari tabs by topic and use the Notify Me tool to be notified of website changes, like price drops or breaking news. Plus, you can create shortcuts to automate tasks. Coupling all of these features and specifications explains why Apple users rate MacBooks so highly for getting the job done.

Apple leads the way on brand retention with 68% of Apple users planning to buy the same MacBook model again, while 29.1% would buy another model from Apple’s lineup. Only 3% would switch to another brand that’s not Apple.

Usually, when you buy one Apple product, you’re likely to stick to the brand’s ecosystem, especially since Apple has its own operating systems to access its unique features and work seamlessly with other Apple products with Apple’s Continuity feature. For example, you can start an email on your iPhone, but finish typing it on your MacBook. Or you can call or send a text from your laptop without reaching for your iPhone. Other features work across Apple devices, including iCloud, Universal Clipboard and AirDrop.

Asus wins as a midtier laptop choice with 67% of Asus owners paying between $500 and $1,499. There’s a sweet spot in this range with 38% spending between $500 and $999, and 28% paying between $1,000 and $1,499.

If you’re planning to spend that amount on a new laptop, you may also be pleased to know that Asus earns a higher satisfaction rating compared with other non-Apple laptop brands — 48% of Asus users say they’re extremely satisfied.

Asus laptops have a few features and specifications that may speak to that rating, regardless of budget. For example, the Zenbook A14 has a thin, lightweight design that’s rigid, with long battery life and an OLED display with strong contrast and color performance. This Copilot Plus PC sells for $1,600 (but retailers like Best Buy regularly have it on sale for less). Comparatively, these premium features from other brands could cost more or sacrifice important must-have features for the price.

Asus has other laptops worth exploring depending on your needs, like the budget-friendly Asus ZenBook S 14 or the premium Asus ROG Zephyrus G14 that could be good for gamers and creators.

Over one-third of Acer laptop owners (38%) paid less than $500 for their laptop — which is greater than other laptop brands in this price range. Yet, 44% of Acer users say their laptop has exceptional value, while 50% say the value is fair — bringing a total positive cost-to-value score of over 94%. Acer also has an overall satisfaction score of 92.6% — the highest of non-Apple laptop brands.

If you’re impressed by Acer’s value and satisfaction scores, there are a few budget-friendly Acer options to consider. The Acer Aspire 16 AI wins CNET’s Editors’ Choice award. It has a big 16-inch screen but is still lightweight (3.5 pounds). And it has good battery life (over 21 hours on CNET’s battery drain test). There are a few other pluses, like the 1440p web camera that may be good for video conferencing and a sizable touchpad. All of those features and more add up to $700, which could make it a good computer for work or casual browsing. If you’re looking to spend slightly less, its predecessor, the Aspire 14 AI, is originally $639, but you may be able to score deals on both models during holiday sales.

Even though Apple is highly recommended by CNET readers, there are plenty of other laptop models and brands worth considering depending on your needs. Here are a few non-Apple laptop brands that are runner-ups to CNET’s People’s Picks winners that you shouldn’t count out of your laptop search.

Lenovo is best known for packing a powerful laptop into a compact design with tablet functionality, like its Yoga line, and its durable ThinkPad business models. But CNET readers with Lenovo laptops say the brand’s battery life stands out most compared with other non-MacBook laptops.

Survey findings show that 16% of Lenovo owners say their laptops can run for 8 hours or more. And 10.5% say their laptop battery lasts between 8 and 10 hours, while 6% say their Lenovo laptops can withstand at least 10 hours before needing a charge. That’s more than what readers say about other non-Apple brands. The Yoga 9i 2-in-1 Aura Edition lasts 25 hours and 45 minutes — making it one of the best laptop batteries CNET experts have tested.

Goldman said Lenovo has such a large and varied PC selection using the latest chips from Intel, AMD, Qualcomm and Nvidia that there is something for everyone and every budget. Even those who spend less on a Lenovo laptop can expect solid performance, including battery life.

If your laptop’s hardware fails or it breaks, you may be in the market for a new laptop sooner than expected. Think about cooling fan problems, overheating or your laptop not powering on at all. Over half (68%) of Lenovo laptop owners said they haven’t had any hardware issues with their laptops — the most compared to other non-MacBooks.

Beyond hardware durability, Lenovo also scores highly for its build and construction — overall making it a sturdy laptop choice. In our survey, 38% of Lenovo laptop owners say their laptop has a premium feel, while 51% say it’s solid — totaling a positive score of 89%.

If you’re looking for recommendations, the Lenovo Yoga Slim 7x Gen 11 has an all-metal design. And the Lenovo ThinkPad X1 Carbon Gen 14 is a business laptop with Lenovo’s well-known X1 Carbon exterior and new Space Frame design, which Elliott says makes it easier to repair or replace parts if needed.

Many Lenovo laptop users are loyal to the brand, which speaks to the overall positive satisfaction rating of 83%. If Lenovo owners were in the market for a new laptop, nearly three in four (73.2%) say they would buy another Lenovo laptop. Breaking down the percentage, 33.6% say they would buy the same model again. Meanwhile, 39.6% would buy a different model but stick to Lenovo.

Here’s why that might be. Lenovo offers a plethora of options to choose from based on your needs — while still evolving its laptops. For instance, the Lenovo Legion 5i Gen 10 has an open M.2 slot for a second storage drive and an OLED screen for crisp, colorful images — making it perfect for gaming. Its Yoga two-in-ones let you seamlessly switch between a tablet and traditional laptop, while Lenovo’s ThinkPad and ThinkBook business laptops offer everything from ultralight 2-pound bodies to expanding rollable screens. And its new designs don’t sacrifice the must-have needs like long battery life, durability and chip efficiency that lend to strong performance.

Asus wins over nearly half of its laptop owners, with 47.5% giving it an exceptional value rating  — exceeding expectations for its price. Take our Editors’ Choice pick, the Asus Zenbook A14. It’s super thin and lightweight, but it’s still sturdy thanks to its Ceralumnium body. Plus, it has supreme 24-hour battery life, 1TB of storage and an OLED display — all for $1,200. While that’s more expensive than some laptops, it’s what’s included in that price that makes it worthwhile compared with other non-Apple laptop brands.

Asus is the top non-Apple laptop option to consider for tasks, with 47.7% of CNET readers giving it high ratings. That’s followed by 40.4% of Asus laptop owners who say it has very good task performance. When it comes to tasks, 37% primarily use their Asus laptops for work, while 5% use theirs for gaming.

Asus is well known for having lightweight laptops with all-day-and-night battery life and stunning display quality. Asus laptops like the Asus ROG Zephyrus G16 have a good port selection, which could be good for gaming, work or simply connecting different devices without the need for additional adapters. And the Asus Zenbook S 14 has a strong neural processing unit that contributes to its high-speed AI performance.

Asus scores highest for its screen brightness and clarity, with over half (51%) extremely satisfied with its screen quality and brightness, while 38.6% say they’re satisfied, giving Asus an overall satisfaction rate of 90%. One common thread we noticed in Asus laptops is their OLED screens, which deliver high-resolution video and graphics. Asus laptops also combine stunning display quality with excellent performance and at surprisingly low prices for all of their features.

For example, the Asus Zenbook 14 isn’t the highest-quality OLED we’ve seen, but it still produces clear text and graphics on a 14-inch screen, along with exceptional black levels, Elliott said. That’s still better than LCDs that many other non-MacBooks have, and could be the reason Asus owners rate it so highly in this category.

Dell wins our readers over as a top choice for high-end laptops. One-third (33.3%) of Dell owners spent over $1,500, and of that percentage, 15.2% spent over $2,000.

Dell’s premium laptops largely cater to business and gaming with their standout features and performance. The Alienware 16X Aurora is Dell’s midrange gaming laptop with user-replaceable RAM, a bright display and strong performance for $2,280. But if you want more gaming features, Dell has the Alienware 16 Area-51, which has even better gaming performance, upgradable RAM and three SSD slots for $2,530. And if you’re looking for a non-Mac alternative that comes close to MacBook quality and performance, the higher-end Dell XPS 14 is the one to choose. It has the Intel Core Ultra X7 358H CPU, Intel Arc B390 graphics and 1TB of storage for $1,950.

CNET commissioned Alchemer to conduct the People’s Picks survey to compile data. The survey was carried out online, and all figures are from Alchemer. The total sample comprised 1,003 adults. The figures have been weighted and are representative of all US adults (aged 18 plus). Fieldwork was undertaken from Sept. 1 to Oct. 10, 2026.

#3119. My Top 5 Stocks to Buy Before 2027 and Hold for a Decade

All of these offer terrific growth opportunities if you buy and hold.

As the market gets closer to ending the year with its fourth consecutive double-digit gain, the S&P 500 is actually down over the past month. There are worries about skyrocketing artificial intelligence (AI) spending, higher interest rates that could slow the economy, and unrelenting inflation that could start giving more consumers pause.

All economies and markets go through cycles, which is why it's so important to have a long-term investing mindset. Trying to time the market can lead to poor timing and losses, while study after study has shown that it's time in, not timing, the market that drives wealth generation.

Here are five stocks with excellent long-term prospects that I believe can lead to fabulous wealth creation if you hold on for the next 10 years.

I debated which large-scale retailer to put on this list, because Costco Wholesale, Walmart, and Target (TGT -2.51%) each offer value for investors. However, the former two stocks are quite expensive right now, which is why Target, trading at a forward P/E ratio of 16, looks like the bargain to buy right now.

Target has been through a lot over the past few years, and it's shown up in lower comparable sales and profits. Target stock is still 42% off its high, but it's made a comeback this year, up 58%.

The market is celebrating the company's wins this year, including a 3.8% increase in comparable sales in the 2026 fiscal second quarter (ended Aug. 1) and earnings per share of $4.11, up from $2.05 last year.

I believe Target can recover and go on to bigger and better things. It's much smaller than Walmart and only U.S.-based, which provides ample expansion opportunities, and it has faced similar challenges before and bounced back. It was way ahead of Costco and Walmart in e-commerce adoption, and it's likely to embrace new technology.

Finally, it's a Dividend King, and it has raised its dividend for the past 54 years, which is passive income you can rely on.

Dutch Bros (BROS -0.66%) is a small coffee shop chain that's expanding quickly. Customers love its exclusive custom beverages that lean toward the cold and the sweet, differentiating it from the standard coffee shop chain. Its portfolio consists predominantly of drive-thru locations, and it's focused on quick, friendly customer service, which matters to today's consumers.

The company consistently demonstrates robust growth, backed up by new stores and increased same-store sales. Total revenue was up 32% year over year in the second quarter, and same-store sales were up 5.8.%. It's also highly profitable, and earnings are rising as the company scales.

Dutch Bros has 1,225 stores as of the end of the second quarter, but it plans to nearly double by 2029. It sees a long-term opportunity of 7,000 stores, and in 10 years it could reach that goal.

MercadoLibre (MELI -0.87%) has a dual ecosystem including the largest e-commerce platform in Latin America and one of the largest and fastest-growing finance apps. It has a major edge in the ecosystem aspect, keeping customers more engaged on the platform.

The company consistently boasts high growth, with a 50% year-over-year sales increase in the second quarter, its highest in nearly four years. There's momentum across the platform, with a 44% increase in gross merchandise volume, a 14% increase in units per buyer (despite a 26% increase in active buyers), and a 56% increase in total payment volume.

Management feels it has many levers to pull to generate higher growth, and it keeps bringing out improvements and new features. It has a membership program called Meli+ that includes streaming subscriptions, and it recently lowered its free shipping threshold in its largest market, Brazil, which has led to accelerating growth there.

MercadoLibre stock was down 19% this year due to falling profits that it says are necessary to invest in future growth, and it looks like a stock to buy on the dip.

e.l.f. Beauty (ELF +0.74%) is a young mass cosmetics and skincare company that has soared to the top of the industry. The brand, which stands for eyes, lips, face, is demonstrating fantastic growth, and it has continued to introduce new products and lines that captivate its core clientele of fun-loving, eco-conscious consumers.

It also recently entered the luxury market with its acquisition of Rhode, a great sign of what the company can do to generate higher growth.

In the 2027 fiscal first quarter (ended June 30), sales increased 36% year over year. e.l.f. is focused on value, and for a long time it priced all of its products at $1. It has since raised prices to offset higher costs, but it's still a magnet for beauty customers as inflation makes it harder to spend money.

The company was battered by higher tariffs last year, but it's since absorbed the impact, and its margins are improving; gross margin was 83% in the first quarter, 14% percentage points higher than last year.

e.l.f. stock is 49% off its high, but it's up 47% year-to-date, and it's a great time to catch it on the way up.

On Holding (ONON +2.76%) is a newcomer to the athletic-wear industry, and it's quickly capturing market share, becoming the shoe of choice for its affluent client base.

The Swiss company has been growing fast despite the challenging economy and expanding its presence across the globe. While competitors like Nike and Lululemon Athletica have struggled, On has reported strong growth and profits.

In the second quarter, sales increased 22% (currency-neutral) year over year and gross margin improved from 61.5% to 65.4%. That's because the company's premium positioning allows it to charge higher prices, and more customers are willing to pay full price.

The market liked its recent investor day update, when management said it expects high-teens growth through 2029 and a gross margin of at least 65%.

On stock is down 27% year-to-date, but at 16 times next year's earnings, now is a good time to buy.

#3118. Gartner: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Retention, not new sales, is what the transformation is built to protect, and it feeds the metric driving top-line growth.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Gartner (IT -13.19%) raised its full-year financial outlook as contract value growth accelerated for the third consecutive quarter. For long-term investors, the company's ability to expand margins while aggressively reducing its share count demonstrates underlying business resilience despite broad economic uncertainty. Three key insights from management stand out for long-term investors.

Enterprise leaders struggle to derive measurable productivity benefits from third-party artificial intelligence, or AI, software. Gartner guides corporate executives through effective strategy implementation, turning widespread technological disruption into expansion opportunities for its core research subscriptions.

Management is maintaining a rigorous share buyback strategy, allocating available free cash flow to repurchase stock when prices are favorable. This ongoing reduction in outstanding shares concentrates future earnings power and boosts long-term returns for remaining shareholders.

Through its Business Transformation Initiatives, the firm is improving the timeliness and analytical impact of its research content. Faster insights directly improve client retention, which boosts net contract value increase, or NCVI, the key metric driving overall top-line growth.

Enterprise spending cycles are showing clear signs of stabilization across core practice areas, and Gartner leverages its vast research footprint, strong pricing discipline, and high client retention to navigate broader budget pressures while sustaining double-digit return on invested capital.

Long-term value creation remains centered on cash generation and disciplined capital allocation. By continually expanding research coverage across emerging technologies, the business maintains a structural competitive moat that reinforces its market leadership.

The Motley Fool recommends Gartner. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3116. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#3115. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, October 30: Noticeably Lower

TL;DR: Mortgage rates slid enough today to get home buyers' attention.

Mortgage rates have mostly recovered today after yesterday's jump scare.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.18% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and two basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates were elevated this month. The average 30-year mortgage rate for all of October was 7.3%, compared to September's monthly average rate of 6.98%.

While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday. Happy Halloween!

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Traders expected that the Fed would vote to hold the federal funds rate steady on Wednesday, so that wasn’t much of a factor for mortgage lenders as they set rates.

In his post-meeting press conference, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said that September’s rate increase is “starting to work some” toward moving inflation down to the Fed's 2% goal, but that central bankers don't have any firm conclusions yet. He also acknowledged that the Fed’s monetary policy actions hadn’t achieved price stability yet, but that they’re “working on it.”

This hints that if inflation data continues to trend upward, central bankers might raise the federal funds rate again at their December 8-9 meeting.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, dropped yesterday— a day too late for central bankers to consider it in their decision-making process.

The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on November 25.

Next week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build an even stronger case for the Fed to raise the federal funds rate again in December, because it would suggest that the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.68% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

#3114. If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, History Says SCHD’s Dividend Grew Through the Last Two Bear Markets. But Here’s the Catch I'm Watching.

SCHD's history is encouraging, but one recent number changes how I read it.

With the market on edge these days, especially heading into Friday's jobs report, a question keeps nagging income investors: What will happen to my dividends if stocks crash?

The Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF (SCHD -0.97%) has already endured two bear markets since its inception in 2011. The S&P 500 fell about 34% in early 2020 and again by over 24% in 2022. Yet SCHD's annual dividends per share rose about 49% from 2019 to 2022, from a split-adjusted $0.57 to $0.85 per share.

I own the ETF and added to my position last week, so I wanted to know what that record can -- and can't -- tell us. Here's a recent catch in its dividend record that I'm watching very closely.

Adjusted for the ETF's 2024 3-for-1 share split, the Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF paid roughly $0.57 per share in 2019, $0.68 in 2020, $0.75 in 2021, and $0.85 in 2022, according to the distribution history on its website. That's an increase of 17.6% in the year of the COVID crash and 13.9% in the year of the bear market.

However, while the dividend went up both times, SCHD's share price didn't escape the downdraft. Its worst three-month return was -21.6% in early 2020. It also declined during the 2022 bear market.

One caution when you look at the payout history is that SCHD's quarterly payments swing wildly:

So, while the payout grew in both 2020 and 2022, it wasn't a straight line.

SCHD's dividend didn't just grow through those downturns. It grew fast. Overall, dividends per share have grown at a 9% compound annual rate from 2020 through 2025. However, over the past 12 months, the payout has only increased by about 2% compared to the prior year.

That's a sharp slowdown, and it matters because the case for a dividend growth ETF rests on that growth. I don't know yet whether it's a blip or a trend. If the pace stays near 2%, the resilience we saw in 2020 and 2022 is worth less than it looks.

It's also worth noting that no two crashes are the same. For example, the 2020 crash was brutal but brief, and the 2022 decline was driven by rising inflation and interest rates. So, a deeper, longer earnings recession would be a tougher test for the dividend.

The slowdown doesn't erase why SCHD's payout held up in the past. The ETF tracks the Dow Jones U.S. Dividend 100 Index, which screens for yield, five-year dividend growth, and financial strength. The index also revamps its holdings once a year, swapping out companies that no longer measure up.

The ETF holds many large companies with long histories of dividend growth. Its top holdings include Procter & Gamble and Coca-Cola. Big, established businesses like these tend to have the cash flow and financial strength to keep paying through a downturn. Procter & Gamble has raised its dividend for 70 straight years, while Coca-Cola is up to 64 straight years. They're both Dividend Kings, companies with 50 or more years of annual dividend increases.

If the last two crashes are any indication, SCHD's dividend will likely keep rising while its share price would probably decline during a market slump. Depending on the catalyst, it might decline less than the S&P 500, due to the more defensive nature of its holdings, its strong dividend income, and its current valuation (at 18.2 times earnings, it's cheaper than the S&P 500 at 23.6 times earnings).

That's not a bad combination if you're collecting dividends. SCHD currently has a trailing 12-month yield of more than 3%. If its share price declines while its payout continues to rise, investors could lock in an even higher yield on newly purchased shares, enabling them to generate even more dividend income in the future.

I'm not selling SCHD because a stock market crash could be coming, as the ETF's history gives me some comfort. It has historically continued paying a higher dividend, even though its share price has taken a hit. However, the catch is that dividend growth has significantly slowed more recently despite a solid economic environment. That's why I'll be watching its next payout announcement in December to see if the slowdown is just a temporary blip or something more concerning.

Matt DiLallo has positions in Coca-Cola, Procter & Gamble, and Schwab U.S. Dividend Equity ETF. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3112. Apollo Global: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Apollo is keeping its own debt-like returns and avoiding equity-style risk in AI deals, a discipline that matters if the private credit push slows.

On its third-quarter earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Apollo Global (APO -4.95%) announced record fee-related earnings, or fee-based operating profit, of $802 million. The asset manager is expanding its private credit infrastructure while shifting investments into higher-quality debt to protect against market volatility. And by scaling its origination network, the firm can capture fee income across market cycles. Three takeaways from the call highlight management's long-term strategy.

High asset valuations and macroeconomic uncertainty led leadership to pull back from lower-rated loans. Instead, management is concentrating capital in senior secured debt and investment-grade assets, which carry credit ratings of BBB or higher. The goal is to preserve principal while still generating reliable yield.

Trading and evaluating private credit has historically suffered from slow pricing systems and limited market liquidity. To fix that, management is spending heavily on new technology to establish daily valuations, standard tracking identifiers, and automated trading platforms for alternative assets.

Financing for artificial intelligence, data centers, and power grid expansions requires massive long-term capital commitments. Apollo Global avoids taking unhedged equity risk in tech. Instead, the firm structures fully amortizing, debt-like loans backed by high-grade corporate guarantees.

Origination volume reached $114 billion in the quarter, with expanding demand across power and compute sectors. Scale, flexible capital, and proprietary origination all support long-term growth as borrowers increasingly seek private solutions over public markets.

And management believes the firm already has everything it needs in-house to deliver on its strategic targets while opening new distribution channels.

The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3111. How to Make a $50 Holiday Gift Feel Like $100

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Trade time and a little extra thought to enhance gifts this holiday season.

Each month, NerdWallet suggests one wallet win to help you feel more confident about your money. This month’s topic is making holiday gifts feel more valuable, and we’re going to show you ways to elevate a gift without overspending.

Making an inexpensive gift feel fancy is a winter challenge I relish.

As a relatively frugal person who often gets sticker shock when shopping for gifts, I’m always looking for creative ways to save money without diminishing the present. Over the years, I’ve developed a few strategies to make a $50 gift feel like it’s worth at least double that.

In the spirit of the season, I’m sharing these strategies with you. I hope you — and your recipients — find them as celebratory as I do.

What does your gift recipient love to do? It helps if they have a favorite hobby, sports team or other interest.

Let’s say your recipient likes to fish. Giving a gift card to a niche fishing store makes it feel far more valuable than the standard Amazon card because it shows that you considered who they are. And there are specialty stores for every hobby these days.

Maybe gift cards aren’t your go-to. You can easily enhance other gifts, too.

Paying $5 extra to engrave or embroider their name on the gift — whether it’s stationery, jewelry or a backpack — makes the item feel far more valuable than its price tag because it’s customized to the individual.

If you choose a gift that couldn’t have been given to anyone else, it increases its value.

Time required: A few extra minutes to think about your recipients’ preferences and apply them when shopping.

A vintage or thrifted scarf can be a wrapping paper alternative that also functions as a gift. Similarly, you could wrap a gift in tea towels, artwork or online prints that can be reused by the recipient.

Another idea: Wrap a gift in printed sheet music that contains a special meaning for the recipient.

Customizing the presentation of the gift elevates it without adding much to the expense.

Time required: Since you have to wrap the gift anyway, this method doesn’t need to take extra time. You’ll just need to find the cloth or paper you plan to use.

One of my favorite gifts in recent memory involved turning a family photograph into a Jane Austen-era image set on a bucolic English estate with the empire-waisted gowns to match. My mom is a big fan of Jane Austen, so I knew she would appreciate seeing the family in a Regency-style photo.

I hired an Etsy artist to transform my original image, but you may be able to do it yourself with a photo app. Similarly, you could create a custom song, illustration or other form of art made specifically for your recipient.

Adding your own creative spin on a gift shows you spent extra time thinking about the recipient to make something extra special for them.

Time required: Applying your own creativity can be time-intensive, but the process is enjoyable so it goes quickly.

I like to call this technique the hybrid gift approach: You buy something, then get crafty to transform the gift into something one-of-a-kind.

One of my favorite ways to do this is with a photo. I’ll buy a nice but inexpensive frame and then put a photo of the recipient, or the recipient alongside loved ones, inside it.

If the person you’re buying for posts on social media, you might not even have to ask them for a photo in advance. Instead, you can find a favorite one that they already shared and surprise them.

The options are endless, especially with online tutorials to help hone your crafting skills. Adding your own customization to a purchased gift makes it feel special, but doesn’t have to cost much.

Time required: Baking, crafting or creating can take several hours, but again, it’s an enjoyable process.

Experiential gifts tend to go over well, and you may even get to have some fun, too.

One year, I gave my mom a membership to a nearby art museum and tacked on an offer to attend an exhibit with her. We had something on the calendar to look forward to together, and she got the option to visit more times throughout the year.

My parents have given me memberships, too. More recently, they gave me one to a local nature center. It’s great because I can visit on my own or attend kid-friendly events at a discounted rate. It makes me feel part of the community, and I love supporting an organization that helps kids learn more about nature.

Another option is to donate to charity in a loved one’s name and then make plans to volunteer at the organization together.

Experiential gifts help create more family memories together, which is one of my favorite ways to celebrate the holidays.

Time required: Setting aside time to visit a museum or other destination can take an entire day, but it’s time well spent.

The bottom line is that giving a highly personalized gift adds value without eating too far into your own budget. Spend $50. Make someone feel like a hundred bucks.

If you’re following along with us on this monthly financial challenge, start by testing out the tips we shared today over the next few weeks. We’ll be back again next month to learn about setting money goals for the new year. And if you missed last month’s edition, take a look back at how to shop the holiday sales.

#3110. These 4 Restaurant Giants Have Different Ways to Win Diners

Cheap eats, menu buzz, table service, or fresh paint? Here's which restaurant strategy is winning diners right now.

McDonald's (MCD -0.98%), Yum! Brands (YUM -0.37%), Darden Restaurants (DRI -1.54%), and Restaurant Brands International (QSR -0.44%) are all chasing the same customer. That's the person staring at a gas receipt and wondering whether dinner out still makes sense.

Each company has its own way of making the case. McDonald's goes cheap and everywhere. Yum keeps the menu fresh and a little weird. Darden takes care of you at the table. Restaurant Brands is fixing up its restaurants and getting back to basics.

Here's how each pitch is landing in November 2026.

McDonald's sells entry-level food at a scale no rival can match, with more than 45,000 locations worldwide. Its traffic strategy starts with price. The U.S. menu offers $5 Meal Deals, Extra Value Meals, and a $4 breakfast deal. CEO Chris Kempczinski says McDonald's base prices for beef, chicken, and drinks now undercut those of its closest rivals. Management has vowed not to get beaten on value.

The spring push didn't exactly go as planned, though. McDonald's rolled out 10 items for under $3, but about a third of U.S. restaurants didn't price them as recommended, and the launch didn't get much attention. At the same time, the app deals regulars loved went away. U.S. comparable sales rose just 0.8% in the second quarter, and fewer people actually came through the door. Ouch.

Kempczinski called it a bad trade, and the fix is mostly to undo it. The company is restoring digital offers and shifting marketing toward proven value bundles. Call it McBusiness 101.

Yum recently sold Pizza Hut, leaving KFC, Taco Bell, and Habit Burger & Grill in the brand portfolio. The common thread is novelty, and Taco Bell is the poster child.

In the third quarter, it brought back retro favorites through its Decades Menu and teamed up with music artist Peso Pluma and seasoning brand Tajín. It is now launching a pink version of its Baja Blast drink with pop star Olivia Rodrigo. Its Live Más Café test stores have produced more than a dozen new beverages that later rolled out chainwide.

KFC is borrowing its sibling's playbook. Overseas, it's pairing value menus with limited-time items like Captain Nacho. In the U.S., its new Popcorn Chicken sold out in a bunch of restaurants.

Novelty didn't save Taco Bell from a rough summer, though. An industrywide cyclospora outbreak in July knocked U.S. same-store sales down 4% in the third quarter. The menu buzz is helping it climb back, with same-store sales up about 3% over the four weeks ending Oct. 30. That's a pretty quick bounce-back from a serious food safety scare.

Darden is the only full-service operator on my list, and that's the whole pitch. At Olive Garden, LongHorn Steakhouse, Yard House, and Cheddar's Scratch Kitchen, someone takes your order, refills your drink, and keeps the food coming. You don't get that service at Mickey D's or Taco Bell. Guest satisfaction scores were at or near record highs last quarter, and a YouGov survey this year ranked Olive Garden No. 1 among casual-dining chains.

"Olive Garden also ranked #1 among Casual Dining brands for service, dining experience and value," CEO Rick Cardenas said on the Q1 2027 earnings call.

And the food really does keep coming. Unlimited soup, salad, and breadsticks are an Olive Garden staple, and this year's Never-Ending Pasta Bowl came with 10,000 Pasta Passes. About 3 million devices logged in to try to snag one, and they sold out immediately. Darden also keeps its price hikes gentle, trailing overall inflation by about 3 percentage points over seven years.

Same-restaurant sales rose 3.2% last quarter. Olive Garden managed just 1%, partly thanks to the World Cup and lettuce worries, but LongHorn grew 6.8% for its 22nd straight positive quarter. My favorite stat: LongHorn spends less than 0.4% of sales on marketing. Good service sells itself.

Restaurant Brands owns Burger King, Tim Hortons, Popeyes, and Firehouse Subs, and its big idea is refreshingly simple. Instead of inventing new stuff, let's try to make the old stuff better.

At Burger King, that starts with the buildings these days. Restaurants remodeled with the new Sizzle design bring in about $2.2 million a year, compared with roughly $1.7 million to $1.8 million for the average location. The remodels are making a difference, and about 40% of the chain still hasn't been updated.

Then there's the menu. Burger King reworked the Whopper and its chicken nuggets instead of chasing trends, and diners noticed. Whopper sales grew by double-digit percentages, and nugget sales soared more than 35%.

As a result, Burger King U.S. comparable sales jumped 8.4% last quarter, nearly 10 points ahead of other burger chains. It's back in the No. 2 spot in the U.S. burger business, trailing only the Golden Arches. Popeyes is trying the same back-to-basics move by trimming wings to focus on its classic fried chicken, but it isn't working yet. U.S. same-store sales fell 2.2%.

Right now, the chains polishing what people already like are ahead. Burger King's makeover and Darden's table service are delivering, while McDonald's patches up its value menu and Taco Bell rebuilds after a scary summer.

With gas prices up, diners need a real reason to pull into the parking lot, and some of these companies are giving them a better one than others. Investors need to check up on margins, sales growth, and franchising strategies too, but Darden and Restaurant Brands look like good spots for further research.

If that analysis involves a few lunch orders, I'm sure these companies won't mind the extra business.

Anders Bylund has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool recommends Restaurant Brands International and Yum! Brands and recommends the following options: long January 2028 $320 calls on McDonald's and short January 2028 $340 calls on McDonald's. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3109. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."
