#3108. Is Google's New Cryptocurrency Service a Reason to Buy Ethereum or Solana?

The company's new platform plays to institutional cybersecurity concerns.

There will soon be a new crypto security service offered by Alphabet (GOOGL +0.37%), and it could well provide a mild boost for Ethereum (ETH +1.20%) and Solana (SOL -0.50%). Google's new cryptocurrency platform, which it calls Google Multi-Party Compute (MPC), was announced on Oct. 28, and Alphabet is already using it to secure its own digital assets parked on both of those blockchains. MPC's commercial launch is scheduled for early 2027.

Google argues its relationships with traditional financial institutions will give it an edge in providing cybersecure crypto custody, but how that will help Solana and Ethereum is another matter. Let's analyze the situation.

MPC is only Alphabet's latest bid to compete more in cryptocurrency.

Google now also runs its own blockchain, the Google Cloud Universal Ledger (GCUL), which Google unveiled in 2025, and which is also aimed at financial institutions and other approved participants.

The technical basis for the new service is splitting a crypto wallet's private key, the secret code that confers control of the wallet, into fragments that are then held on separate computer systems. This makes it much more difficult for an attacker to assemble a complete private key and proceed with the subsequent theft of funds. Google's tool is also supposed to check each incoming transaction against a list of a financial institution's rules, like spending and transfer limits, before allowing the transaction to proceed.

The fit between this product and its target market is already pretty plainly obvious: In a 2026 survey by American Banker, 47% of the surveyed financial institutions cited cybersecurity as a top barrier to their adoption of blockchain tech. That issue is particularly salient at the moment. On Oct. 29, the largest crypto exchange, Binance, was hacked, and the cyberthieves made off with $6.3 billion.

Google plans to support two different transaction signing methods that institutions can use at launch.

One is the Elliptic Curve Digital Signature Algorithm (ECDSA), which Ethereum uses and which is very widely used elsewhere, and the other is Ed25519, which Solana and many other networks use. Importantly, ECDSA also secures Bitcoin, and the ledger behind XRP, so the company's approach doesn't favor any specific chain.

The bullish factor here for Solana and Ethereum is that Alphabet already uses the platform to secure some of its own digital assets on both chains. It's a minor boost at best.

Offering safer storage to institutions won't make anyone want these coins who does not want them already. Big investors already have custodied access through crypto exchange-traded funds (ETFs), and demand there is both volatile and frequently soft. In the first seven trading days of October 2026, Ethereum ETFs experienced $635 million in net outflows, while Solana ETFs saw $24.8 million in net outflows.

Furthermore, coinholders gain mainly from new net buying and new network usage. Parked coins in long-term storage are rarely used for transactions, adding little to the network activity backing what makes these cryptocurrencies valuable.

Google's GCUL network will be competing for capital and activity, too. Google is pitching the GCUL as a "credibly neutral" permissioned network that any bank can use for payments and asset tokenization, or transforming the ownership rights of stocks and bonds into tradeable crypto. Every bank that chooses to do its work there is business that Ethereum and Solana don't win, and assets they don't get to have parked on their networks.

Therefore, investors should treat this service launch as a small positive, and as a confidence-building step that could eventually lead to some more institutional capital heading to crypto.

In the first half of 2027, watch both coins' ETF flows, and watch for bank announcements of crypto operations naming Google's service as a collaborator. If net ETF inflows return right as banks are signing on, it means that the asset custody story is giving way to new demand, and the situation will be more bullish than it is now.

Alex Carchidi has positions in Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Solana. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, and XRP. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3107. Viking Therapeutics Trades 23% Below Its Own $35 Secondary Offering Price. Here's Why That Could Be a Gift for Patient Investors.

Viking aims to enter the weight loss drug market.

Viking Therapeutics (VKTX -2.76%) has been a company to watch over the past few years. That's because the biotech is involved in one of the healthcare market's biggest growth areas: weight loss drugs. Viking hasn't yet commercialized a product, but it's nearing the finish line. The company's lead candidate is involved in two phase 3 trials, and it recently launched trials for an additional candidate.

Viking aims to compete in a space dominated today by pharma giants Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk. While I wouldn't expect Viking to oust these players from leadership, the company still could score a major win in the space. This is because demand is high, leaving room for more than one or two companies to operate here.

And today, investors are looking at a key opportunity. Viking is trading about 23% below its $35 secondary offering price. Let's find out why this could be a gift for patient investors.

We'll start by taking a look at the weight loss drug opportunity. The market may reach nearly $100 billion by the end of this decade, analysts predict, and that's due to the tremendous demand for drugs in the GLP-1 class. These products act on hormonal pathways that control digestion, so they help manage blood sugar levels and appetite. Lilly and Novo have generated blockbuster revenue from their injectable GLP-1 drugs, and this year they launched oral products, further broadening the potential patient group.

Viking's VK2735 is being developed in injectable and oral formats. Like Lilly's injectable drugs, Mounjaro and Zepbound, it is a dual GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist, so it activates two hormonal pathways involved in digestion. VK2735, as an injectable, is involved in an ongoing phase 3 trial; the oral format recently began its phase 3 study. The company is also studying the candidate in a maintenance dosing trial, with the goal of eventually offering a variety of dosing options to patients.

If all goes smoothly for Viking, it could launch at least one weight loss drug within the next few years. This would be a major moment for the company, ushering in an era of revenue growth.

Meanwhile, Viking also recently started testing another candidate in phase 1, using a different approach. This is an amylin and calcitonin receptor agonist, mimicking the body's hormone amylin and stimulating calcitonin receptors -- both help regulate digestion. This candidate is much further from the finish line, but it's encouraging to see that Viking is filling its pipeline to support potential growth down the road.

The company last month completed a secondary offering, including common stock and convertible senior notes, and raised $575 million to support the phase 3 development of VK 2735. Viking priced the stock at $35, and today, shares are trading about 23% below that level. The stock fell after the operation, as investors frowned on the share dilution that accompanies such an offering. They also didn't like the fact that the offering came at a 16% discount to the previous day's closing price.

Today, however, I'm saying that Viking trading at the current level is a gift to investors. This is because the company is making significant progress in its weight loss pipeline and is nearing the regulatory review stage. If all goes well, Viking could be the next company to join Lilly and Novo in the high-demand weight loss drug space.

In the meantime, clinical trial updates could drive stock performance. Viking stock is known to react to such reports; when it announced phase 2 results for VK2735 a couple of years ago, the stock surged more than 100% in one trading session. That sort of extreme movement may not happen again, but it's likely that positive news still will lead to an increase -- and potential product approval down the road should result in further gains.

Viking's level today, trading at less than $30, seems very reasonable for a biotech at this stage of the development road. All of this means that the stock, trading below its secondary offering level, is a fantastic buy for the patient investor.

Adria Cimino has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Eli Lilly, Novo Nordisk, and Viking Therapeutics. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3106. I Don’t Buy With a Little Help From My Friends

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

To save money on holiday gifts this year, Americans are using these 5 strategies that require coordination with friends and family.

Gift giving can bust a budget, so some Americans plan to have conversations with friends and family this year about setting limits around holiday gifting.

This isn’t as Scroogey as you might think: 55% of holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy that likely involves coordinating with loved ones, according to a new NerdWallet survey conducted online by The Harris Poll.

Money can be a taboo subject, especially among family and friends. But a focused discussion on gift boundaries could be the difference between staying within your budget and piling up debt this holiday season.

What other Americans are doing: 28% of holiday spenders plan to set a per-gift dollar limit with family or friends.

Why this works: It’s simple and easy to remember. Unlike some other ideas on this list, you can probably coordinate this one over text.

For best results: If you’re the one bringing up the topic, you should come with a dollar amount that would be reasonable for everyone in your gift exchange group, so no one feels excluded or overextended.

Your circle of friends and family has a lot of kids.

What other Americans are doing: 23% of holiday spenders plan to buy gifts for children in a family, but not the adults.

Why this works: Cutting out adult gifts altogether can rescue a gift budget that has undergone major “kidflation” as new children join the family.

For best results: If you still want to include adults, give a gift that’s fun for the whole family, like a board game or an experience. And make sure your kid-free loved ones are on board.

You traditionally exchange gifts one-on-one with a friend, but the last few gifts you’ve given — and received — were duds.

What other Americans are doing: 18% of holiday spenders plan to skip exchanging gifts with at least one friend or family member.

Why this works: If you’re feeling gift fatigue, there’s a chance they are, too.

For best results: Frame this as an improvement to the gift-giving tradition rather than a scaling back. Propose an alternative way to celebrate your friendship, like swapping out the $40 gift that’ll probably get shelved for a $7 coffee together.

What other Americans are doing: 16% of holiday spenders plan to do a secret Santa gift exchange, or another game that requires purchasing only one gift.

Why this works: 15% of holiday spenders list buying gifts for more people than they originally planned for as among the reasons they’re most likely to overspend this holiday season. This strategy keeps your list in check, even if the number of people playing grows.

For best results: There are bound to be people in your group for whom the rules to the game are important. If you’re the one organizing, be ready to answer questions — can you trade or steal gifts, for example, and, if so, how many times? Also, this works best for family or friend groups who aren’t likely to go rogue and buy extra gifts anyway.

You have no idea what your college friend’s kids (or spouse) are into.

What other Americans are doing: 12% of holiday spenders plan to give one shared gift per family instead of individual gifts.

Why this works: In addition to saving money, this avoids the awkwardness that comes with uninformed gift giving.

For best results: Presentation is key. Don’t sheepishly give an UNO game you clearly bought on the drive over. You may be cutting back, but enthusiasm is free.

Talking about the holidays and talking about money can be stressful conversation topics. About four in five Americans avoid discussing some money topics with family and friends, according to a 2026 survey by the CFP Board. So discussing both at once may seem daunting. But without a doubt, you’re not the only one thinking about them.

Nearly 9 in 10 (86%) holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy this holiday season. Start with an icebreaker: “Hey, have you noticed how expensive everything is lately? Would you be open to talking about how we can do gifts this year while keeping spending under control?” This conversation might be the thing that’s secretly on everyone’s wish list.

#3105. Tech Sell-Off: 2 Growth Stocks Investors Might Regret Not Buying on the Dip

Artificial intelligence stocks are leading the broader market lower, creating opportunities for investors.

After almost tripling from its 2022 bear market low point, the technology-heavy Nasdaq-100 index is now facing a big test. Investors are increasingly concerned about the sustainability of the artificial intelligence (AI) spending boom, placing pressure on semiconductor stocks like Nvidia and Micron Technology, which are among the largest members of the index.

As a result, the Nasdaq-100 was down 9% from its peak as of the market close last Friday, and the decline could get worse if investor sentiment doesn't improve. However, history suggests this could be a great buying opportunity for investors, because the broader market has always recovered to new highs over the long term.

I'm going to share two stocks investors might regret not buying when they look back on this moment in a few years' time.

Hackers have started using AI to stage highly sophisticated cyber attacks on corporate networks, but enterprises are also placing themselves at risk by exposing their valuable data to third-party AI models without the appropriate guardrails. Zscaler (ZS +0.58%) was one of the early pioneers of the zero trust cybersecurity architecture, which has become one of the best defenses in the AI era.

Zscaler's Zero Trust Exchange protects the entire enterprise, starting with the identity layer. It treats every network login attempt as hostile, analyzing the user's credentials, their location, and the device they are using, which maximizes the odds of catching an imposter. Further, the Zero Trust Exchange only allows users to access the applications they need to do their jobs, so even if a hacker bypasses the identity layer, they won't have unrestricted access to the entire network.

Zscaler introduced new tools to the Exchange earlier this year, which protect enterprises when they are deploying AI. There is AI Broker, which constantly monitors communications between AI agents, and Endpoint AI Security, which protects the computers and devices employees are using to run AI software.

Zscaler believes a "Zero Trust Everywhere" approach is the best way to protect against AI threats. Hackers can't attack what they can't see, so keeping critical data, applications, and agents hidden behind the Exchange is the ultimate form of preventative security.

Zscaler had a record year in fiscal 2026 (ended July 31), with revenue growing by 25% to $3.35 billion. Its stock is trading at a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of 11.3, so it's already cheaper than competitors like CrowdStrike and Palo Alto Networks. Therefore, if a further sell-off in the broader market drags Zscaler stock down with it, investors might be presented with a fantastic long-term buying opportunity.

Meta Platforms (META +2.36%) is the parent company of the Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads social media platforms. Over 3.6 billion people use at least one of them every single day, and considering that is almost half the world's population, it's getting harder for the company to find new signups. As a result, it's trying to lift engagement instead -- if each user spends more time online, they will see more advertisements, and Meta will make more money.

AI is a big part of that strategy. It's embedded in the recommendation algorithms on Facebook and Instagram, where it learns what types of content each user likes to see, and feeds them more of it. According to Meta chief executive Mark Zuckerberg, engagement hit record highs on those apps during the third quarter (ended Sept. 30), so the AI-led strategy appears to be working.

However, there is an even bigger opportunity in the pipeline, and it transcends social media. Meta launched a personal AI agent called Muse in September, and it has already attracted over 10 million users. Zuckerberg recently said one-quarter of them are using it as a personal shopper, with an average purchase value of $200, while others are using it to manage emails, among other things.

Zuckerberg believes Muse could eventually serve billions of people, and it will be capable of improving every area of their lives, whether they want to learn new skills or automate some of life's everyday tasks. This will substantially increase Meta's addressable market, because it could replace the need for traditional internet search engines like Alphabet's Google Search.

Meta stock is down 20% from its peak amid the recent tech sell-off. It's now trading at a price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of just 20.9, so it's much cheaper than the Nasdaq-100 which has a P/E of 29.9. Therefore, this already looks like a good buying opportunity for long-term investors.

Anthony Di Pizio has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, CrowdStrike, Meta Platforms, Micron Technology, Nvidia, and Zscaler. The Motley Fool recommends Palo Alto Networks. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3104. CME vs. Nu: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

CME boasts a 62% net margin and fortress balance sheet, while Nu's 43% revenue growth signals emerging-market momentum, but their risk profiles diverge sharply.

Investors looking for exposure to the financial sector often choose between stability and growth. This comparison evaluates the established CME Group (CME -0.71%) against the high-growth challenger Nu (NU -0.09%).

CME operates the world's largest marketplace for futures and options, benefiting from market volatility. Nu, also known as Nubank, provides digital-first banking services to over 140 million customers in Latin America. While one offers reliable cash flow, the other provides aggressive expansion in emerging markets.

CME Group operates as a pillar among financial stocks, serving a vast array of clients including professional traders and central banks. It maintains an exclusive index partnership with S&P Global (SPGI -1.92%) and recently expanded into AI-related tradable products. One clearing firm accounted for nearly 12% of transaction fees in 2025. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In its latest annual report, filed for fiscal 2025, revenue reached approximately $6.5 billion, representing a growth rate of roughly 6.4% over the prior year. The company reported net income of nearly $4 billion, resulting in a net margin of 62% for the period.

As of CME's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio, which compares total debt to shareholder equity, is approximately 0.1. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term obligations with current assets, is nearly 1. Free cash flow for fiscal 2025 was close to $4.2 billion. FCF is the cash generated after accounting for capital expenditures.

Nu is a dominant force in digital finance, having expanded from Brazil into Mexico and Colombia. The company utilizes a digital-first model to provide credit cards, personal loans, and investment accounts without the overhead of physical branches. Its latest expansion includes a launch in the United States, targeting a massive new market of tech-savvy consumers.

In its latest financial filings covering fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $15.9 billion, a 43% increase over the previous year. The company posted net income of approximately $2.9 billion, resulting in a net margin of roughly 18.1%. This growth reflects the rapid adoption of digital banking services across its primary geographic markets.

As of Nu's December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.5. The current ratio is nearly 0.6. Free cash flow reached nearly $3.5 billion in fiscal 2025, providing the capital needed to fund its ongoing international expansion.

CME Group faces intense competition from established rivals such as Intercontinental Exchange (ICE -0.78%) and Cboe Global Markets (CBOE -1.42%). The company relies heavily on third-party technology partners like Google Cloud, creating a potential point of failure for its high-frequency trading platforms. Regulatory changes regarding digital asset trading or clearing operations could also increase operational costs or limit future growth opportunities.

Nu operates in emerging markets where economic volatility and shifting interest rates can impact loan delinquency rates. The company must compete with traditional regional banks and other digital entrants that are aggressively lowering fees to retain customers. Changes in financial regulations in Brazil or Mexico could also restrict its ability to offer certain high-margin credit products.

Nu appears cheaper using the P/S ratio, which compares market value to sales over the past 12 months, while CME Group carries a higher forward P/E.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Nu and CME Group are two companies in very different stages of their life cycle. CME Group is posting modest revenue growth but has an impressive 62% net margin. Meanwhile, Nu's revenue increased by more than 40% in fiscal 2025. Nu's net margin is not as high as CME Group's, but it's still respectable for a growing digital bank.

Here's how the two stocks have performed over the past five years:

CME Group has bested Nu, but the more revealing part of this chart is the S&P 500's return. The index has outperformed CME Group and Nu by 28 and 50 percentage points, respectively. So if you owned either financial specialist over the past five years, you were trailing the market at multiple points -- and sometimes by quite a lot.

The other thing to note in the chart is Nu's volatility. It's not a big, boring bank, and that may make some investors nervous.

Past performance is no indication of future results, but sometimes neither stock is a particularly attractive buy. I'd sit this one out.

#3100. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3098. Interactive Brokers Group vs. Morningstar: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Interactive Brokers trades at a premium valuation, while Morningstar boasts superior profit margins -- a key tension for value-conscious investors.

Choosing between a high-speed trading engine and an essential data provider requires balancing growth against steady research fees. Will Interactive Brokers Group (IBKR +3.52%) or Morningstar (MORN -0.85%) serve your portfolio better through 2027?

Interactive Brokers specializes in providing low-cost, automated trade execution for sophisticated traders around the world. Morningstar offers indispensable data and research to investors and advisors. Each represents a different segment of the capital markets, yet they both benefit from the long-term trend of increasing global participation in investing.

Interactive Brokers Group operates as an automated global electronic broker, providing direct-access trade execution and clearing services. It serves approximately 4.4 million institutional and individual customers, including hedge funds, registered investment advisors, and proprietary trading groups. This broad reach across more than 200 countries allows it to capture diverse activity among investors.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached nearly $10.2 billion, representing growth of approximately 9.8% over the prior year. The company reported net income of roughly $984 million. This resulted in a net margin of nearly 9.6%, which reflects the portion of total revenue kept as profit after all business costs are paid.

As of Interactive Brokers' December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is 0, showing the company carries no total debt relative to its shareholder equity. The current ratio, which measures the ability to cover short-term obligations with current assets, is approximately 1.1. Free cash flow, calculated as cash from operations minus capital expenditures, was nearly $15.7 billion in fiscal 2025.

Morningstar provides investors with data, research, analytics, and investment products through platforms like Morningstar Direct and PitchBook. The company serves a wide array of clients including wealth managers, asset managers, and retirement plan providers. By positioning itself as a primary source of independent research, it creates a recurring need for its tools among both professional and retail market participants.

In fiscal 2025, revenue reached $2.4 billion, reflecting growth of roughly 7.5%. The company generated net income of approximately $374.2 million for the year. This performance yielded a net margin of nearly 15.3%, indicating the company is effective at converting its sales into actual profit for shareholders.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, Morningstar carries a debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 1.2. This ratio measures total debt against shareholder equity, suggesting the company uses a moderate amount of leverage to fund its operations. The current ratio is approximately 1, and the company generated free cash flow of nearly $442.6 million during fiscal 2025.

Morningstar deals with risks involving potential reputational damage if its dual role as a researcher and investment manager is perceived as a conflict of interest. The company is also exposed to liabilities from errors in its credit ratings or research tools, which are used by massive institutional players such as BlackRock (BLK -0.50%). Additionally, its PitchBook segment is sensitive to cyclical trends in private capital markets, and it faces stiff competition from other data giants like S&P Global (SPGI -1.92%) and MSCI (MSCI +0.40%).

Morningstar appears more affordable based on its lower earnings multiple, while Interactive Brokers Group trades at a higher premium relative to its sales and future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Both of these companies are grouped in the "financials" category, but they do (mostly) different things. Interactive Brokers facilitates trading, while Morningstar provides data (and some investment management products). So it's a little bit hard to compare; it's not an apples-to-apples situation.

Neither company has reported especially impressive revenue growth recently. Morningstar's net margin is better than Interactive Brokers', but the latter has a higher valuation. Why might this be?

Investors are clearly willing to pay a premium for Interactive Brokers, and they might be on the right track here. Morningstar shares haven't even managed to beat the S&P 500 over the past 10 years. Past performance is no indication of future results, but I find that chart pretty persuasive. I think Interactive Brokers Group is the more attractive stock here.

#3095. Venture Global Wants to Be an "AI Utility." Its 10-Gigawatt Plan Still Needs Customers.

Venture Global knows how to build massive energy projects. Can it leverage that expertise to cash in on the data center power crunch?

Hyperscalers are spending staggering amounts on capital expenditures. In their rush to build data centers across the U.S., technology companies face a massive bottleneck that could limit the compute capacity they can bring online: electricity.

The demand for power is creating opportunities across the energy and industrial sectors for companies that can meet these needs and supply the fuel to run them. Venture Global (VG +1.01%), a top-tier exporter of liquefied natural gas (LNG), is getting in on the action as the company looks to transform into a vertically integrated "AI utility."

The company has laid out a plan to bring on 10 gigawatts (GW) of data center compute capacity over roughly the next decade through modular "power islands" and has framework agreements with equipment suppliers, but it has yet to sign a firm agreement with a major hyperscaler.

Venture Global exports U.S. shale gas globally and has taken a unique approach to its business. Traditionally, LNG exporters would build massive liquefaction trains to cool methane gas into LNG.

Building these liquefaction trains is a massive, capital-intensive undertaking, and Venture Global took a different approach with smaller, modular trains that can be prefabricated and shipped on-site. In addition, the Gulf Coast regional grid couldn't supply the electricity needed to freeze and liquefy methane, so Venture has or will soon have 4.5 GW of its own gas-fired power generation capacity.

This laid the foundation for Venture's "AI utility" pivot. Management realized data centers face similar requirements: multi-gigawatts of reliable baseload power and a power grid that cannot meet this capacity demand. By leveraging this expertise, Venture aims to bring on 10 GW of compute capacity through 16 combined-cycle "power islands" by 2034.

What makes this endeavor appealing for Venture is that it also controls the natural gas pipeline interconnects and related logistics, and can provide the power islands along with the natural gas fuel they require. The shale boom has also created a glut of natural gas, which Venture can turn around and sell to its data center customers at better margins -- if energy demand remains strong.

Venture Global has secured framework agreements with GE Vernova for gas turbines and power equipment, and Baker Hughes for systems and controls, spanning 2028 to 2034. The company looks to convert its first reservation into a binding order by the end of this year, with power projected to first come online in 2028 to 2029.

Venture Global's pivot to an AI utility builds on its history of constructing massive power-generation facilities in its LNG business. Next, Venture Global needs to turn this into firm customer commitments from major hyperscalers, which could rewrite the investment thesis for the company. This gives Venture Global strong upside potential, but risks are also high as it invests heavily in building out these power islands.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates kicked off the week with a drop, but they’re still above 7%.

Mortgage rates are cooling off as we roll into November. However, they’re still above the 7% threshold.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.07% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 11 basis points lower than yesterday and 13 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.57% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3092. To Defend Against AI Attackers, Start Thinking Like One

Yair Grindlinger is CEO and Co-Founder of Surf AI.

AI is not black magic. It still has to work through real systems, identities, applications and infrastructure, which means its limits are knowable. That matters, because fear is only useful when it’s aimed at the right things.

For enterprises, the right thing to fear is how AI changes the attacker. AI-driven attacks are faster, more persistent and more sophisticated, and they can exploit a single critical weakness or chain together a handful of small ones until they find a way in. ​​

There are several obvious ways AI changes attacks. First is speed. We’re seeing attacks happen in hours instead of days or weeks because AI systems can operate 24/7. The window for defenders is already shrinking. CrowdStrike’s 2026 Global Threat Report found that the average time it took criminal attackers to move from initial access deeper into a network​ fell to 29 minutes, and the fastest observed breakout took just 27 seconds.

But I think stamina may be even more important than speed. A typical human attacker might spend a few hours trying to get into an organization. If nothing works, they may move to the next victim. AI doesn’t necessarily have that limitation. If you tell an AI system to find a way in, it can keep working 24/7 until it finds something.

Then there is breadth of knowledge. Human attackers are typically good at something, whether it’s social engineering, zero-days or a particular system. AI can have the whole playbook. The same system can work across identity, software vulnerabilities, configurations and other attack surfaces. Because it has both stamina and breadth of knowledge, it can connect those weaknesses together.

That ability to connect weaknesses changes how we need to think about exposure. An attack might begin with an exposed secret, then move to an asset with a risky configuration, a software vulnerability or an identity problem. Each issue might look relatively insignificant on its own. You might classify each one as medium or low risk and decide none is critical enough to demand immediate attention. But together, they can create a critical attack path.

Microsoft made essentially the same point in its July 2026 “Secure Future Initiative“ progress report, noting that attackers can use “frontier models to discover vulnerabilities, chain attack paths, and scale exploitation faster than manual approaches allow.” It also observed that serious security failures can emerge when identity gaps, unmanaged assets and inconsistent configurations sit next to one another.

The Hugging Face intrusion offers a vivid example. In July 2026, the company disclosed that an autonomous AI agent, which had escaped an AI lab’s evaluation environment, carried out an end-to-end intrusion into part of its production infrastructure, with no human directing its steps. The attack moved from code-execution weaknesses to credential harvesting and lateral movement across internal clusters. Hugging Face later reconstructed roughly 17,600 attacker actions as the system searched for a viable path.​ ​Most of those actions failed. The agent simply kept going until something worked.

A human attacker may look for one big open window and move on if it isn’t there. AI will find that window faster, and if it isn’t there, it will patiently look for the little things and chain them together.

Attackers and defenders often see the environment differently. From a defender’s perspective, we frequently manage security in islands. There may be a cloud vulnerability team, an identity team, IT teams and someone else responsible for third-party applications. The attacker doesn’t care about those organizational boundaries. To the attacker, all of those things are part of the same attack surface.

Suppose a team is looking at a cloud vulnerability. On its own, it might not appear urgent. But what if the asset can be accessed by someone whose MFA is misconfigured and who also uses a highly privileged third-party application? If the teams responsible for those areas don’t know the issues are connected, they don’t understand the real priority.

That becomes especially important as AI gives defenders visibility into more exposures. In many cases, these weaknesses were probably always there. We simply know about more of them now. That’s a good thing because defenders can use powerful tools to find the same types of exposures attackers are looking for.

The problem is that organizations will get a lot of results. It will be impossible to manage everything at once. Prioritization therefore has to change. Instead of looking only at how severe an individual vulnerability appears to be, ask whether it is exposed, how it connects to other weaknesses and whether it forms part of an attack chain.

A critical exposure on an important asset still demands attention, whether or not it’s part of a chain. But something that appears lower-risk may also offer a quick win because fixing it breaks a larger chain.

AI has made finding vulnerabilities faster and easier than ever. Closing them still depends on knowing who owns the affected asset, understanding what a fix could affect and getting approval, and that’s where most organizations get stuck. In many of my conversations with CISOs, we never get to attack chains. They have critical exposures they can’t close.

One CISO at a large enterprise told me that 25% to 35% of employees leave or change roles in a given year. Many of them own systems, and the asset inventories meant to track that are almost never up to date. So security teams spend their days chasing people to find out who can approve a fix. That isn’t the job anyone signed up for, and it’s the bottleneck that keeps exposures open. Before you can think in chains, you have to be able to close the basics quickly.​

I see two fundamental requirements for responding to this shift. First, organizations need to know their assets: what they have, who owns each one and what a change could affect. Second, they need automation operating continuously on top of that knowledge.

If attackers are operating at machine speed, defenders cannot depend entirely on human-speed processes. Organizations need systems that continuously collect information, connect exposures and help security teams identify which problems matter most.​​ Then they need to get each fix to the right people, because a prioritized list nobody can act on doesn’t reduce risk.

This matters even for threats no one has seen yet. You can’t patch a zero-day before you know it exists, but you can shrink your blast radius every day by closing the exposures around it, so the next one matters less before it’s even disclosed. And when it does land, you can be ready to answer “are we exposed?” in hours, not weeks.​

For CISOs, my practical advice is to pause and rethink exposure management. The way we have managed exposure until today isn’t necessarily going to work for the AI era. Assign someone, or create a team, whose job is to think about exposure holistically. Then reconsider the people, process and technology required to support that approach.

AI creates real reasons for concern. But the most useful fear is a specific one: attackers are becoming faster, more persistent and more sophisticated. Defenders need to respond in kind: know their assets, shrink their blast radius every day and be ready for the next one.

In other words, we need to start thinking a little more like the attackers.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3091. Breakfast News: Did Nvidia Just Favor Marvell?

Nvidia’s likely role in the Marvell-Lumentum deal, AI stocks hit by midterms, and more…

In June, Nvidia (NVDA +2.29%) CEO Jensen Huang interrupted Marvell’s keynote to call Marvell Technology (MRVL +0.33%) the next trillion-dollar company. Five months later, on Monday, Marvell agreed to buy Lumentum (LITE -0.62%) in an all-stock deal valued at $115 billion. All three companies are Fool recommendations, and we plan to hold the combined Marvell and Lumentum indefinitely.

Both companies play a key role in a change inside artificial intelligence (AI) data centers, where more data now moves between chips as light instead of electricity. Every light-based connection needs a laser, and Lumentum is one of the few companies that can make them at scale. Marvell already designs the chips that work alongside those lasers, so this deal gives Marvell a laser supply of its own.

We believe Nvidia “helped” bring these two companies together, because a stronger Marvell gives Nvidia a heavyweight ally against Broadcom (AVGO +3.07%).

The companies’ upcoming merger filing will list every meeting that led to this deal, including any involving Nvidia. Until then, we’re holding both stocks and plan to own the combined company for years.

In a single 24-hour window on Wednesday, three distinct forces – corporate security, global diplomacy, and electoral politics – converged to prove that the biggest threat to AI growth isn't model performance, but friction.

The AI game is changing. Revenue won't flow to who builds the biggest model. It will flow to who builds the strongest governance, runtime security, and infrastructure to keep it running safely.

Team Hidden Gems sees EMCOR Group (EME +3.61%) reaching $1,523.33 within five years, more than double today’s price of about $669. Our first Team Hidden Gems rec in November 2024 is already up nearly 50%. EMCOR wires and cools hospitals, factories, and data centers, and it wins by bringing a Hidden Gems favorite, technical superiority, to an industry built on hard hats and blueprints.

At about 21 times trailing earnings, EMCOR’s tech edge comes at a fair price, and we expect it to carry the stock toward our target.

#3089. If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, Warren Buffett's 7-Word Investing Advice Has Never Been Wrong

Volatility could be coming, and Warren Buffett's time-tested advice is key right now.

Stocks have been shaky in recent weeks, with the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%), Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.38%), and Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +0.83%) slipping by around 3%, 4%, and 8%, respectively, from their recent all-time highs.

Investors are also feeling pessimistic about the future. The Fear and Greed Index measures investor sentiment on a scale from 0 to 100, with lower numbers indicating fear and higher values suggesting greed. As of this writing, the index sits at just 23 -- in the "extreme fear" category.

If a stock market crash is coming, it's not all bad news for investors. Warren Buffett has a simple seven-word strategy for times like these, and it could help you generate life-changing wealth amid market volatility.

In 2008, Buffett penned an opinion piece for The New York Times. The U.S. was deep into the Great Recession at that point, with the S&P 500 having plunged nearly 40% over the previous year, and many investors were losing hope that the market would ever recover.

In the essay, Buffett encouraged investors to not only stay in the market, but to buy more. He noted that the market had endured severe hardship over the previous century and still managed to deliver record-shattering returns. Those who reaped the biggest rewards were the investors who continued buying.

"[B]ad news is an investor's best friend," he emphasized. "It lets you buy a slice of America's future at a marked-down price."

Since that piece was published, the S&P 500 has earned total returns of more than 1,000%. In other words, if you'd invested $10,000 in an S&P 500 ETF the day Buffett offered this advice, you'd have more than $112,000 by today.

"You might think it would have been impossible for an investor to lose money during a century marked by such an extraordinary gain," Buffett continued, discussing the Dow's meteoric rise from 66 to 11,497. "But some investors did. The hapless ones bought stocks only when they felt comfort in doing so and then proceeded to sell when the headlines made them queasy."

If the headlines are making you queasy right now, that's understandable. But the right investments can set you up for lucrative long-term returns, even if a crash or recession is coming.

Not all stocks are strong enough to weather a significant downturn. During the dot-com bubble burst, for example, countless tech companies crashed and never recovered. Many powerful companies collapsed during the Great Recession, too. Whenever the next bear market begins, there will inevitably be some companies that don't pull through.

Organizations with strong foundations are the most likely to succeed. These companies will have sustainable competitive advantages, sound finances, competent leadership teams, and other factors that set them apart from their peers.

"To be sure, investors are right to be wary of highly leveraged entities or businesses in weak competitive positions. But fears regarding the long-term prosperity of the nation's many sound companies make no sense. These businesses will indeed suffer earnings hiccups, as they always have. But most major companies will be setting new profit records 5, 10 and 20 years from now."-Warren Buffett, The New York Times (2008)

Over time, the market is all but guaranteed to thrive. Since 1919, the S&P 500 has ended every one of its rolling 20-year periods with positive total returns. This means that if you'd invested in the S&P 500 at any point in the last century and held your investment for 20 years, you'd have made money.

Nobody can say when the next market crash or recession is coming, but strong companies are the most likely to thrive over time. With a healthy portfolio and a long-term outlook, you can set yourself up for lucrative gains.

Katie Brockman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased again today, giving buyers modest relief from rates above 7%.

Mortgage rates are continuing to cool off as we head into November.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 6.93% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and 17 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

A significant drop is eye-catching, but bear in mind that you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. Since September, mortgage rates have been hovering around 7%. That’s a full percentage point higher than they were at the beginning of 2026.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.43% or higher — but there aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3082. Cipher Digital: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

How upfront engineering and on-site power infrastructure protect long-term returns from inflation and grid delays.

On its third-quarter 2026 business update call, held on Nov. 2, 2026, Cipher Digital (CIFR +18.37%) reported its first revenue from high-performance computing (HPC) leases as it shifts away from Bitcoin (BTC +1.15%) mining.

The company develops data centers for artificial intelligence workloads and leases them to major tech companies under long-term contracts, while tenants supply their own computing hardware. Protecting development margins and securing power availability will determine how effectively the business compounds value over the next decade.

Three takeaways from the call stand out for long-term investors.

Cipher Digital's contracted campuses and most of its pipeline sit inside ERCOT, the Texas grid operator. Management argued that data center regulation is evolving nationwide, and that sites elsewhere often face longer waits for power and steep utility deposits. Final ERCOT classifications for several pipeline sites, including the 1-gigawatt Colchis campus, are expected in December.

To avoid cost overruns in long-term colocation leases, in which the company provides turnkey facility space rather than buying expensive microchips directly, management now completes 30% to 60% of the site design before signing agreements. Securing equipment early insulates projects from supply chain bottlenecks and rising construction costs. Change orders at Cipher's first campuses drove up costs, and this is the resulting adjustment.

Grid connection delays can stall new data center developments for years. Cipher Digital commenced development of natural gas lateral pipelines to supply on-site power generation across multiple sites, which could create a faster path to commercialization for high-demand tenants.

Demand for large-scale compute capacity is expanding, allowing the company to prioritize long-term lease terms.

Project financing remains in place through completion, giving the parent's balance sheet flexibility to execute on its pipeline without rushing lease agreements.

The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Bitcoin. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3078. Docusign vs. Okta: Which Tech Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Docusign trades at a significant valuation discount, but Okta's double-digit growth and stronger balance sheet metrics may justify the premium investors are paying.

As digital workflows become standard, many investors are choosing between the leader in electronic signatures and the titan of identity security. Here is how Docusign (DOCU -1.58%) and Okta (OKTA +2.60%) compare today.

Docusign focuses on automating agreement workflows through its Intelligent Agreement Management platform. Okta operates as a leader in identity security, ensuring only authorized users access corporate systems. While both provide critical cloud services, their growth profiles and market valuations offer distinct paths for investors looking ahead to 2027.

Docusign provides cloud-based software that simplifies the way organizations prepare, sign, and manage agreements. According to its latest annual report, filed for the period ending in early 2026, its Intelligent Agreement Management platform serves over 1.8 million customers globally. The company relies on deep integrations with partners like Salesforce (CRM -1.76%), Microsoft (MSFT +2.73%), and SAP (SAP -1.42%) to drive its product adoption.

In fiscal 2026, revenue reached nearly $3.2 billion, representing growth of approximately 8.2% over the prior year. This steady expansion is supported by a broad international presence in over 180 countries. The company reported net income of roughly $309.1 million during this period, showing continued profitability even as it invests in new artificial intelligence agreement features to remain competitive.

Docusign maintains a healthy balance sheet with a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.1, which measures total debt against shareholder equity. As of its January 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is 0.7, which compares a company's short-term assets to its short-term liabilities. Free cash flow, which is cash from operations minus capital expenditures, reached approximately $1.1 billion. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 53.4% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation, as SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Okta provides identity and access management solutions to more than 20,000 customers globally, including large Fortune 50 enterprises and government agencies. Its latest annual report highlights a land-and-expand sales model used to increase its footprint among tech stocks. It manages an extensive integration network featuring over 7,000 connections with various cloud and mobile applications.

In fiscal 2026, revenue reached nearly $2.9 billion, representing approximately 11.8% growth over the previous year. This double-digit revenue increase remains higher than that of its e-signature peer, reflecting strong demand for cloud-based identity security. The company reported net income of roughly $235 million, a significant improvement from the fiscal year ended in 2024, when it reported a net loss.

Okta maintains a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.1, indicating its total debt is low relative to its shareholder equity. As of its January 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is 1.4, suggesting the company has ample short-term assets to cover its upcoming liabilities. Free cash flow for the period was approximately $863.0 million. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 61.5% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation, as SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Docusign faces intense competition, particularly from Adobe (ADBE -2.12%), which offers a rival document platform. Potential disruptions to the business model could arise from rapid advancements in artificial intelligence. Furthermore, the company deals with long and unpredictable enterprise sales cycles that make forecasting future revenue difficult. It also relies on third-party cloud service providers for infrastructure, which presents operational risks.

Docusign appears cheaper than Okta when comparing their forward P/E and P/S ratios.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

One key difference between Okta and Docusign is how important their offerings are to keeping an organization running. I have personally used Docusign in the past, but I've also used other e-signature platforms that weren't all that different. They are all alike enough that I couldn't even tell you the specific software names. So there is a question in my mind of whether Docusign customers could simply substitute some other platform that's just as good (or close enough).

Okta, on the other hand, provides critical software to maintain organizational security and identity management. It's more important than ever for companies to lock down data for reputational, regulatory, and legal reasons. I think this makes Okta's products and services stickier than Docusign.

Okta stock has handily outperformed Docusign and the S&P 500 over the past decade or so. Okta's valuation is considerably higher than Docusign's, but I think the chart above indicates why investors are willing to pay that premium. Okta looks more attractive to me.

Both cards are useful for American Airlines flyers, but one offers more flexibility.

Airline miles, hotel points, travel insurance

This page includes information about these cards, currently unavailable on NerdWallet. The information has been collected by NerdWallet and has not been provided or reviewed by the card issuer.

Both the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard®  and Citi Strata Premier® Card can help you rack up AAdvantage miles. The former earns them directly; the latter transfers points to American Airlines at a 1:1 ratio. So which one is better for American Airlines flyers?

For earning rewards, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the winner. Although it's not an American Airlines credit card, it can make it easier to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases with its higher earning rates and the option to transfer ThankYou points to American.

Still, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® can help you achieve AAdvantage elite status and offers perks when flying American Airlines, including a free checked bag and preferred boarding. In 2027, the annual fee will increase, but the card will add new bonus categories and other benefits.

The Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better fit for travelers who want to earn American Airlines miles without committing all their rewards to a single airline program.

Although the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® is an American Airlines credit card, the Citi Strata Premier® Card can be a faster way to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases.

That's because Citi Strata Premier® Card holders can transfer ThankYou points to American Airlines AAdvantage — and several other loyalty programs — at a 1:1 ratio.

Currently, the Citi Strata Premier® Card offers more rewards than the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® in every bonus category. On Jan. 24, 2027, when the benefits on the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® change, that will change — but only in a couple of narrowly defined categories: select streaming services and eligible Rides and Rails purchases, which include taxis, rideshares, subways and trains. Unless those categories make up a significant portion of your spending, the Citi Strata Premier® Card will likely yield more points overall, even if your ultimate goal is American Airlines miles.

» Learn more: 11 Benefits of the Citi Strata Premier® Card

The Citi Strata Premier® Card also offers a $100 annual hotel benefit when you book a single hotel stay of $500 or more, excluding taxes and fees, through Citi Travel. Using the full benefit can more than offset the card’s annual fee, and hotel bookings through Citi Travel earn 10 ThankYou points per dollar.

Just keep in mind that booking through Citi Travel may mean giving up hotel loyalty program benefits you would receive by booking directly. Whether the $100 hotel perk offers enough value to justify booking through Citi Travel will depend on the property, the rates available and how much you value those hotel loyalty benefits.

» Learn more: Is the Citi Strata Premier® Card worth its annual fee?

One of the biggest advantages of the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the ability to earn ThankYou points, which can be transferred to several airline and hotel loyalty programs. If you want the option to transfer points to American Airlines but also see value in the card’s other airline and hotel partners, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better choice.

Although you can transfer points from the Citi Strata Premier® Card to American Airlines, you won’t get any Loyalty Points.

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® is a strong choice if you want American Airlines benefits. Keep in mind that the card is getting revamped in 2027 with a higher annual fee and new benefits. See NerdWallet's coverage for more details.

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® comes with several benefits that can significantly offset the annual fee:

Free checked bag: Get a free first checked bag on domestic American Airlines itineraries for the primary cardholder and up to four companions traveling on the same reservation. If you check bags with American, this benefit alone could make the card worth getting.

Preferred boarding: The primary cardholder and up to four companions on the same reservation receive preferred boarding.

American Airlines Vacations discount: Save 10% on eligible American Airlines Vacations packages.

Inflight discount: 25% off inflight food and beverage purchases. This perk is being replaced on Jan. 24, 2027, with an inflight statement credit.

$125 flight discount: Spend at least $20,000 during your card membership year and renew the card to receive a $125 American Airlines flight discount. This perk is also changing in 2027; see below.

Starting Jan. 24, 2027, the card will offer:

Inflight statement credits: Receive up to $100 back in statement credits annually on inflight purchases. This replaces the old 25% off inflight food and drink purchases.

$150 flight discount: Spend at least $20,000 during your card membership year and renew the card to receive a $150 American Airlines flight discount. This replaces the $125 flight discount; the spending requirement is the same.

The inflight statement credits, in particular, will be easy to maximize and could potentially offset a meaningful portion of the card’s annual fee. Combined with the ability to earn Loyalty Points toward elite status and the card’s other perks, that can make the card more valuable for American’s flyers.

» Learn more: Is an American Airlines credit card worth it?

The Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® offers Turo credits. You receive up to $180 annually in statement credits on eligible Turo rentals, with up to $30 back per eligible rental.

On Jan. 24, 2027, the card will add several more perks:

Walmart+: Primary cardholders receive a complimentary annual Walmart+ membership, valued at $98.

Global Entry or TSA PreCheck: Receive up to a $120 application fee credit every four years.

FOX One: Get 50% off an annual FOX One subscription.

Travel protections: Get trip delay protection, trip cancellation and interruption protection, worldwide car rental insurance and lost baggage protection.

It’s unusual to see a Global Entry or TSA PreCheck credit on a card with an annual fee under $200. When that perk kicks in, the card could become a good option for travelers who prioritize AAdvantage miles but also want a credit card that fully reimburses the application fee for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck.

» Learn more: American Airlines credit card benefits guide

Cardholders receive 1 Loyalty Point for every eligible AAdvantage mile earned from purchases. That means everyday spending can move you closer to AAdvantage elite status, in addition to earning miles you can redeem for flights. American’s entry-level elite status requires 40,000 Loyalty Points, so putting significant spending on the card can help you reach that threshold.

If you want to earn rewards on everyday spending and have the flexibility to move those points to American Airlines — and you don’t need the airline-specific benefits — the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the best choice.

That said, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® makes more sense for frequent American Airlines flyers who can take advantage of benefits such as a free checked bag and preferred boarding, or for travelers who want to earn Loyalty Points toward American Airlines elite status through credit card spending.
