#3074. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3066. 5 Dividend Stocks to Buy in 2027 and Hold for the Next 5 Years

Dividend stocks' total returns have historically beaten dividend non-payers by a wide margin.

S&P 500 companies that grew or started paying dividends delivered total returns of about 10.2% a year from 1973 through 2025, versus about 4.2% for dividend non-payers, according to Hartford Funds and Ned Davis Research. That's a strong argument for building a 2027 portfolio around stocks that are reliable dividend growers.

Coca-Cola (KO -1.07%), Colgate-Palmolive (CL -1.86%), Procter & Gamble (PG -0.92%), PepsiCo (PEP -2.24%), and Target (TGT -2.51%) have each raised their payouts for over 50 straight years, through recessions, inflation spikes, the 2008 financial crisis, and the pandemic. Here's why these dividend stocks are worth buying in 2027 and holding for the next five years (or more), whatever the market brings.

Coca-Cola has raised its dividend annually for 64 straight years, and at the current share price, it yields about 2.2% based on the quarterly payment of $0.53 per share. Management has hiked the dividend by an average of about 4.5% per year over the past five years. Importantly, over the past year, it distributed roughly 78% of free cash flow -- the cash left after running and reinvesting in the business. That leaves the company plenty of breathing room to further increase the dividend even if its profits or free cash flow don't grow in a given year.

Its bottling partners sell Coca-Cola, Sprite, and Fanta across developed and emerging markets. Demand for the company's many beverage brands remains fairly consistent regardless of the economy. Unit sales volume grew 5% year over year in the third quarter -- a strong result in an environment where affordability has been a key concern for consumers.

CEO Henrique Braun said the company's "confidence in the long-term opportunity remains unchanged." Analysts expect its earnings to grow by roughly 7% a year over the next five years, which should lead to more dividend increases.

Colgate-Palmolive has raised its dividend annually for more than 60 straight years, and at the current share price, it yields about 2.2% based on the quarterly payment of $0.53 per share. Management has only hiked the dividend by an average of about 3% per year over the past five years, but it's currently paying out just 44% of trailing-12-month free cash flow, leaving it with plenty of room to deliver larger raises from here.

The consumer products company is the world's largest seller of toothpaste, with about 41% of the global market share. It also owns a host of popular soap brands, household cleansers, deodorants, and the Hill's pet food brand. All are the sorts of products that people use daily, and that households keep buying even when budgets tighten. In the third quarter, its organic sales growth (which excludes the impacts of currency swings and deals) was nearly 4% year over year.

In 2025, the company laid out a new strategic growth and productivity program. On its most recent earnings call last month, CEO Noel Wallace said the goal is "to emerge with a stronger business and an even more resilient business moving forward." Analysts expect about 5% annual earnings growth over the next five years.

P&G has paid a dividend for 136 years and raised it for 70 straight years. It yields about 2.7% based on the quarterly payment of $1.0885 per share. Management has boosted the dividend by an average of about 5% per year over the past five years, and the dividend accounted for about 67% of trailing free cash flow.

Households will need products like Tide and Pampers in good times and bad. P&G holds over 35% of the global fabric care market where it competes. Across all product categories, the company's organic sales rose by 4% last quarter.

CFO Andre Schulten said P&G has "the brands, the capability, and most importantly, the people required to win." Analysts expect about 5% annual earnings growth over the next five years.

PepsiCo has raised its dividend for 54 straight years and offers the highest yield among these five stocks, at about 4.3%, based on the quarterly payment of $1.48 per share. Management increased the payout by an average of nearly 7% a year over the past five years, and the company paid out about 75% of trailing free cash flow.

PepsiCo owns snack brands such as Lay's and Doritos as well as popular beverages such as Gatorade and Pepsi. That diversified brand portfolio can give some stability to sales when one category slows. Third-quarter organic revenue grew about 3% year over year, its fastest pace since late 2023, and CEO Ramon Laguarta said growth is "on its way to the long-term 4% that we want to get to."

Analysts expect about 5% annual earnings growth over the next five years. Paired with its high yield, that could add up to solid returns for shareholders.

Target has paid a dividend every quarter since 1967, and CFO Jim Lee said the retail giant aims to "build on our more than 50-year record of annual increases." At the current share price, it yields nearly 3% based on the quarterly payment of $1.16 per share. Management has increased the payout by about 11% per year over the past five years, and the company distributed less than half of free cash flow over the past year.

Target sells everyday essentials that keep shoppers coming back. It experienced weaker sales during the high-inflation environment in recent years, but it has proven resilient over the last several decades. During its fiscal 2026 second quarter, which ended Aug. 1, comparable sales rose nearly 4% year over year, driven mostly by higher store traffic.

Analysts expect about 6% annual earnings growth over the next five years, which should help it keep the dividend growing.

John Ballard has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Colgate-Palmolive and Target. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3060. If You Invested $10,000 in Intel Stock at Its Dot-Com-Era Peak, Here's How Much You'd Have Today

Intel stock recently plunged 29% in a single day, marking its worst day in over 52 years.

In the late 1990s, Intel (INTC +0.97%) was one of the most important companies in the technology industry. Its processors powered millions of personal computers, and its dominance in semiconductor manufacturing made it a compelling choice for investors looking to capitalize on the internet revolution.

On Aug. 31, 2000, Intel stock closed at a then-record $74.88. More than 26 years later, that optimism has delivered surprisingly disappointing results. As of the closing bell on Nov. 2, Intel shares traded at $59.97. This means a $10,000 investment made at its peak during the dot-com boom would be worth approximately $8,009 today, excluding dividends.

Intel's struggles have become apparent throughout the artificial intelligence (AI) revolution. Recently, shares collapsed nearly 29% on Oct. 30 following the company's third-quarter earnings report.

What went wrong with this former semiconductor powerhouse, and could its latest sell-off represent a lucrative opportunity for investors?

Intel's third-quarter earnings report initially looked encouraging. Revenue increased 21% year over year to $16.5 billion, while adjusted earnings per share (EPS) reached $0.52, more than doubling from the prior-year period.

Its Data Center and AI (DCAI) segment was particularly impressive, growing 78% year over year to $7.3 billion in revenue. Demand for Intel's Xeon server processors has strengthened as AI developers expand computing infrastructure to support more sophisticated applications across agentic AI workloads.

However, the company's PC business is facing challenges. Revenue from its Client Computing and Physical AI (CCPG) segment declined 5% to $8.1 billion. Even more concerning was management's outlook. Intel expects fourth-quarter revenue between $15 billion and $16 billion, below the $17 billion target Wall Street had anticipated. Adjusted earnings guidance of $0.25 also disappointed investors.

Intel's sharp sell-off shows that investors are reassessing how quickly the company's turnaround can realistically translate into sustainable growth across the entire business.

For decades, Intel enjoyed competitive advantages in processor design and manufacturing. However, several strategic missteps gradually weakened its position.

Intel struggled to capitalize on the smartphone revolution. This allowed competitors to develop processors using alternative chip architectures. Meanwhile, manufacturing delays enabled Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing to establish itself as the industry's leading foundry for advanced chips.

These setbacks have become particularly noticeable as demand for AI infrastructure accelerates. Unlike Nvidia, whose graphics processing units are essential for training and deploying AI models, Intel remains heavily dependent on traditional central processing units (CPUs).

Indeed, CPUs remain critical components of accelerated computing systems, and Intel's latest data center results underscore that AI is driving meaningful demand for its products. With that said, the company hasn't captured the same economic opportunity as Nvidia, which stitches together accelerators with networking technology, CUDA software, and a developer ecosystem through an extensive roster of open-source models.

Intel's manufacturing strategy introduces another headache. The company is investing heavily in its foundry business in hopes of manufacturing advanced semiconductors for outside customers. Unfortunately, this transformation remains costly.

Intel Foundry generated $6.7 billion in revenue during the third quarter but posted an operating loss of $1.9 billion. In addition, most of the company's foundry revenue comes from internal products rather than external customers.

Intel isn't necessarily a lost cause. The company's latest financial performance indicates strong demand for server processors. Intel also benefits from the strategic importance of domestic semiconductor manufacturing, which has attracted government support.

Nevertheless, the company's long-term performance offers an important warning. The distinction between a cheap stock and an attractive investment is particularly relevant today in the AI era. Following its latest sell-off, Intel might look inexpensive relative to recent trading levels, but its valuation needs to be considered alongside manufacturing losses, ongoing capital expenditures (capex), the competitive landscape, and uncertain earnings growth.

For now, I see Intel as a speculative turnaround rather than a dependable long-term compounder. While its data center business is encouraging, the company still needs to prove its foundry investments will generate sustainable profitability.

Investors seeking exposure to semiconductor stocks have several alternatives with greater durability. Overall, Intel's disappointing performance since 2000 shows why investors should regularly reassess their investment theses. The company has opportunities to recover, but buying shares simply because they've fallen sharply risks confusing a potential bargain with a value trap.

Adam Spatacco has positions in Nvidia. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Intel, Nvidia, and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3059. The Signals That Tell Founders It's Time To Expand Globally (Not Just Go After More Local Market Share)

David Natroshvili is founder & CEO of SPRIBE, bringing cross-industry leadership to global gaming, regulation and product strategy.

Most founders can recognize when their local market starts to feel smaller than their ambitions. Fewer can point to actual evidence that going global is the right call, rather than a reaction to boredom or ego.

At my company, SPRIBE, the evidence showed up as data we could have ignored, but mostly didn’t. Today, SPRIBE operates in more than 60 regulated markets. Getting there took a series of small decisions to trust what the numbers were already showing us, long before any of it looked like a deliberate strategy.​​​

Aviator, our flagship product, has found some of its strongest audiences in Latin America, Africa and Eastern Europe, regions shaped by mobile-first habits and a strong appetite for social, real-time entertainment. Regional traction like that deserves different treatment than growth in a market that is targeted deliberately. It suggests the product itself, not marketing spend, is doing the work of finding an audience.

For a leadership team deciding where to invest next, that distinction matters. Growth you engineered proves your playbook works somewhere. Growth you didn’t have to engineer proves your product works in more places than your playbook accounted for.​

Before I put real budget behind a region, I look for three things to hold up together. Retention matters more than the initial spike, so I want to see players coming back on their own in the weeks after, not just showing up once out of curiosity. I want to see the growth holding without paid acquisition or localization work on our end, since that is what separates a market discovering a product on its own from a market we simply haven’t tried yet. And I want it to survive more than one quarter, because a short run can be a promotional bump or a single creator moment as easily as it can be a shift in the market. ​

Global partnerships tend to get evaluated on brand recognition, and not enough on geography. Our partnership with AC Milan is a useful example of the gap. The obvious value of teaming up with a football club that size sits in Europe, where the club’s following is already massive.​

For SPRIBE, the more useful value sits in Asia, a market where our brand and AC Milan’s fanbase have far less overlap today, and where the partnership gives us a credible way into a region we hadn’t built for organically. A partnership’s real worth, for a company thinking about where to expand next, is measured by the markets it opens rather than the ones it simply reinforces.​

Getting to that answer takes asking a few unglamorous questions before anything gets signed. Where does the partner already have trust that we don’t, and does that trust actually transfer to a product like ours, or only to their own brand? Do they have real distribution in the region, whether that’s media relationships, retail presence or a local commercial team, or is it just recognition with no way to reach anyone? Can they introduce us to the local operators, regulators or partners we would otherwise spend a year finding on our own?

A partnership that answers even one of those questions well is probably worth pursuing. One that can only point to audience size is a sponsorship, not an expansion strategy.​​

The clearest signal is often the simplest: A growth-rate gap between regions wide enough that treating every market the same way stops making sense.

In 2025, our business in Asia doubled while other regions grew at a steadier pace. That gap is a large part of why India and Southeast Asia now sit alongside Latin America at the top of our growth priorities for 2026, alongside continued investment across existing markets in Europe and Africa. The numbers made that case well before any strategy memo did.​

A single strong year isn’t enough to reallocate resources on its own, so before treating a gap like that as a mandate, I ask what actually drove it. If the growth traces back to one product feature, one campaign or one operator relationship, that’s narrower evidence than a genuine market shift, and it usually calls for testing further before committing headcount. If growth is broad across products, channels and player segments instead, that’s a different kind of evidence, one that justifies moving people and budget rather than waiting for a second data point. Understanding which one you’re looking at takes talking to the people closest to that market, not just reading a dashboard.​

For leaders weighing whether it’s time to expand beyond a strong local position, I’d suggest auditing for these three kinds of evidence before committing resources: traction you didn’t create, partnerships whose real value lies in the markets they open rather than the ones they reinforce, and growth-rate gaps large enough to reorder your priorities.

Ambition is necessary to act on a signal. It shouldn’t be mistaken for the signal itself.​

The most common mistake I see leaders make is treating any one of these signals as sufficient by itself. A spike in a new region gets mistaken for demand. A recognizable partner’s name gets mistaken for market access. One good year gets mistaken for a trend. Each of those signals only earns your investment once you’ve tested whether it survives contact with reality, which is a slower and far less exciting process than simply announcing an expansion.​

Ambition is necessary to act on a signal. It shouldn’t be mistaken for the signal itself. The lesson I’ve taken from building SPRIBE across more than 60 markets is that patience with evidence beats the size of the ambition behind it, every time.

#3058. This Is How the Stock Market Typically Performs After Midterm Elections

The next six months tend to be a very good period for stock market returns.

The midterm elections are over. But as far as the stock market goes, Tuesday's voting results probably don't matter much, aside from some elevated market volatility.

But there's some good news about the midterms, and it has nothing to do with which party came out on top. Historically, the six-month period following midterm elections -- from November of the midterm election year to the following April -- is the best in the four-year presidential cycle for the S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%). That's according to the Stock Trader's Almanac, which tracks market returns closely.

And that means this is a good time for the stock market.

The Four-Year Presidential Election Cycle Theory holds that the stock market has a relatively predictable pattern surrounding presidential terms. In the first two years of office, the new president focuses on top priorities, such as healthcare and education, and the market's performance is below average.

In the third year -- or, really, beginning late in the second year, around the time of midterm elections -- presidents often focus on stimulating the economy. As a result, the market tends to outperform that year. Then, in the fourth year, election anxiety returns, and market gains fall again.

Here are the numbers over the period from 1950 to 2023: In year one of the cycle, the S&P 500 index gains 7.9% on average; in year two, that falls to 4.6%. Year three, the best year of the cycle, sees a 17.2% average gain. And in year four, the next presidential election year, the market returns about 7.3%. For comparison, the average annual return for the S&P 500 has been about 10% since 1957.

These are averages, of course, but the general pattern has held up well over time. And the six months following midterms are a particularly good time for stocks.

Depending on the outcome of this week's midterms, some investors might be tempted to overhaul their portfolios or even move to the safety of bonds or other less risky asset classes. But they should resist the temptation. An old investing pro I worked with for years had a few ironclad rules about investing, and one of them is to never base investing decisions on politics.

So, while we may see some heightened volatility over the next few months as the market adjusts to the new political arrangement, whatever it is, keep in mind that getting out of the market now could mean you'll miss some of the best gains it has to offer.

Matthew Benjamin has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3055. Meet the Only "Magnificent Seven" Stock That's Outperforming the S&P 500 and Nasdaq This Year (Hint: It's Not Nvidia)

Apple is hovering around an all-time high despite a broader sell-off in tech stocks.

The "Magnificent Seven" was popularized in 2023 by Bank of America analyst Michael Hartnett as a term for seven rapidly appreciating tech-focused growth companies. In 2023, Apple (AAPL -1.90%), Nvidia (NVDA +2.29%), Microsoft (MSFT +2.73%), Alphabet (GOOG +0.08%) (GOOGL +0.37%), Amazon (AMZN +2.06%), Meta Platforms (META +2.36%), and Tesla (TSLA -1.32%) were instrumental in driving the Nasdaq Composite's (^IXIC +0.83%) monster 43.4% gain -- with all seven companies posting even bigger returns.

Big gains followed in 2024, with every Magnificent Seven stock except for Microsoft outperforming the Nasdaq and S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%). But in 2025, only Nvidia and Alphabet outperformed both indexes. And after last week's sell-off, the only Magnificent Seven stock left standing above the indexes is Apple, which surpassed Nvidia as the world's most valuable company on Oct. 30.

Here's what separates Apple from its Magnificent Seven stock peers, and if the tech stock is a buy now.

Apple is up 26.8% year-to-date, outpacing the major indexes, the broader tech sector, and its Magnificent Seven peers.

Apple is unique because artificial intelligence (AI) capital expenditures (capex) don't make or break its investment thesis. By comparison, Amazon, Alphabet, Microsoft, Meta Platforms, and Tesla continue to ramp up their AI spending. And although Nvidia has low capex, its major customers are the hyperscalers, so it needs them to see a return on their chip and associated AI infrastructure investments to justify future spending.

AAPL Free Cash Flow (Quarterly) data by YCharts

At the beginning of the AI ramp-up, cash-cow companies like Amazon and Alphabet were able to support higher spending with free cash flow (FCF) alone. But spending has grown so massive that Amazon, Alphabet, and Meta Platforms are now FCF-negative.

Alphabet is taking on debt, diluting shareholders by issuing stock, and is guiding for even higher spending in 2027. Investors are losing patience, with Alphabet experiencing a record $505 billion market cap plunge in a single session and plummeting around a 52-week low. Apple is the exact opposite narrative.

Apple's balance sheet is in impeccable shape. It continues to generate gobs of FCF that it can use to reinvest in the business or return to shareholders through buybacks and dividends.

Apple isn't spending record capex to build data centers. Rather, its focus is on providing products that allow consumers and enterprises to interact with AI, such as the first touchscreen MacBook Pro, its first foldable iPhone Duo, its smart home system HomeView, smart glasses, an AI-powered pendant, and more. Apple is also directly passing along higher memory chip costs to customers through price increases. To help bridge the affordability gap, it is offering a new leasing program facilitated by Klarna, in addition to the multi-year service contracts already offered by carriers.

Apple doesn't need to spend a lot on AI capex because new product updates and AI features on its devices are manageable costs. Developers depend on Apple App Store downloads to gain traction, all the while funneling more revenue to Apple in fees.

What makes Apple particularly appealing is that it is exposed to virtually all of the benefits of AI adoption with very few of the cons. Whereas frontier models like Anthropic's Claude, OpenAI's ChatGPT, and Alphabet's Gemini are all competing for user engagement. Amazon, Microsoft, and Alphabet are racing to attract cloud spend. By comparison, Apple is a catch-all way to bet on AI innovation and productivity improvements.

Outside of AI, Apple has evolved into a modern-day consumer staples company more than a tech stock. Its integrated ecosystem of products and services is essential for many users. And demand has proven incredibly sticky despite inflationary and cost-of-living pressures.

Apple is hovering around an all-time high because AI acts as a cherry on top of its investment thesis rather than the entire sundae. If there were a cyclical slowdown in AI spending or an AI-driven stock market correction, Apple would be one of the best-positioned tech stocks to weather it.

The problem with buying the stock now is that Apple trades at 39.5 times earnings and 36.1 times forward earnings -- which is a significant premium compared to its 10-year median price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 27.6. However, Apple's valuation is digestible for investors who truly view Apple as a recession-proof, consumer staples/tech hybrid. Especially when compared to recession-resistant companies like Walmart (WMT -2.22%) and Costco Wholesale (COST -2.05%), which are even more expensive than Apple. Walmart fetches a 42.8 forward P/E, and Costco's is 44.4.

Investors willing to pay a premium price for a safe stock may want to consider buying Apple, even around an all-time high. But investors who believe that AI spending will eventually pay off for hyperscalers will find stocks like Alphabet and Amazon at far more attractive valuations.

#3053. Cyber Resilience Is A Business Operating Model, Not An IT Recovery Plan

Michael Campbell, CEO at Fusion Risk Management.

Whether a cyberattack or other disruption, such as a supplier factory fire or any number of other kinds of events, originates within your enterprise or your partner ecosystem, its impact can spread rapidly. A single incident can expose hidden dependencies, disrupt critical services and force leaders to make high-stakes decisions in real time.

Evidence of enterprise disruptions’ downstream impacts is everywhere:

• An attack on a medical device company’s Microsoft platform brings operations to a standstill.

• A data breach prompts an edtech business to take its portal out of service, leaving 15,000 schools unable to handle assignments, courses and tests.

• Cyberattacks take out a service on which thousands of car dealerships rely, stalling their businesses and exposing confidential customer information.

• The Korean Leaks exploit allowed access to multiple financial services companies through a managed service provider, creating havoc.

And now, enterprise adoption of AI has the potential to dramatically increase the frequency and scale of cyberattacks, while AI model drift and explainability gaps further expand enterprise risk.

A growing number of organizations are taking a broader view beyond only threat monitoring, narrow testing and recovery plans by considering everything that can create vulnerabilities and shifting the focus from recovering IT systems to minimizing business loss.

Meanwhile, enterprises are pushing CISOs to take on responsibility for business continuity management and disaster recovery. But a Gartner analyst cautions that CISOs with this new remit “should lead through influence” and “center on cyber resilience, reset board expectations, and coordinate with the CIO, CRO and CDAO to scale sustainably.”

Despite emerging organizational mandates, shifting roles and good analyst advice, organizations lack living models of their critical business services and dependencies that create vulnerabilities. They still need visibility into how those risks could impact their finances and long-term success. And although they may have plans on paper, most haven’t established the operating capabilities they need to build true enterprise resilience and protect their businesses.​

CISOs should convene the leaders who own security, IT disaster recovery, business continuity, operations, finance and the affected business services. Together, they should document who can authorize recovery, set service priorities, accept temporary workarounds and escalate conflicts. A short decision-rights matrix is more useful than a broad mandate because it tells teams who decides when recovery conditions change.

CEOs and boards should assign one executive sponsor for cross-functional recovery governance, require regular reviews of the organization’s most critical services and dependencies, and ask leaders to test plausible but severe disruption scenarios specific to their business. Their role isn’t to direct technical recovery. It’s to ensure the enterprise has the authority, investment and accountability to make trade-offs when normal operations can’t immediately resume.

This executive-level ownership is critical. Many of the preparations made today are still highly siloed. The need to get them to work together prior to a crisis is a big surprise to many. It needs to be tackled up front.

For each critical service, identify its dependencies, tolerance for disruption and the minimum conditions for safe recovery. Use a tabletop exercise to test whether leaders have the information and authority to set recovery priorities as conditions change. Gaps in the exercise reveal the information, governance or plans that need work before a real event.

Cyberattacks and AI-related risks get a lot of attention, but even a relatively unremarkable incident can have notable downstream consequences. Here’s an example: Two manufacturers relied on the same supplier, whose factory caught fire.

One had already mapped its supply chain dependencies. Within days, it identified an alternate source, re-sequenced its production lines and contained what could have been a $250 million revenue hit to $15 million.

The other was still working out which products touched that supplier a month later. By the time it found an alternative, the damage led to significant lost revenue and customer defections so severe the company was eventually acquired.

Both organizations had the same supplier exposure. Their outcomes reflected the decision model each had established before the fire.

#3051. When Thinking Slow Becomes Instant

Fabio Moioli, Executive Search & Leadership Advisory at Spencer Stuart. Passionate about artificial and human intelligences.

In an earlier article, I went back to Adam Grant’s Think Again and asked whether AI could become part of a challenge network. In this new article, I want to move one step earlier. Before we can change our minds, we first have to form them.

That brought me back to Daniel Kahneman’s Thinking, Fast and Slow. The book made me question what happens to human judgment when thinking is no longer something that happens only inside the human mind.

Kahneman’s book gave us some powerful vocabulary to work with when it comes to thinking modes. System 1 is quick, associative and intuitive; System 2 is slower, requires more effort and is capable of checking what intuition proposes. But Kahneman’s argument was never that System 2 is simply rational and System 1 irrational. Intuition can embody genuine expertise, while deliberate reasoning can become a lawyer hired to defend a conclusion reached earlier.

What matters is whether confidence is justified, whether the evidence is sufficient and whether the environment gives intuition a chance to learn. AI complicates all three. It is not a “System 3.” It is an external cognitive actor that can reinforce System 1, assist System 2, frame the problem for both and increasingly do so before we have formed independent judgment.

Consider the concept of anchoring. A CEO might believe an acquisition feels right and ask AI to develop a strategic case. Seconds later, the intuition has synergies, market logic, risks and mitigations. Nothing has necessarily gone wrong, but a hunch has acquired the appearance of analysis.

But AI has not created the anchor; it has professionalized it. Reverse the sequence and another problem appears. Ask AI before forming a view, and its first interpretation may become the anchor around which subsequent human thought revolves. We used to ask, “What is influencing my judgment?” But now we ask, “Who is anchoring whom?”

The concept of availability changes in a similar way. What comes easily to mind may increasingly be what comes easily to screen. Ask AI for the principal risks, and those risks become the agenda; what it fails to surface may never enter the discussion. I think of this as synthetic availability.

The concept of framing also moves upstream, into the prompt itself. “How should we implement this restructuring?” and “Under what circumstances should we abandon it?” may produce excellent answers to very different problems. AI can reason brilliantly inside the territory we give it without ever telling us that we chose the wrong territory. The prompt is therefore not merely an instruction. It is already an act of judgment.

Kahneman’s “What You See Is All There Is” concept may be even more consequential in this context. Generative AI is an extraordinary machine for producing coherence. Give it fragments, and it connects them; give it incomplete evidence, and it can still produce a satisfying narrative.

The danger is not simply hallucination. Obvious errors can be challenged. The harder problem is a very good answer constructed from an incomplete representation of reality. The better AI becomes at connecting the dots, the easier it may become to forget that some important dots were never there. Coherence can then create what I would call borrowed confidence: Our conviction increases because the explanation became more fluent, not because the evidence became stronger.

This is also where attribute substitution becomes harder to notice. “Is this a good strategy?” quietly becomes “Can AI produce a convincing strategic case for this strategy?” “Will this person succeed as CEO here?” becomes “Can we produce a sophisticated assessment of this person?”

Neither of these is the question. AI gives us so much analytical surface area that the existence of analysis can begin to feel like proof that thinking has occurred. Yet, the same technology can do the opposite. AI can industrialize confirmation bias, but it can also industrialize challenge. The difference often lies not in the model, but in the cognitive posture with which we use it.

The concept of prospect theory changes, too. AI can generate vivid futures: 10 ways a strategy could fail, 10 geopolitical scenarios or 10 paths to disruption. This can improve risk management, but vividness is not probability. A richly narrated possibility feels more real. I think of this as possibility inflation: When hypothetical futures become effortless to generate, we may overweight the ones that are easiest to imagine. Leaders may therefore need fewer scenarios and better calibration.

Even dissent can become deceptive. Ask AI to challenge your strategy, and it will oblige, but if it inherited your framing, assumptions and language, the disagreement may remain inside your intellectual world. That is challenge theater: the experience of being challenged without sufficient independence of thought. Ask several models, and agreement can create a consensus mirage if they all received the same facts and the same framing. More broadly, organizations may reduce human noise while increasing common errors: Everyone becomes more consistent because everyone is consulting the same cognitive infrastructure. This does not necessarily lead to increased accuracy.

The deeper implication is that bias may increasingly live in the loop, rather than in any single participant. Intuition shapes the prompt, the prompt defines the frame, AI produces a coherent answer, the answer changes what feels available and plausible, and confidence shapes the next prompt. After several iterations, an initial impression has acquired the weight of analysis without any individual step looking unreasonable.

This is why the next discipline may not be prompt engineering, but cognitive sequencing: deciding who thinks first, who frames the problem, when AI is allowed to recommend and which judgments should remain independent.​

Kahneman spent much of his career teaching us that ease, coherence and confidence are not the same thing as truth. AI is poised to make ease, coherence and confidence abundant. That may be one of its greatest gifts and one of its most dangerous illusions. The challenge, then, is not merely to keep humans in the loop. It will be to keep doubt in the loop.​​​

#3048. Steph Curry’s historic streak ends at worst possible time in Warriors thriller

SAN FRANCISCO — Stephen Curry had stepped to the foul line 50 times this season with the same result. Swish. Bottom of the net. Put a point on the board.

So, when he was fouled with the Warriors leading by two and less than six seconds left Monday night against the Suns, the game had to be as good as over … right?

Only after Curry clanked a free throw for the first time this season. Dillon Brooks grabbed the rebound and passed it ahead to Collin Gillespie, who got off a last-second heave from 36 feet.

It was a clean look, albeit an unlikely one, and could have sent the game to overtime. The shot fell short and Curry's ill-timed miss proved insignificant.

Stephen Curry of the Golden State Warriors looks on during a game against the Phoenix Suns on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Chase Center in San Francisco. NBAE via Getty Images

But in a game that he finished with 34 total — his third in a row with 30 or more — it was the one point Curry left on the board that drew the attention.

"I mean, he's just made so many big free throws in his career," coach Steve Kerr said. "Obviously he makes all of those. The law of averages catches up every once in a while."

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry (30) shoots against the Phoenix Suns in an NBA basketball game in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jed Jacobsohn

In addition to being the greatest 3-point shooter in history, it would make sense then that Curry keeps the same standard in a controlled setting, 8 feet, 9 inches closer to the basket. His 91.3% career rate makes him the most efficient foul shooter of all time.

Curry is also responsible for two of the NBA's four longest stretches of success from the stripe to begin a season. With his streak, he was closing in on himself (52 in a row in 2017-18) for third place and Steve Nash (53 in 2007-08) for second for the most foul shots without a miss.

It would have taken some time to snatch the record away from Jose Calderon (84 in 2008-09).

But with a chance to put the game on ice and move into a tie for third place, for the first time in a competitive game in 204 days, Curry's shot clanked off the rim, into the hands of Brooks.

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry shoots against Phoenix Suns guard Devin Booker and center Khaman Maluach in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jed Jacobsohn

Fortunately for the Warriors, the 11 free throws, five 3s and four shots inside the arc that did find the bottom of the net proved to be just enough in the 116-113 win, their fifth in a row.

While Curry's free throw streak came to an end at 51, he also extended another with his 34-point performance. At age 38 and 233 days, only two older players in NBA history have dropped 30 three games in a row: LeBron James and Michael Jordan.

One miss in a vast ocean of splashes, of course, did nothing to shake Curry's confidence.

"I'm shocked," Curry smirked, "when I miss anytime."

#3046. Why Human Judgment Still Decides Whether Your AI Agents Are Safe

Petar Besalev is the EVP of Cybersecurity and Compliance Services at A-LIGN.

​AI agents are moving out of pilot programs and into production, taking on decisions in procurement, security and customer operations. As that shift accelerates, there is an irony at the center of it: The standards built to govern these systems still depend on humans to validate them.​

The industry has spent the last year asking whether AI will replace compliance professionals. But that’s the wrong question. The right question is who’s accountable when the AI gets it wrong. And right now, the answer is still a human being.​

Not long ago, most conversations in this industry started and ended with “we have AI.” Today, the more useful framing is: “We have people with experience and professional judgment, supported by technology.”​

That shift reflects where the technology actually is, not where the hype says it should be. AI is a genuine enablement tool, making assessments faster and more efficient while removing a meaningful amount of manual work. But efficiency and judgment are not the same thing, and leaders who treat them as interchangeable are setting themselves up for a hard lesson.​

Regulated industries already have a clear answer for what happens when something goes wrong: Someone signs their name to it.​

Under frameworks like the Cybersecurity Maturity Model Certification (CMMC), the affirming official personally attests that an organization’s systems meet the required standard. That signature carries liability under the False Claims Act. No AI system can assume that liability. Only a person can.​

Leaders should treat that as the test for any process they’re automating. If something goes wrong, who is exposed? And can that exposure sit with a model? In almost every regulated context today, the answer is no.​

New technology tends to move faster than the frameworks meant to govern it, and AI governance is no exception. There is no single mandate for AI compliance yet, only an emerging patchwork that includes the NIST AI Risk Management Framework, ISO 42001, the EU AI Act and newer standards built specifically for AI agents.​

That gap can look like a weakness in the system, but it’s actually the system working as intended, since regulators tend to wait for evidence before they mandate trust. But that doesn’t mean the market will wait.

According to recent industry survey data, 80% of companies using AI said customers are already asking about their AI risk management practices, yet one-third of organizations still have no AI compliance strategy in place at all. That’s why organizations should be adopting a recognized framework now, before it’s required.​

As agentic AI takes on more autonomous work, from executing transactions to making real-time security decisions, the accountability question only gets more important. An agent can act faster than a person can review its actions, which makes the human role more important.​

The organizations best positioned for this shift are the ones already building governance structures that assume a human owns the outcome, regardless of how autonomous the underlying tooling becomes.​

Those building out governing systems for agentic AI need to make that structure explicit. They must separate independent technical testing on how the agent performs from a distinct governance audit of the policies, data handling and controls around it. That second layer is where human judgment lives. It’s evaluated on its own rather than folded into a single automated check, and it’s increasingly reassessed on a recurring basis, rather than treated as a one-time sign-off, since an agent’s behavior can drift over time.​

The mechanism matters less than the principle: Autonomy in execution should never mean autonomy in accountability.​

As AI governance and certification options multiply, a familiar problem is resurfacing: Not all validation is built the same.​

Risk teams are already rejecting compliance reports that come from firms without the depth or track record to back them up, and that same scrutiny will apply to AI governance reviews. A superficial check against a framework creates false assurance, and false assurance is more dangerous than an honest gap because it hides the risk instead of surfacing it.​

The frameworks gaining traction share a few traits: input from the security and risk leaders who have to live with the results, a regular update cadence rather than sitting static and dozens of specific, testable requirements rather than one broad checkbox.​

As this space matures, the organizations that ask who is validating their AI systems, how rigorously they’re validating and how often they do it will be the ones that hold up under scrutiny.​

This was never a question of people versus technology. It’s people, enabled by technology, which is a very different outcome. AI is a genuine competitive advantage when it’s paired with the judgment to use it responsibly. But the accountability, the liability and the professional judgment that regulators and customers are actually paying for will remain human, and that isn’t changing anytime soon.​​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3045. Cerebras Systems vs. Innodata: Which Tech Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

One of these AI stocks is growing fast but burning cash, while the other is already profitable and cheaper on sales and forward earnings.

The artificial intelligence (AI) boom is shifting from experimental software to massive infrastructure builds. Choosing between hardware pioneer Cerebras Systems (CBRS +1.51%) and data specialist Innodata (INOD +1.40%) depends on your appetite for risk.

Cerebras Systems manufactures giant chips designed to process AI workloads faster than traditional hardware. Meanwhile, Innodata focuses on the fuel for those chips, providing the high-quality data engineering required to train large language models. Both companies play critical roles in the expanding AI ecosystem, but their financial profiles and market positions differ significantly.

Cerebras Systems operates in the competitive semiconductor landscape, where innovation, speed, and efficiency are crucial. The company creates massive wafer-scale engines, which are essentially dinner-plate-sized chips that integrate compute and memory to reduce latency. This specialized hardware supports both AI training and inference, offering these capabilities through physical data centers and flexible cloud services to customers worldwide. Its mission is to accelerate the development of artificial intelligence by providing a faster, more efficient alternative to standard industry processors.

On a trailing-12-month (TTM) basis through the June quarter, revenue reached nearly $681 million, representing a substantial 91% increase over the previous year. The company reported a net loss of $512 million during the same period.

Based on its June 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is 5.8x, indicating the company's ability to cover short-term debts with short-term assets. It carries a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.2x, indicating a small amount of debt relative to shareholders' equity.

TTM free cash flow, which is cash from operations minus capital expenditures, was negative $680 million. This negative cash flow indicates the company is spending more on capital investments than it is generating from its core operations.

Innodata specializes in data engineering and evaluation frameworks that help major technology firms build generative AI systems. The company operates under master service agreements and counts five of the "Magnificent Seven" firms as major clients. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, as one specific customer accounted for nearly 58% of total revenue in 2025. Its strategy focuses on providing the human expertise and data labeling necessary to refine complex AI models.

TTM revenue reached $317 million as of the June quarter, representing a year-over-year increase of 39%. The company reported net income of roughly $47 million for the period, maintaining positive earnings for two consecutive years. Innodata has remained profitable over the last two fiscal years.

As for financial health, the June 2026 balance sheet shows a current ratio of nearly 1.7x, indicating strong short-term liquidity. The debt-to-equity ratio, which measures total debt relative to shareholders' equity, is nearly 0.1x.

TTM free cash flow was approximately $184 million, providing cash for potential reinvestment.

Cerebras Systems faces intense competition in the specialized hardware market, particularly from much larger incumbents with vast research budgets. The company must continually innovate to prove its wafer-scale architecture remains superior to more conventional networking and processing solutions.

Innodata contends with massive customer concentration, making it vulnerable to the budget shifts of just one or two major technology partners. Both Cerebras and Innodata face the same risk in terms of a broad slowdown in AI spending. Any slowdown in global AI infrastructure spending could impact their growth trajectory.

Innodata looks cheaper based on its P/S ratio, measuring sales over the past twelve months, and its Forward P/E relative to future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

I would buy Innodata mainly because it is generating robust revenue growth and free cash flow, while Cerebras is reporting large losses on the bottom line. Analysts expect Innodata's earnings to grow at an annualized rate of 22% in the coming years. That can support its forward P/E.

Innodata's revenue surged 58% year over year in the second quarter, with adjusted operating profit up 92%. It is positioning itself as an AI deployment assurance layer to help enterprises safely use agents. It recently launched an AI Cyber Training Suite to train coding agents and fix issues in a company's software.

I would wait to buy Cerebras until it shows a clearer path to sustainable cash flow growth. It has significant growth potential, with highly efficient, fast computing systems that could compete with traditional GPU-powered systems. Still, the stock is very expensive for a business that has not yet generated consistent profits.

John Ballard has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Innodata. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3044. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3042. The Missing Thread In Product Development

Venkatesh Sankaran is the Founder & CEO of Saguna Consulting Services, turning AI, cloud & digital transformation into business outcomes.

​Sooner or later, many product teams hear the same question from the market: Why didn’t this work? They can explain what happened, but often cannot show which belief, design choice or trade-off led to it. AI can help a team build the wrong product faster, and with even less record of why.

After three decades in consulting, product development, technology transformation, go-to-market and more recently, AI-led innovation, I have seen one pattern persist: Learning gets lost in the handoffs. Nowhere is this more evident than across the product development lifecycle (PDLC), from initial concept to commercial outcomes.

As AI becomes embedded in every stage, from research synthesis to production monitoring, the competitive advantage is shifting from how quickly teams build to how effectively they learn. What matters is not just accelerating execution, but preserving the reasoning behind decisions, connecting assumptions to outcomes and applying those insights to build better products.

A product moves through five stages: idea mapping, experience architecture, product development, deployment and commercialization. Yet the decisions shaping its success begin even earlier, with the investment itself. Which problem deserves funding, what returns are expected and how success will be measured are often agreed upon in planning meetings, only to lose visibility as the product moves forward.

This disconnect continues across the lifecycle. Critical assumptions, design choices and market insights rarely travel with the product from one stage to the next. Much of that context remains with individuals rather than becoming part of a shared record, making it vulnerable to team changes and shifting priorities.

AI has made this gap more consequential. Many teams begin where the gains are most visible: writing code. But the decisions that determine what to build, for whom and why receive far less attention. The result is faster development without necessarily improving the quality of the decisions driving it.

The consequences of this disconnect extend beyond product development to business performance. McKinsey’s The State of AI in 2026 report points to a related gap: About 80% of respondents said AI improved their personal productivity, yet only 37% reported any EBIT impact. Roughly 6% were high performers, attributing at least 5% of EBIT to AI, and they were far more likely to have redesigned workflows around it. Productivity is easy to feel; business value is harder to establish.​

Our experience with a healthcare company brought this challenge into sharp focus. We worked on a patient care application that used an AI assistant to support patients between consultations. Technically, the solution performed well. The assistant passed its tests, clinicians validated its responses and the development teams met their targets. Yet four weeks after onboarding, only about 25% of new patients remained active. The investment in AI was delivering functionality, but its contribution to patient engagement remained unclear.

The problem extended beyond the technology. The initiative had started with a broad objective of improving patient engagement, but without a measurable outcome connecting product decisions to patient behavior. Each team had optimized its own responsibilities, while the assumptions behind those decisions remained scattered across functions.

We shifted the focus to measurable product outcomes, establishing a target of approximately 35% four-week retention. Using AI to analyze patient conversations alongside application usage patterns, we identified a critical disconnect: The assistant was drawing on information captured during registration, while clinicians had updated patients’ care plans. The technology was functioning as designed, but the information it relied on no longer reflected the patient’s latest clinical context.

The solution was not another AI feature. We introduced a shared context layer that connected product objectives, decision rationale and current care information across clinical, product and engineering teams. Four-week retention increased to approximately 36%, an improvement of 11 percentage points, or 44% relative to the baseline.

The experience reinforced a lesson we have seen repeatedly: AI can improve how individual components perform, but sustainable product value depends on how effectively decisions, information and outcomes remain connected across the lifecycle.

What we uncovered in healthcare was more than a disconnect between an AI assistant and a patient’s care plan. It exposed a larger vulnerability: A product can meet every technical milestone and still drift away from the business objective that justified its investment.

That begins with keeping the reasoning behind the investment connected to every decision that follows. Not through a monolithic AI platform, but through interconnected layers that preserve context as the product evolves.

At the idea-mapping stage, AI can challenge the investment assumptions against market intelligence and customer needs. As the concept moves into experience architecture, those insights inform design choices, which can then be tested against actual user behavior. During development, the same context follows the product through requirements, code and testing.

Once deployed, AI can connect usage patterns, performance issues and customer feedback to earlier decisions. At commercialization, adoption, revenue and customer outcomes can be assessed against the expectations set at the investment stage, helping leaders decide whether to scale, refine or redirect.

The real opportunity is not to make each stage independently smarter, but to ensure that the intelligence gained at one stage informs the next. That is how AI moves from accelerating product development to strengthening the decisions that determine its success.

What if the greatest value of a product launch wasn’t just what it delivered, but what it taught the organization?

With connected context, AI can reason in two directions. Forward, it traces how investment assumptions shape product decisions and where risks emerge. Backward, it connects market outcomes to the decisions and beliefs behind them, surfacing explanations for teams to validate against evidence.

This changes the economics of product development: The return on an investment is not only what the product delivers, but also what the organization learns.

The real risk isn’t building a product that fails. It’s building one, learning nothing and investing in the next with the same assumptions.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3041. Q&A with Hall of Famer Mitch Richmond, talking Yaxel Lendeborg, LeBron, Jokic, Game Pulse

Mitch Richmond is NBA history personified: NBA champion, Rookie of the Year, five-time All-NBA, six-time All-Star and Hall of Famer. For many fans, he is best remembered as part of the legendary Run TMC era of the Golden State Warriors, with Tim Hardaway and Chris Mullin.

Before sitting down for an interview with NBC Sports, Richmond was interacting with fans on the NBA App through Game Pulse. For those unfamiliar, Game Pulse combines a live court visualization with highlights and advanced statistics, including shot charts, in a live-updating experience that also features commentary and perspectives from NBA legends such as Muggsy Bogues, Carlos Boozer, Gary Payton, Joakim Noah and more.Here is our conversation with Richmond (edited for length and clarity).

NBC Sports: You've been on this Game Pulse NBA app experience with the fans. How has that been for you?

Richmond: It's been great. You know, that's my first time being on the Pulse. I always watch the association, and sometimes I already catch everything on NBA TV. But it was my first time, so it was a good experience.

Q: It seems like it's a good experience for the fans. They get real interaction not just with the studio people, but with people like yourself, who happen to be Hall of Famers.

Richmond: Yeah, no question about it. It's just the fan engagement. The fans want to be as close as they can, and sometimes they want to hear from other players watching the game. So it gives me an opportunity to kind of reach out to them, and especially since I played so long ago, so they don't know me. But it's a time to start Googling me. You know what I mean?

Q: I think people underestimate, though, like how great and how impactful Run TMC was, and that era of Warriors basketball. As you look at the league now. Is there a player that kind of reminds you of Tim Hardaway?

Richmond: That's a good question. Oh man, who reminds me of Little Tim? Oh man, I don't, it's hard. He had such power when he dribbles the ball, he gives you that shoulder as he goes by that you can't get back in front of him. Yeah, man, that's hard to do.

Q: It might be even harder with Chris Mullin in some ways.

Richmond: Oh man, let's see who Mully would be. I mean, Mully was not the fastest guy, but it is back to what I say: It's how you use your body, and how you use your skills, and how you move. We see it now in today's game, it's all about getting guys in angles when they pick-and-roll. They want you on their back and they want to make sure that you never get back in front of them. Mully had the tendency, if you get on the side of him, he has a way that you never get back around him. And so he was really, really methodical of how he's going to move and take his little steps, and might do a little bird flip. So yeah, he was very tough to guard.

Q: You're close to the Warriors still to this day. They've been fun to watch at the start of the season. What's impressed you about Yaxel Lendeborg as a rookie, just coming in like this?

Richmond: Coming in with that attitude, man, like I belong. And truly, truly, they need a spark like that. We don't know how long Curry is gonna Curry is gonna give us, but they need a young star to come in and kind of help some of that load for Curry right now. I think he's the guy. He has the temperament, and I like him. I like how he came in with Luka, arguing a little bit, but hey, I think that you need that. You need that spark. You need that spice.

Q: I like that [Warriors coach Steve] Kerr trusted him enough to give him that assignment in his first NBA game.

Richmond: I'm pretty sure he wanted it, and I think that's what great players do at times, man. It's for greatness. I want to guard the best player. You know what I mean. I have no problem. I want to guard the best player. We haven't seen that in a while.

Q: What other rookies have stood out to you?

Richmond: I mean, you look at Caleb. Man, he's going to be a star down in Chicago. Caleb Williams. I like AJ [Dybantsa of the Wizards]. AJ is good. Even the young [Cameron] Boozer. Hopefully, he can start to get his game rolling. But I think it's a lot of good young talent that we have, and I just love watching the games, to tell you the truth.

Q: Houston is off to a fast start. Dallas has knocked off two undefeated teams. What are you liking about those teams? What have you seen from them that's making them look kind of dangerous early?

Richmond: I spent a little time with them in Macau when the Rockets played the Dallas Mavericks over there [preseason], and so they looked like just a fun team. And actually, both teams was personable, and I think they get along with one another. One, and they got two good coaches that's coaching them right now. So, man, I thought Houston had a little bit more than Dallas, but Dallas has been playing excellent this year.

Q: Houston, I like the way they're built. I think they can be really dangerous in a West that feels a little more open right now.

Richmond: No question about it. I think the West is wide open. I think someone... can Portland come out of there? You know Portland is looking pretty good right now. I think the West, like you say, the West is wide open. Lakers are even looking pretty good.

Q: You mentioned Portland. Is there anybody else who's just like, wow! When they're on, I kind of want to watch them. I want to watch those players.

Richmond: I'm loving watching the Knicks, tell you the truth. I love watching the Knicks and how they play. Then I just start going for players to see how they're doing. I go and pop over at Denver and see what [Nikola] Jokic is doing. I'm always on the channel with Golden State. I'm always turning the channel to Dallas. I mean, the Lakers, because the Lakers can actually do something as well. We didn't expect that, but the center that they got from Utah?

Richmond: I mean, no one ever saw this, buried in Utah, and now seeing him, and he was hurt as well. But to see what he's been doing and the numbers he's been putting up, you know, they needed a big man like that to run the floor and catch it at the rim.

Q: On the other side, the Heat have just not gelled yet. It hasn't come together consistently. When you see them, what's missing?

Richmond: I think just a little bit more time together. You know what I mean? I think sometimes, teams mesh very well. Look at Philly. You know, how LeBron [James] just came and and haven't missed the beat over there. They're just playing real fluid ball as well. I think Boston is playing pretty good as well. I think sometimes you need just a little bit more time to gel. We haven't seen that yet, so hopefully they can they can catch a groove and start winning some games.

Q: Philadelphia has gelled really quickly, but is that just LeBron bringing his own culture and that's a team where guys seem willing to sacrifice, and they're kind of in the right place to make plays.

Richmond: I think the sacrifice is a little different when you have a star player coming in that is a volume shooter. LeBron kind of plays every part of the game, so he doesn't need those 40-point nights. You know, he might have one this year, but he has enough guns around him. And I think the guys, you have your shooting hands ready to shoot the ball when you have a guy that is a willing passer, and LeBron has been more of a willing passer, especially when he knows that he got young guns at his beck and call anywhere he goes. These guys can really shoot the ball. Look at Embiid. Embiid is in better shape. The guys are bringing enthusiasm. They had some enthusiasm over there. Let's see if they can keep it the whole year, because at some point in time, teams are going to start figuring them out, and see how it happens. But I do like the start of them; they look like a team to be reckoned with later.

Q: You mentioned Jokic's passing and a couple other guys. There are some great passers in the league right now. Who are some of the guys you want to tune in to watch because they see the court differently?

Richmond: Jokic is that guy. You know, if I played with him, man, I'd be coming off screens all day, going back door because he truly, truly finds guys. We have to put LeBron, he's still there. LeBron still knows how to get passes to the right guys. It's a few others out there, but I just love how guys like that move the ball, share it, and it looks wonderful.
