#3040. Why Altria Group Shares Bumped 15.9% Higher In October

Investors want safer stocks amid market volatility.

Shares of Altria Group (MO -0.30%) jumped 15.9% last month, according to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence. The tobacco giant is not the most exciting stock, but it keeps delivering steady earnings for shareholders and is being prized as a safe haven amid market volatility. The company reported strong earnings last quarter, and investors were pleased with the results.

Here's why the stock was up in October, and whether shares are a buy right now.

Altria sells tobacco and nicotine products, primarily in the United States market. Last quarter, it delivered solid results despite the decline in cigarette usage in the country.

Its volumes for premium Marlboro cigarettes declined by 6.4% year-over-year, but growth in its discount category led to overall cigarette volume declines of 2.9%. Through price increases, Altria is able to offset these volume declines, with the smokeables business segment reporting 3.7% revenue growth net of excise taxes, and similar levels of earnings growth.

Its oral tobacco category had flat revenues, but its on! nicotine pouch brand saw 13.3% growth in the period, which investors were likely happy about as well. Management keeps repurchasing stock to lower shares outstanding, while the dividend was raised in August, marking 57 straight years of consecutive dividend hikes.

With Altria's share price zooming to new highs, it now trades at a price-to-earnings ratio (P/E) of 16. This is one of its highest levels in years, and indicates that investors want safer stocks, with volatility appearing in the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Altria should be able to keep up its steady earnings growth in the years ahead. It can raise prices to offset volume declines, while moving into new categories like nicotine pouches and electronic vapor that are experiencing user growth in the United States and around the globe. Through share repurchases, it can keep increasing earnings per share (EPS), which will fuel even more dividend hikes that can be paid to shareholders.

However, right now, a P/E ratio of 16 does not look overly appealing for a slow-growth stock. The dividend is currently yielding just under 6%, which is pretty good for an income stock, but don't expect much more in returns (dividends plus dividend growth) in the coming years because of volume declines, an inherent headwind for the business.

#3038. From Dashboards To Decisions: Why Business Intelligence Needs A Reset

Abeer Shrivastava is a data analytics and AI leader at Tredence Inc.

​”Data is a precious thing and will last longer than the systems themselves,” Tim Berners-Lee said. Most companies I’ve worked with have kept the data. Far fewer can use it to decide anything faster.

After 15 years in business intelligence, I believe most companies don’t have a visibility problem. They have a decision problem, and it comes from three misalignments: metrics nobody agrees on, data with no context for action and no one who owns trust in the numbers. More dashboards fix none of them.

At two large tech companies I worked at, I watched finance, marketing and operations bring different revenue figures to the same executive meeting. Stakeholders were used to pulling numbers straight from source systems, and those figures didn’t match the centralized lakehouse behind the executive dashboards. Each leader defended their own number, and nobody could say which one was wrong. The meeting ended with an action item to “reconcile the data,” and the actual decision waited another week. Nobody lacked data. The meeting went to deciding whose number was right, and leadership left frustrated.

What I took from it: The discrepancy wasn’t a pipeline bug. Each number was correct for the system it came from. What was missing was a decision about which definition the company would stand behind, and no engineer could make that call alone.

Tools can’t settle this. Someone has to decide what “revenue” or “active customer” means, write it down and make every team use it. A semantic layer, such as Looker’s modeling layer, enforces that decision. It can’t make it.

Takeaway: Before building anything new, ask three teams to define your most-quoted metric. If you get three answers, fix that first.

A report can show that margins fell last quarter. It can’t tell the plant manager what to change on Monday. The reports that work put the metric beside the action: the threshold, the owner, the next step. At a BFSI client, flagging high-net-worth customers who showed signs of leaving gave leadership something to act on, and attrition in that segment fell 20% in relative terms, keeping billions of dollars in assets with the firm.

Takeaway: For every dashboard, name the decision it supports and who makes it. If you can’t, retire it.

At a retail client, the enterprise data warehouse was nearly abandoned. Stakeholders and power users didn’t trust the data, so they went back to building their own reports. The breaking point came when two regional teams presented conflicting sales numbers in a review, and both said the warehouse was the reason they’d stopped relying on it.

My instinct as a data leader was to fix quality from our side. We could have kept auditing pipelines and tightening checks, and honestly, that was my first plan. But the users weren’t saying the data was wrong. They were saying they couldn’t tell whether it was right, and no amount of testing on our end could answer that for them. Some on the team worried that handing certification to the business would slow everything down, and some stakeholders weren’t sure they wanted the accountability. We had the stakeholders certify the datasets themselves anyway. They became accountable for the data and not just consumers of it. Active users grew 36% within six to nine months and kept climbing.

What I learned: We’d been treating trust as a quality problem, and it was an ownership problem. People trust what they’ve signed off on. Once the business put its name on the datasets, the data was theirs, and they defended it instead of working around it.

Takeaway: Trust can’t be delivered to people. Give the business ownership of certifying the data they depend on.

I think the future of reporting is conversational, and I’m implementing it now at a large retail client. Leaders are already asking for AI agents they can chat with instead of static dashboards. I’d go further and say most teams should cut their dashboard count in half, and many should eventually retire the rest.

But conversational BI is where the three misalignments get expensive. A chatbot on top of conflicting definitions, context-free metrics and datasets no one owns gives you confident wrong answers instantly. The same foundations decide whether it works.

Takeaway: Before you invest in AI agents, check whether your metrics, context and ownership could survive a question asked in plain English.

The companies that pull ahead won’t be the ones with the most dashboards or the most AI. They’ll be the ones where everyone works from the same numbers and knows who answers for them.

#3036. Why Cipher Digital Stock Lost 53.9% in October

Is Cipher Digital’s October crash a buying opportunity? The answer depends on the debt market.

October was a rough month to own Cipher Digital (CIFR +18.37%). According to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence, the stock fell 53.9%, while the S&P 500 barely blinked with a 0.9% dip.

The data center developer was already sliding alongside other AI infrastructure stocks as investors grew nervous about the sector's heavy borrowing needs. Cipher stood out by raising $850 million of convertible debt on Oct. 13.

On paper, the deal sounds almost too good. Cipher sold convertible notes due 2032 with a 0% coupon, so it pays no interest for six years. Noteholders can swap their bonds into stock at $15.31 per share, and Cipher spent about $90 million on a capped call to blunt dilution. It also retired about $166 million of older 2030 notes in exchange for fresh shares.

Investors focused on dilution and timing. The stock fell 12.8% on Oct. 13 and another 12.4% the next day as the bond deal priced.

Part of the proceeds funded a $359 million investment in Cipher's Barber Lake campus, where tenant change orders and rising labor costs had pushed construction expenses above earlier guidance.

Then came a double whammy on Oct. 30. A Barclays analyst warned that AI financing problems were already here, and a record $6.3 billion Binance hack knocked crypto stocks around. Don't forget that Cipher used to focus on Bitcoin (BTC +1.15%) mining rather than AI computing, and that 78% of Q3 revenues still came from Bitcoin mining. So the stock caught both punches on the chin and fell another 10%.

Here's the odd part. Cipher has $16.6 billion in contracted revenue, with leases averaging 17.3 years. Management expects roughly $864 million of annual net operating income once its three contracted campuses stabilize. At the end of October, the whole company was worth about $3 billion. CEO Tyler Page described these sites as AI expressways that will collect tolls for decades.

But Cipher owes about $6.7 billion, its next projects need more capital, and the credit market isn't feeling generous. Construction costs remain under pressure, and nearly all of its sites sit in Texas, where data center regulation faces growing scrutiny. Those concerns popped up again in early November, when the stock showed extreme volatility around Cipher's mixed third-quarter report and the midterm elections.

One strange silver lining: with shares under $8, that $15.31 conversion price looks a long way off. Dilution from the new notes isn't an urgent problem at today's prices, though a lot can happen before 2032.

For long-term investors, the key question is whether Cipher can sign new leases on terms that justify its heavy borrowing. Its 5.9-gigawatt development pipeline offers plenty of opportunities. The financing environment will limit how many it can pursue.

Anders Bylund has positions in Bitcoin. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Bitcoin. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3033. ‘I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers

'I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Jaylen Brown has spent the opening weeks of the season describing the Sixers as a work in progress.

He offered a similarly humble perspective Monday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena. His performance, however, provided the clearest look yet at how he can influence this group while sharing the floor with the club's other established stars.

Brown finished with 25 points, 11 rebounds and five assists in the Sixers' 126-107 win over the Chicago Bulls. He shot 9 for 15 from the field, made four three-pointers and did not commit a turnover.

Five of his rebounds came on the offensive end, an area Brown has emphasized while the Sixers continue piecing together their frontcourt rotation.

"Just a mindset," Brown said. "Just trying to create extra opportunities for my team. As we're trying to patch together our fives and our front line, I try to make an extra contribution and emphasis on the glass."

Brown has also been careful about how he describes his place on a roster that already belonged to Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey.

Maxey scored 15 of his 26 points in the fourth quarter Monday, continuing his torrid start to the season. He has led the Sixers in scoring while being the only starter to appear in all eight games.

Brown is not interested in interrupting that.

"Maxey's been leading the way, 100 percent," Brown said. "He's been here. This is his team. Him and Joel have been here for a while, so the mindset has been to complement those guys. I'm finding my footing, but Maxey has been leading the way for sure."

Brown's footing looked firm against Chicago.

Beyond his scoring, he helped the Sixers control the glass, moved the ball within an offense that generated 23 assists during the first half and supplied the physicality Nick Nurse expects from him.

"The dude is tough and he wins," Nurse said. "I'll take that all day long. The degree of difficulty on some of the shots he hit was really amazing. I thought he pounded the glass and got some key rebounds. Makes a lot of winning plays. Man, he's a winner."

Brown spent the first 10 years of his career in Boston. He won a championship there, reached another NBA Finals and grew from a 20-year-old prospect into one of the league's premier two-way wings.

Moving into a new system alongside James, Embiid and Maxey was never going to be seamless.

"I mean, he's fitting in along with all of us," James said. "We're getting better and better. It's not going to happen overnight. He spent his first 10 years in a system in Boston, in the city, so it's going to take some adjustment.

"It takes adjustment for him. It's going to take adjustment for the rest of the guys that joined this franchise as well. But tonight was a great step."

James pointed to Brown's "pressure, his shot-making ability, his rebounding ability" and his professionalism.

Brown's early influence has extended beyond his production.

The Sixers entered Monday with one of the NBA's best half-court defenses. Brown has been one of the group's most persistent voices, stressing communication, preparation and attention to opposing players' tendencies.

"That's the part that I care about the most as a group, not individually," Brown said. "Having a mentality night in and night out, respecting your opponent, respecting the game, making sure that we are executing our coverages, we're being clear in our communication and then we're also taking away tendencies on a night-to-night basis.

"Those things I just explained are over 50 percent of the battle."

Brown understands that maintaining those details across an 82-game schedule will require more than reminders.

"A little bit of accountability," he said. "You learn different guys require different things. How you communicate with each guy may be a little different, but accountability for sure and confrontation if need be.

"The offense is going to be what it's going to be, but our defense has got to be non-negotiable. That's something I say all the time. Non-negotiable. You want to be a good team, defense is non-negotiable."

The Sixers showed both sides of their defensive potential Monday.

They built a double-digit lead during the first half, allowed Chicago to score 36 points in the second quarter and then created separation after halftime. Philadelphia held the Bulls to 20 points in the third quarter before pulling away in the fourth.

Brown did not celebrate their ability to raise their intensity after it slipped.

"I don't like that we have that," he said. "It's good that we have shown the potential, but we've got to keep that switch on. It doesn't work like that where you just flip the switch.

"It can catch up to us and it can bite us against a better team or on a night that we're not being as crisp on offense. It can't be a switch just because we're playing a lesser opponent or whatever the case may be. We've got to make sure that mentality is from the beginning to end. Can't skip any steps."

That urgency accompanies considerable optimism.

Brown said the Sixers enjoy playing together. He loves when James grabs a rebound because "he's like a quarterback," capable of launching an outlet pass or immediately pushing the pace.

The group is learning when to attack, when to move the ball and how to avoid hesitating in its effort to play unselfishly.

"It's a lot of talent, man," Brown said. "We like each other, and we're just kind of building that chemistry of how we can get this thing to a well-oiled machine. I think we're taking steps forward every single day."

The Sixers are 7-1 despite Brown believing those steps remain incomplete. They have won close games, erased sizable deficits and received important contributions from different players.

Brown's performance Monday showed another version of how their pieces can fit.

"We still have a long way to go to being a really, really solid, great team," Brown said. "But we've been able to win some games. We started out the season great, 7-1, and you haven't seen our best version yet.

#3030. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3029. Bucks Reacts Survey: Are the Milwaukee Bucks too nice?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NBA. Throughout the year we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bucks fans and fans across the country.

This Milwaukee Bucks season was never going to be pretty and, so far, well, the Bucks could crack mirrors. There's still plenty to be excited about, of course—namely, the team's three youngest players—but to this point these Bucks aren't exactly the dawgs we hoped they'd be. Porous on the perimeter and feeble inside, are these Bucks simply too nice?

After seven games, the Bucks have shown promise on the glass—even if there are some warning signs—in an attempt to win the possession game and maximise their opportunities to keep the scoreboard ticking over and the games close. But there is more than one way to skin a cat, and the Bucks aren't doing much to make life difficult for their opponents—certainly not from a disruption point of view. So far, the Bucks sit dead last in steals per game (5.6 SPG), nearly half a steal worse than their nearest opponent (the Memphis Grizzlies, who average 6.0 SPG). They're also dead last in opponent turnovers, forcing just 12.7 per game (nearly a full turnover fewer than the New York Knicks and Utah Jazz, who force 13.6 turnovers a night). All this means that Milwaukee isn't generating extra looks through its defence.

Compounding the issue, the Bucks are mediocre at best when it comes to turning back shot attempts. Myles Turner and (sometimes) Kel'el Ware aside, these Bucks simply do not block shots, averaging just 4.6 BPG thus far, "good" for 18th in the league. But the Bucks' rim troubles don't stop there. They're currently 23rd in the league in defending the paint, conceding 50.9 points in the paint per night, while also allowing 27.6 free throw attempts per night (22nd in the league). It should come as no surprise then that they rank 21st in the league in opponent effective field goal percentage (at 54.8%).

Realistically, these numbers shouldn't be too surprising. While Turner blocks shots, he's never been an elite defender or physical presence—and he's the Buck with the best defensive reputation. Kyle Kuzma and Jaime Jaquez Jr. compete, but they don't have the physical gifts or explosive athleticism to be more than useful team defenders. In the backcourt, Tyler Herro has long been a defensive liability—or at least regarded as such. And none of these guys have any "nasty" in 'em.

Ryan Rollins, on the other hand, has the length, and has shown the capacity to lock down at times. He's also got some of that defensive stink to him (see his battles with Tyrese Haliburton). But in the early season, Rollins has seemed more intent on getting his shot off in a completely understandable bid to (1) prove himself worthy of the expectations placed on him prior to the season and (2) prove himself worthy of a new, lucrative contract—from Milwaukee or someone else—next offseason. Forest for the trees type stuff.

Of course, it's also worth noting that, despite their limitations, the starting group prior to last night's change-up has actually been a real positive for the Bucks. This group—the team's most frequent five-man lineup with 75 minutes of playing time—has a +21.6 net rating (per Basketball-Reference). Comparatively, just four of the team's 19 other five-man lineups have fared better, but each of these have played fewer than seven minutes together. In fact, besides the starting group, no other five-man lineup has played more than 17 minutes of action thus far, making it difficult to truly determine which combinations are working and which aren't.

Another factor to consider is the team's bench. Outside of AJ Green, the core reserves are all very young, meaning they're simultaneously learning the NBA game and trying to establish themselves in the league. This is not a new combination, but it's not an easy one either—and certainly not one that typically leads to winning basketball. Youth aside, the Bucks' bench also lacks any true defensive aces—at least at this stage—despite their effort (see Kasparas Jakučionis, Brayden Burries, and Nate Ament) or gifts (see Kel'el Ware). All of this is to say that, like the players themselves, Jon Horst has more work to do.

On a bigger scale, could the Bucks' struggles to disrupt opposing offences in part boil down to a mantra malfunction? Their new motto, "Compete. Together. Get better," sure is wholesome, but is it all a bit too Brady Bunch? And is Jenkins leaning too much into relationship-building with his new herd of fawns? His decision to bench Rollins suggests not. The likely answer, then, is that this is simply a case of culture first, results later. Build rapport—nurture the youth—then let the metamorphoses begin.

Regardless, for the Bucks to start doing more than show glimpses between the cracks, they might have to find a way to get a little meaner.

What do you think? Are these Bucks too squeaky clean? Let us know your thoughts by voting below—and don't forget to explain your reasons in the comments.

#3028. Pfizer: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The patent-expiry question is still open: whether cutting costs now buys enough time for late-stage trials to replace fading blockbuster revenue.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Pfizer (PFE -2.19%) raised its full-year revenue outlook to a range of $62 billion to $64 billion. The company faces upcoming loss of exclusivity, or patent expirations, on key commercial drugs later this decade. Whether it can replace that fading revenue comes down to reinvesting cash into pipeline development and cost efficiency. Three key takeaways from the call stand out.

New treatments and recent acquisitions are generating immediate sales growth that buffers against legacy product declines. That top-line momentum gives management the leeway to fund new clinical programs while the transition plays out.

Manufacturing cuts and operational efficiency are shielding operating margins as older blockbusters lose patent protection. Management expects these structural cost reductions to fund heavy research and development investments on the other side.

Long-term value creation depends on expanding oncology pipelines and launching monthly obesity treatments. Once the patent transition finishes, management projects a sustainable compound annual growth rate, or CAGR, on the other side.

Clinical catalysts and regulatory decisions are approaching rapidly across core oncology and immunology focus areas. And management isn't hiding its drive to execute. Deep commercial reach in cancer care and an expanding pipeline of monthly weight-loss peptides position the business to rebuild its product portfolio. Management is watching clinical milestones and pricing discipline across international markets.

With cost-cutting programs protecting margins through the patent cliff, the long-term thesis comes down to converting late-stage trials into commercial revenue.

The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Pfizer. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3022. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3002. 'We don't have enough time to recover:' Is the NBA game outpacing the players?

Standing near half-court with a towel draped over his shoulders, Thunder center Isaiah Hartenstein attempted to drum up encouragement for his rookie deputy Aday Mara as he approached the bench. Teammates Micah Peavy and Bennett Stirtz waited to do the same, while occasionally tilting their heads back at the jumbotron, which flashed a deficit of nearly 20 points. Veteran guard Alex Caruso, dressed in street clothes, could only clap his hands in hopes of bringing a jolt of energy that simply would not come in a 119-98 loss to the Rockets — an unimaginable, disheartening new normal.

The 2026-27 season kicked off a fortnight ago, but Oklahoma City has already endured a campaign's worth of misfortune. Jalen Williams, lost to a fractured left fibula and a dislocated ankle. Cason Wallace, lost to a torn left labrum. Chet Holmgren, lost to a right patellar tendon tear. A tight-knit group that has always preached the importance of being where their feet are now is struggling to find its footing. A team that stood atop basketball's pinnacle less than 18 months ago now is dealing with questions about ever seriously challenging again, despite still boasting the reigning MVP and a former Coach of the Year. It's a unique type of hoops purgatory the organization hasn't experienced before.

"The morale's been pretty good all things considered," coach Mark Daigneault said on Saturday. "A week ago, the circumstances were a lot different than they are today. We understand that. We try to have an approach that can transcend these circumstances and the guys have done a great job. Obviously, the challenge is a little different now than it was prior to those injuries, but we have to recalibrate to what the challenge is and attack it a day and a game at a time."

While injuries have always been a part of the game, especially when competing at the highest level of the sport, the widespread volume and degree of ailments is troubling. One team being suddenly down 60% of its projected starting lineup is one thing, albeit a needle-in-a-haystack type probability. But the list of unavailables — Anthony Edwards, Tyrese Haliburton, Ja Morant, Ben Simmons, Jaren Jackson Jr., Paolo Banchero, Jalen Duren, Kawhi Leonard, Jalen Suggs and others — is growing, with various timelines ranging from multiple weeks to multiple months. Stars and teams are also being cautious with back-to-backs, whether due to early load management or having returned from long-term injuries. (The Pacers had 10 players out against the Hawks on Monday night, the second night of a back-to-back, citing a combination of rest and injury management.)

But the games aren't slowing down for anyone. The season has become streamlined to some degree, with the shortening of training camp/preseason (the typical ramp-up period) and the insertion of the NBA Cup. It's clearly taken a toll on the players, which in turn takes a toll on the overall product. And there doesn't seem to be an immediate fix for cramming 82 games into a six-month schedule.

Rockets guard Marcus Smart, who's dealt with serious injuries over his 12-year career, had an honest take on the growing epidemic.

"The level of basketball has become so extreme on the human body and we don't have enough time to recover like we used to," Smart said. "When I first came in the league, training camp and preseason were damn near three weeks, multiple games to get your body ready for an 82-game season. Now, depending on how far you go in the playoffs, you might get two months off? It's tough on the body.

"People don't understand; you're flying, your body's inflamed, you're landing in different time zones, go to sleep late and gotta be up early. Run as hard as you can, jump as high and fast as you can and be as physical as you can. All you're doing is asking yourself for injuries. But we signed up for it, and have to figure out a way to minimize it. Some of our best players are getting hurt and it's very unfortunate. We all want to be out here doing what we love."

There is a long list of sports scientists, doctors, data analysts and professionals who will attempt to dissect such a rapid rise of injuries in a short amount of time. But simply watching every night, the games look more rigorous, more demanding and more taxing.

Possessions are up, with the Chicago Bulls topping out at 110.2 a night, up from 107 courtesy of the 2025-26 Miami Heat. League average pace from the previous campaign is now hovering over the bottom third. The game has also become more aggressive. Last season's Pistons team, thought to be one of the more physical units around, committed 22 fouls per game. That same figure would rank 18th today. More teams are comfortable sending bodies to the offensive glass, with the Milwaukee Bucks' 33 chances per night a whopping 10 marks higher than last season's No. 1. On the defensive end, no team averaged double digits in contested rebound rate last season — there are eight such teams now. Average team speed and distance are up, too.

"There's not just one straightforward answer," said Jeff Stotts, injury analyst and owner of InStreetClothes.com. "I think you're seeing younger players get injured with a higher frequency because they're likely to have more miles on their legs before they get to the NBA. … I think the game has outpaced the player. And now you're asking your bigger, stronger athletes to also cover more ground, not only work on accelerators but also their brakes. Asking our big guys to stretch their games all the way out to the 3-point line. All those things increase the chances and likelihood of injuries occurring along the way."

Last season, only three players averaged more than 36 minutes a night — Amen Thompson, Kevin Durant and Luka Dončić. That list has since nearly tripled in size. The aforementioned list of injured stars sans Haliburton also has another thing in common: all of their usage rates were lower than the previous season. What that means is while you may be on the ball less, you're off the ball more — relocating, cutting, changing direction and using your body in other ways that still can accumulate pressure and load.

A good indicator of injury prevention is measuring an athlete's workload against their overall fitness level. Essentially the more they're asked to do physically, the more time they need to recover.

"We're in this constant back and forth between rest and acute-to-chronic load ratio, where we're trying to minimize spikes," Stotts said. "Sudden increases in load on the body in relationship to the overall conditioning of the athlete. The teams that have found success have found ways to quantify the data in a way that helps buy-in. Some teams won't rest players; others will build it in. There's no great cure."

Is such a spike in absences this early into the season enough to warrant an alternative look at scheduling or the larger umbrella of how the sport is played altogether? No magic wand can guarantee a clean bill of health, but the games, pace and aggression are increasing in frequency — and the players, whom the entire business model and ecosystem revolves around, are suffering because of it.

#2999. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2998. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#2990. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2989. Adou Thiero ‘trending in the right direction,’ could return from knee injury by end of week

After garnering considerable praise during the offseason and training camp for his development and diligent work, Adou Thiero has unfortunately yet to suit up in the regular season as he deals with swelling in his left knee. But according to head coach JJ Redick, it sounds like the second-year forward is potentially nearing a return.

"He's had some live exposures and I think he's trending in the right direction," Redick told reporters prior to the Los Angeles Lakers' win over the Miami Heat on Monday. "Hopefully by the end of the week he'll be available."

Thiero's latest injury bout marks a turbulent first year and change in the NBA. He missed considerable time as a rookie with swelling in his left knee, which sidelined him for all of preseason and the first 13 games of the regular season, before a sprained MCL in his right knee rendered him unavailable from late December to mid-February.

Fortunately, it sounds like he should be back sooner rather than later; although, swelling in the same knee a year later is undoubtedly concerning.

His point-of-attack defense and general activity, both of which popped at times during the preseason, could help invigorate the Lakers' 18th-ranked defense. They've often struggled to contain the ball and looked overmatched athletically on the perimeter at times. If he can find a reliable role offensively — and playing alongside Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves increases that possibility — Thiero's size and athleticism can remedy some of those issues.

For now, though, the focus is on Thiero becoming fully healthy and returning to the floor. Once he does that, determining whether there's a spot for him in Los Angeles' wing rotation, one that's off to a pretty commendable start through eight games, can enter the conversation.

In the meantime, Los Angeles will keep relying on Ziaire Williams, Matisse Thybulle, Jake LaRavia and Quentin Grimes to hold things down at the forward spots. The Lakers will look for their seventh win of the season when they face the Sacramento Kings inside Golden 1 Center Wednesday night at 7 p.m.
